Adaptation

Vol. 20, No. 1 · 2026-10-07 · 4 篇 · Oxford UP · Film/Literature
  1. Editorial Celebrating twenty years of Adaptation: Volume 20 commemorative issue
    Deborah Cartmell, Timothy Corrigan, Justin Smith, Imelda Whelehan · online 2026-10-07
  2. It’s Not Game of Thrones , We Swear!: a review of A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms
    Rachel M Hartnett · online 2026-10-13
  3. Multimodal ideological reconstruction in the graphic novel adaptation of The Good Earth
    Xu Lian · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    Intermedial adaptation is not a mechanical transposition between sign systems; rather, it necessarily involves a reconstruction of the source text’s meaning. Adaptations may incorporate ideas and positions absent from or even resisted by the original text. These ideas and positions may be either progressive or reactionary and are at the same time multidimensional. This article uses Nobel laureate Pearl Buck’s novel The Good Earth and its graphic novel adaptation as a case study to examine the phenomenon of ideological reconstruction in the process of adaptation. This adaptation introduces Orientalist and feminist perspectives into the original work, generating a dynamic tension between progressive and reactionary politics. Bertozzi’s graphic novel adaptation perpetuates the longstanding Western dichotomous imagination of the East: at times it depicts China as an idealized utopia, while at other times it inherits the Orientalist lineage represented by Smith and Ross, portraying China as a poor, backward, and barbaric land. Moreover, the adaptation reduces the complexity of the character Wang Lung, intensifies his portrayal as an embodiment of patriarchal oppression, and alters or omits negative depictions of female characters, thereby demonstrating a feminist perspective. Graphic adaptation is far from a natural process of semiotic conversion; rather, it is a cultural practice in which the adapter’s social and ideological background profoundly shapes the final work.

  4. The camera misreads too: class, the gaze, and the reproduction of Briony’s error
    Stefanus Galang Ardana, Antra Gautam Gurnani · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    This essay reconsiders the doubled fountain scene in Joe Wright’s Atonement (2007). The film’s makers, publicity, and press reception repeatedly framed the adaptation through faithfulness, while scholarship has read the doubling formally without asking from what social position it places the spectator to see. Drawing on cinematic-gaze theory, a historically qualified use of apparatus theory, and Jost’s distinction between ocularization and focalization, the essay argues that three sequences—the fountain, the arrest, and the letter—organize classed mediation through different relations of seeing, knowing, and reading. They align the spectator with Briony’s restricted social position while also marking its limits; elsewhere, her retrospective narrational authority persists without her literal sightline. Wright systematically removes key passages of McEwan’s interior class-narration and redistributes their work across framing, shot scale, editing, and sound. The case shows how substantial correspondence at the level of event can coexist with consequential transformation at the levels of narration, enunciation, and spectatorial position.

Animation: An Interdisciplinary Journal

Vol. 21, No. 3 · 2026-11 · 8 篇 · SAGE · Animation
  1. Editorial
    Suzanne Buchan · pp. 209-213 · online 2026-10-29
  2. Exploring the edges of animation: The colour disc experiments of Joseph Plateau
    Deirdre Feeney · pp. 214-235 · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    Nineteenth century Belgian physicist Joseph Plateau developed a series of colour discs as part of his PhD thesis exploring retinal persistence. In histories of science, animation and film, these perceptual experiments are often only briefly alluded to, due to the outdated cause of retinal persistence on motion perception and its lingering misattribution in moving-image histories. As an artist-researcher interested in Plateau’s experimental oeuvre, engaging firsthand with his colour discs provided an opportunity to develop creative reconstructions of Plateau’s collection of colour disc experiments. This practice-enabled investigation, culminating in the artwork Plateau’s Dream , provides new insight into the significance of these discs for theories and practices of expanded animation. It discusses how Plateau’s colour disc experiments present a kind of ‘animation thinking’ and how his interest in and ability to imagine the in-between state of things aligns with the concept of metamorphosis. Additionally, the article relates how developing Plateau’s Dream , revealed resemblances between the hand-painted discs and digital techniques for reducing frame rates. Lastly, it speculates on how Plateau’s disc experiments inadvertently created a system of harmonics of virtual colour.

  3. George Debels, pioneer of useful animation in the Netherlands
    Mette Peters · pp. 236-255 · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    George Debels was the most productive pioneer of Dutch animation film in the interwar period, but his work has largely been neglected in the discourse on Dutch film history. This article challenges such marginalization and instead argues in favour of recognizing Debels’ role as a pioneer of Dutch animation. The main argument will address the following questions: What are typical features of Debels’ films and creative practice, and how do they compare to other animations from that time? The focus is on three distinctive elements: the intermedial contexts of advertising in film and print media; the balancing of promotional messages with entertainment value; and the materials, technology and animation skills used in the production process.

  4. The public opinion crisis surrounding I Am What I Am : When ACGN nationalist discourse encounters identity backlash
    Mengxue Wei · pp. 256-270 · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    In creative practices and academic research, Chinese animation has maintained an intrinsic connection with nationalism. This study examines the operational mechanisms of animation, comics, games, and novels (ACGN) nationalism in Chinese animation, focusing on I Am What I Am 雄狮少年. This animated film series, centring on lion dance and Chinese martial arts, has received support from official institutions but generated a public opinion crisis because of its unconventional artistic style, character designs, and thematic expressions. This article argues that the national identity of Chinese animation is no longer a unidirectional self-definition but instead requires three levels of validation: recognition by official discourse, conscious construction by creators, and cultural identification by audiences. The last, and third, is critical as audiences not only determine commercial returns but also are active participants in the nationalist discourse. Since ACGN audiences are aesthetically conservative and culturally perceptive, innovation in animated filmmaking leads to market risks and public controversy.

  5. UPA’s Fudget’s Budget : Metaleptic play and monetary critique
    Mats Carlsson · pp. 271-282 · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    In a bid to develop a critical economic analysis of the United Productions of America (UPA) cartoon Fudget’s Budget , this article combines Erwin Feyersinger’s concept of metalepsis in animation with heterodox monetary theory framing money as a social relation rather than a commodity. Originally developed by French literary critic Gérard Genette, the narratological device of metalepsis in animation refers to the transgressions of ontologically distinct worlds often seen in cartoons, such as the breach of the animator into the world of his creation or vice versa. As this article argues, the economic system of representational money can itself be examined through the lens of metalepsis at least insofar as the relation between the transcendental reference point of a currency unit and its tokenized in-world circulation of coins, bills, and digital denominations puts money in two irreconcilable places at once. It is against this background that the stylized bookkeeping grid hovering over the animated characters for the duration of Fudget’s Budget makes for such an exquisite case study on the metaleptic tension between transcendentalism and worldly circulation, both within the cartoon and beyond. In conclusion, therefore, this article seconds UPA’s contention that animation can find a suitable form for any idea , and this form is only dependent upon the idea to be expressed .

  6. Reimagining myth and ideology in Ne Zha 2 : A multimodal critical discourse analysis
    Changsong Wang · pp. 283-302 · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    For the first time, a film from an Asian country has broken into the top 5 of the global box office, with Ne Zha 2 becoming the highest-grossing animated film worldwide. Centered on the Chinese mythological figure Ne Zha, the film demonstrates both technical sophistication and powerful narrative design. This study adopts apparatus theory to analyze both the technical apparatus and the institutional apparatus in order to understand the film’s ideological function within contemporary Chinese society. Employing multimodal critical discourse analysis, the study explores how Ne Zha 2 constructs and disseminates ideological narratives through visual storytelling, character development, and the reinterpretation of mythology. The findings contribute to a deeper understanding of how contemporary Chinese animation functions as a vehicle for ideological expression and cultural diplomacy, offering critical insights into the intersection of film, politics, and soft power in the context of China’s global media strategy.

  7. Book review: Animation – Process, Cognition and Actuality TorreD. (2017). Animation – Process, Cognition and Actuality. Bloomsbury. 302 pp. ISBN 9781501308147.
    verina gfader · pp. 303-306 · online 2026-10-29
  8. Book review: Chinese Animated Film and Ideology, 1940s–1970s: Fighting Puppets BobrowskaO. (2023). Chinese Animated Film and Ideology, 1940s–1970s: Fighting Puppets. CRC Press. 196 pp. ISBN 9781032148939; 9781003241607.
    Chengcheng Qi, Yuan Zhang · pp. 307-309 · online 2026-10-29

Camera Obscura

Vol. 41, No. 1 · 2026-05-01 · 11 篇 · Duke UP · Film/Feminist
  1. A Mode of Production Is Born: The Amphimixis of Eroticisms in Eisenstein's Old and New
    Cassandra Xin Guan · pp. 1-51 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    This essay offers a novel interpretation of Old and New (a.k.a. The General Line, dir. Sergei Eisenstein and Grigori Aleksandrov, USSR, 1926–29), shedding light on the ideological motivations of its famously erotic montage sequences. The peculiar task Eisenstein assigns to montage—namely, inducing a state of ecstasy like an orgasm—is reappraised in the context of the Bolshevik campaign for “primitive socialist accumulation.” In Soviet economic discourse, this term refers to the fundamental challenge of the state socialist economy: how to transition from a predominantly peasant-based to an industrial mode of production in the absence of capitalist imperatives. Yevgeni A. Preobrazhensky, the foremost theoretician of this dilemma, forced his fellow Bolsheviks to confront the paradox of capital formation in the aftermath of a proletarian revolution. In the ensuing controversy, Leon Trotsky envisioned a path to industrialization that hinged on workers’ willingness to create surplus value voluntarily. To this end, he called upon the masses to consent to a period of temporary “self-exploitation” and shortly thereafter endorsed cinema as an instrument of proletariat education. Focusing on the efficacy of film aesthetics as a means of economic reconstruction, this essay shows how Eisenstein “cinefied” Trotsky's vision of primitive socialist accumulation by remediating the peasant way of life to give birth to a new mode of production. Taking advantage of the human capacity for self-alienation, Eisenstein invented a new form of montage. Its deployment in Old and New sexualized the imperative of labor, thereby creating the stimulus for socialist accumulation. Out of its radical experiment with film form would emerge a proletarian subject ready to enjoy their self-exploitation.

  2. Luma's Labor: British New Realism and the Intersections of Class, Gender, and Animal Rights in Andrea Arnold's Documentary Cow
    Laura Minor, Bronwen Wilson · pp. 53-85 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    While the emergence of the multispecies documentary has created new possibilities for representing animal consciousness on-screen, this article argues that these global developments must be understood within specific national contexts. Andrea Arnold's documentary Cow (UK, 2021) demonstrates how new realist techniques—evolved from Britain's social realist tradition and transformed by female filmmakers in the twenty-first century—offer distinctive approaches to documenting nonhuman experiences. Through analysis of Arnold's observational strategies, including handheld camerawork, intimate sound design, and embodied cinematography, the authors contend that the film adapts new realism's concern with class consciousness and gender dynamics to examine industrial agriculture's interconnected forms of exploitation. By following Luma, a dairy cow, through four years of repeated pregnancies, calf separations, and routine milking on a Kent farm, Arnold reveals the systematic commodification of maternal bonds and reproductive labor within contemporary British agriculture. Drawing on theoretical frameworks from animal studies, feminist film theory, and British cinema, the article argues that Cow's engagement with domestic traditions—including irreverent humor, pastoral imagery, and working-class sensibilities—demonstrates how national cinematic forms can illuminate international systems of biopower and commodification while maintaining their cultural specificity. This article reveals how Arnold's phenomenological approach creates unprecedented intimacy with nonhuman subjects while preserving the political critique that defines both British social realism and its evolution into new realism.

  3. The Future of Next Wednesday Night: Douglas Crimp's Queer Planning in an Early Year of AIDS
    Shana Cohen-Mungan · pp. 87-113 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    This essay examines writer and AIDS activist Douglas Crimp and the optimism embedded in his weekly planners in the early years of the AIDS crisis. It focuses on Crimp's handwritten plans—for ACT UP meetings, study groups, social gatherings, hospital visits, and the Second National March on Washington for Lesbian and Gay Rights—to explore how he imagined queer futures during an obliterative epidemic. The losses of the AIDS crisis led some to claim that no future was available to queer people. In response, others positioned queerness in a distant utopia. But Crimp's planning engages, the author argues, a queer future that is both physically and temporally proximate, insisting on a temporality of possibility amid mass death. The paper planner's handwritten plans document the movements of queer bodies and record Crimp's aspirations for futurity and relationality alongside a generation of queer activists.

  4. Introduction: The Matter of Abstraction: Race, Gender, and Sexuality in Contemporary Experimental Film and the Arts
    Tess Takahashi · pp. 114-117 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    In the essays that comprise this dossier, abstraction emerges as an artistic strategy for living in a world that is not conducive to one's being in it. As a strategy, abstraction can operate as refusal, as displacement, as reverberation, as decomposition, as drawing away, as drag, as transmutation, as a prying open, and in many other ways. In this sense, abstraction is not one thing but rather encompasses a multiplicity of approaches that pull on the web of discourse that seeks to order the unruly materialities of bodies.

  5. The Ecstasy of Abstract Animation: Ja'Tovia Gary's An Ecstatic Experience
    Tess Takahashi · pp. 119-127 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    Rather than reading An Ecstatic Experience (dir. Ja'Tovia Gary, US, 2015) as presenting the reality of the history of Black people in the United States, as some critics have claimed, this essay examines the film's formal qualities as repeatedly articulating a disjunctive cut between invisible experience and visible manifestation. Over and over, An Ecstatic Experience argues for the impossibility of adequately visualizing complex internal forms of experience: the religious ecstasy of baptism in the Holy Spirit; the contradictory and sometimes fleeting feeling of freedom under the restrictions of slavery; and the mixture of despair, rage, and hope that often underlies embodied forms of political resistance in the age of #BlackLivesMatter, with which the film closes. Gary figures all of these as unrepresentable. Throughout the film, Gary manipulates the emulsion of filmic excerpts, including found black-and-white 16mm footage of people in church, a black-and-white extract of actress Ruby Dee performing a monologue in a 1965 kinescoped television presentation, a video clip from the 1980s featuring Assata Shakur talking about her escape from a US prison to Cuba in 1979, and recent color digital video footage of street-level political resistance in the face of anti-Black police violence in the 2010s. Neither do Gary's painting, drawing, and scratching over her carefully assembled collection of contemporary and historical images elicit the one-to-one correspondence between form and image sometimes called “mickey mousing” in the animation world. Instead, An Ecstatic Experience's abstract animated shapes and patterns emphasize the difficulty of translating the complexity of internal transformation into legible form outside the self.

  6. Amanda Williams's Color(ed) Theory and Other Ideas to Live Nearby
    Lauren McLeod Cramer · pp. 129-137 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    Leveraging the idiosyncrasies of color, architect and artist Amanda Williams's Color(ed) Theory (2014–16) experiments with form to express the kind of domestic intimacy that exists when nothing around looks like home. Over the span of three years, Williams enlisted locals to paint eight vacant homes on the South Side of Chicago a different shade inspired by the vivid and cloying colors of consumer products Williams recalls being marketed to Black Americans in the 1960s, 1970s, and 1980s. This short reflection on Williams's artwork considers the “theory” it enacts as a study of the Black sensorium: the dynamic lateral relations that form amid displacement. In likening the tastes, textures, smells, and sounds of Black home life to echoes, what reverberates in empty space, this essay explores the resonances of Black kinship, the echo chambers that surround it, and the possibility of finding family in art and theory.

  7. “Fluid in All Aspects, Except for Race”: Black Faces and the Limits of Abstraction
    Jessica Ruffin · pp. 139-153 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    This article considers anti-Blackness and implicit bias through the lens of aesthetics, tracing their manifestations from Immanuel Kant's philosophy to contemporary news and avant-garde media. The article treats anti-Blackness as global and environmental, eschewing approaches that focus on individual spectacles of violence, racist representations, and systemic reform. The essay's central thesis is that the racialized and white-supremacist content of the philosophical foundations of Euro-American aesthetics, particularly Kant's “ideal of beauty,” introduced the racial as a transcendental aesthetic category. The underinterrogation of the racial in the history of aesthetics has contributed to a persistently colorblind optimism regarding the ethical promise of abstraction, particularly in cinema studies. This argument is developed through a close reading of specific sequences from Terence Nance's experimental television series, Random Acts of Flyness (HBO, 2018–22). The analysis focuses on two key moments from the first episode—the “Black Face” montage and a segment on the “fluidity” of identity—which the article argues implicitly critique early twentieth-century abstract cinema. The article demonstrates how the ethical figuring of aesthetic abstraction—while hegemonically lauded as universal and objective—further marginalizes the material experiences of Black subjects, and other people of color, when the racial is not engaged as a transcendental aesthetic category.

  8. Escape from the Medically Abstract: Jeamin Cha's Nameless Syndrome and Its Dissociative Narrative
    Soyi Kim · pp. 155-165 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    Korean artist Jeamin Cha's video Nameless Syndrome (2022) is based on the stories of Korean women who couldn't receive proper diagnoses for their symptoms. Rather than presenting these stories as a striking exposé, the video work shows a disjunctive aesthetics, assembling seemingly unrelated stories and visual footage, highlighting a double sense of failure—of the patients’ experience and of medicine's lack of self-critique regarding its neoliberal ideals, racism, and sexism. Nameless Syndrome juxtaposes visual footage of Korean women undergoing medical procedures with narration based predominantly on Western and white references. Wary of the fact that black-and-white judgment-making can entail the risk of reinforcing medically abstracted body image and discourse, this video doesn't so much grant an easy conclusion as it excites its audience's curiosity and doubts. Undercurrent to the endeavor is the idea that the more abstract the perception of medicine becomes, the less light is shed on its persevering sexism and racism, the inequitable power dynamic between doctors and patients, and the effort to make accessible representations of the problems. The article reads the cognitive challenge that Nameless Syndrome poses as a unique form of resistance aligned with the aesthetic and discursive strategy of escape central to queer-of-color critique and postcolonial scholars’ caution against the often-uncontested Euro-American centrism in critical thought.

  9. Refusal of/as Form: American Artist, A Refusal (2015–16), Looted (2020)
    Lou Silhol-Macher · pp. 167-177 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    In 2013, the artist now known as American Artist changed their legal name to a generic singular, making themselves impermeable to author-reference and identification. Manifesting both their official entry in the art world and a withdrawal from it, American Artist describes this name change as the “basis for an ambivalent practice—one of declaration . . . and erasure.” This performative act was only the first in a series exploring recognizable form and identification as technologies of power. In 2016, the online performance A Refusal replaced all images on Artist's social media profile with blue rectangles, and in 2020, Looted, commissioned by the Whitney Museum for its website, covered all of the Whitney's thumbnails of artworks with images of plywood sheeting. Following surfaces as formal elements invested with experimental potential in those three performances, this essay's argument is twofold. First, if refusal is a guiding gesture across these works, its objects are forms of identification (blue obfuscates social media identity; plywood blocks recognizable artworks; generic name prevents traditional author-form). Second, abstraction is not a void to be filled with concepts or affects, but a surface for formal experimentation. Surfaces and surfacing are a mode of abstraction in Artist's work that relates to what Tina Post has coined “deadpan aesthetics.” The deadpan as a surface aesthetic allows us to visualize what a refusal of form may mean and how it operates across the spheres of art and the social. In other words, how does American Artist's work illuminate abstraction as a refusal of form?

  10. “Try To Remember/The Way We Were”
    Michael Boyce Gillespie · pp. 179-193 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    Thinking with Morgan Quaintance's Missing Time (UK, 2019), the article considers the politics of methodology and film analysis for the study of black film. Concentrated on film blackness, the essay explores issues of ambivalence, aesthetics, abstraction, the essay film, experimental cinema, and Cold War historiographies. Thus, the essay seeks to propose alternate values for the study of black film that might give more attention to the formal operations and critical capacities of film.

  11. Trans Abstractions, Decomposing Figurations: Young Joon Kwak and Kiyan Williams
    Lex Morgan Lancaster · pp. 195-206 · online 2026-05-01
    摘要

    This article argues that the sculptures and installations of Young Joon Kwak and Kiyan Williams offer trans and crip tactics of abstraction that materialize impure and expansive conceptions of embodiment through decomposing figurations. In the works of Kwak and Williams, bodies are partially present but refuse to cohere or compose into representative bodies (bodies that must either maintain normative composition or be subject to ongoing surveillance and violence). Deploying an embodied trans approach to formalism along with affective, relational understandings of trans and crip as material processes and forms of engagement with the world rather than merely appearances, the article opens the generative space of tension between abstraction and figuration as a site for navigating corporeal unmanageability. Kwak and Williams deploy material processes that interrupt perceptions of a form-as-body while foregrounding the often violent material histories and forces that shape bodies, including the potential violences of abstraction. At the same time, their work underscores the fugitive capacities of their materials, which circumvent control and subvert stable binary notions of what is natural or artificial, internal or external, past or present. In short, they show us how to work with, and from within, unmanageable material states.

Celebrity Studies

Vol. 17, No. 3 · 2026-07-03 · 11 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Media
  1. Hollywood stardom, glamour shots and poetry in 1930s Portugal: the cinephile poems of António Botto
    Joana Isabel Duarte · pp. 335-356 · online 2025-05-21
    摘要

    This paper explores the relationship between movie stars’ images and poetry, shedding light on an underexplored connection between cinema, stardom and literature. While extensive research has been conducted on cinema and poetry, the focus has rarely been directed towards film stars in particular. Film magazines provide a fertile ground for the dissemination of various creative expressions, including poetry. These publications actively fostered and promoted the pairing of actor photographs with poems – written both by readers and editorial boards. This study takes a closer look at the Portuguese context, offering insights into the representation of film stars and local fandom. In particular, it focuses on the case of António Botto – a poet known for his unconventional homoerotic themes – and his poems published in Animatógrafo, a Portuguese film magazine active during the early years of the ‘New State’ regime (1930s). This analysis explores Botto’s engagement with film stars and their images, offering insights into how actors were depicted in verse. It also aims to reposition a peripheral and often overlooked geography – Portugal – within the broader field of Celebrity and Star Studies, tracing its early cultural foundations through film magazines and the writings of António Ferro.

  2. Translating the ‘untranslatable’: is it the way to become a translator celebrity?
    Seda Taş İlmek · pp. 357-374 · online 2025-05-28
    摘要

    This article aims to explore how Turkish translators of James Joyce have gained recognition in Türkiye as ‘translators of the untranslatable’ and seeks to understand how translating works known for their untranslatability contributes to the translator’s celebrity status. The study focuses on the four Turkish translators of Joyce’s works, particularly Ulysses and Finnegans Wake, and employs Akashi’s (2018b) categorisation of fame in its theoretical framework to investigate the level of recognition achieved by the translators in Türkiye. Data was collected from various online platforms, including newspapers, magazines, forums, blogs, and social media accounts to understand how the translators are perceived. The study concludes that translating the untranslatable has not created the celebrity translator as a distinct phenomenon, but has nonetheless significantly contributed to the translators’ prominence. While some of the translators are regarded as ‘media active’ due to their increased visibility through media engagements, others have remained ‘recognised’ translators. Thus, the study reveals that while all translators have managed to highlight their role through the act of translating the untranslatable, what distinguishes their prominence is their ability to leverage media visibility.

  3. The memoir and the mediated self: the crafting and performance of Madhur Jaffrey’s persona in Climbing the Mango Trees (2006)
    Meghna Gangadharan, Ananya Ghoshal · pp. 375-390 · online 2025-06-17
    摘要

    As a narrative based on the recollection of memories, the memoir is a site of performance for the mediated selves of extraordinary individuals and celebrities like Madhur Jaffrey, who use it as a platform to extend their professional influence through the performance of their public personas. This article delves into Jaffrey’s memoir Climbing the Mango Trees (2006), examining it for its narrative strategies that connect to her present professional status as an international expert on Indian cuisine. The article argues that the narrative strategy of her memoir is derived from her extensive experience hosting cookery shows and, therefore, features characteristics like direct address, building of familiarity, and drawing of authenticity. The article contends that such a narrative approach facilitates the fusion between her identity as an Indian immigrant and her profession as a cookbook author and TV personality. Additionally, the article establishes how the memoir links thematically to her long list of cookbooks and cookery shows, showcasing a cohesive structure and narrative that reinforce her persona as a celebrity with expertise in Indian cuisine.

  4. History ‘for the gram’: AI beauty and the vintage, analogue, and ‘throwback’ celebrity
    Marissa Spada · pp. 391-404 · online 2025-06-18
    摘要

    The popular photo and video-sharing social media platform, Instagram, is abundant with decades-old images of female celebrities that have been digitally manipulated using AI-powered photo-editing applications, such as Facetune. These newly retouched images conform to an idealised facial aesthetic that has emerged in the last decade, largely as the result of Instagram and other image-centric social media services. This article analyses this trend within the broader discourses on photo manipulation and the commodification and objectification of famous women’s bodies. I demonstrate that while this visual content is produced and shared with varying degrees of authorisation and intentionality, the phenomenon ultimately reinforces the norm and expectation that feminine beauty, which is itself historically contingent, should take precedence over historical accuracy. The facial aesthetic that emerges from these images reaffirms that women’s bodies can and should be ‘edited’ to produce a timely and economically viable aesthetic.

  5. Mom celebrities of Instagram: a literary analysis of momfluencing culture in Ellery Lloyd’s People Like Her (2021)
    Sumana Mukherjee, Amrita Satapathy · pp. 405-421 · online 2025-08-05
    摘要

    This article examines the contemporary phenomenon of momfluencing and how literary texts explore the process of maternal content production, circulation, and consumption on social media. As Internet celebrities, momfluencers showcase their monetised everyday life on Instagram and often reduce maternal authenticity to a mere performance. Ellery Lloyd’s People Like Her (2021) follows ambitious momfluencer Emmy Jackson and her eventual pursuit of influencer stardom. Blurring the lines between genuine self-representation and manufactured online content, her insatiable desire for digital fame leads her to lose her morale, marriage and family safety. Employing theories of Crystal Abidin’s calibrated amateurism, Erving Goffman’s Impression management, Lauren Berlant’s cruel optimism, and Leon Festinger’s social comparison, this study explores the creation, dissemination, and impact of maternal content consumption. Through a close reading of People Like Her, this research highlights the tension between authenticity and fabricated (im)perfection and its effect on the modern anticipation of authentic motherhood on digital platforms.

  6. ‘She really gets my blood boiling': moral judgements, dislike and celebrities
    Melissa A. Click, Rebecca Tukachinsky Forster · pp. 422-439 · online 2025-08-11
    摘要

    Despite ample evidence that morality is a significant component of audiences’ feelings about and responses to celebrities, its explicit impact has been investigated infrequently in explorations of audiences’ negative reactions to media figures. Understanding media audiences’ moral responses is important in our contemporary culture in which ‘cancelling’ is a frequent response to public figures’ transgressions. We argue that audiences’ feelings of dislike are an ideal site for exploring moral orientations to media figures because dislike often surfaces moral judgements. The 43 interviews we conducted demonstrate that audiences use moral intuition and reasoning to form opinions about media figures whom they dislike. We discuss the major themes that developed from the interview data, including participants’ moral intuitions and reactions, the moral domains of participants’ dislike and possibilities for transforming participants moral judgements.

  7. The making of an Oceanic superhero: Dwayne Johnson, mixed identity, and non-conforming heroism in Black Adam (2022)
    Ting-Ting Chan · pp. 440-456 · online 2025-08-02
    摘要

    WWE icon and Hollywood superstar Dwayne Johnson’s portrayal of Black Adam in the 2022 DC Extended Universe (DCEU) superhero film departs from the prevalent Eurocentric and colonialist narratives within Hollywood superhero cinema. This article examines how Johnson embodies an empowered, non-conforming, non-white superhero who embraces Indigenous Pacific Islander cultural identities and worldviews. Highlighting fluidity, diversity, and multiplicity, the film explores the complexities of identity formation, drawing from Johnson’s own mixed cultural heritage. With a critical lens, this article probes into Black Adam’s Indigenous cultural references and the recurring motif of boundary-crossing and argues that the film engages with identity politics, colonialism, self-determination, and power dynamics within the context of American imperialism in the Pacific Islands. By showcasing Johnson’s mixed cultural background, Black Adam exemplifies self-assertion and resistance against Western colonial strategies of militarisation and racialisation. This analysis demonstrates the ongoing geopolitical struggles and negotiations within the Pacific region, highlighting the film’s potential (and also Johnson’s efforts) to advocate for the recognition of unique Pacific Islander cultures and subjectivities within a framework of colonial rule.

  8. Hate content and gendered shaming in Vietnamese social media: the case study of Trấn Thành
    Thi Gammon, Anh Ngoc Quynh Phan · pp. 457-475 · online 2025-08-24
    摘要

    Few Vietnamese showbiz artists have enjoyed the same success as Trấn Thành, an entertainer with a thriving career in television and filmmaking. One of the most popular Vietnamese artists, Trấn Thành has also endured immense hate. In March 2023, at a press meeting, Trấn Thành bemoaned with tears the misery of being a high-profile figure, insisting that ‘being an artist is much more difficult than you may think’. Rather than garnering sympathy, however, he was subsequently met with criticisms from members of the public, who ridiculed him for his perceived lack of modesty and ingratitude towards his privilege as a successful entertainer. He also got derided for his failure to conceal emotions, thus failing to observe societal expectations of ‘tough’ masculinity. Through a discourse analysis of impactful Facebook posts discussing this press event and of netizens’ reactions to these posts, this paper identifies the discursive strategies used to ridicule and shame Trấn Thành. It reveals the reasons behind the backlash: an expectation for entertainers to remain humble and conform to norms. The paper contributes to the literature on digital hate content in Vietnam, which receives little scholarly attention, and the broader international scholarship on celebrity culture and online shaming.

  10. Becoming Ye Xiu: fictional celebrity and platformed fame in The King’s Avatar
    Jing Lei · pp. 478-486 · online 2026-07-06
    摘要

    In contemporary Chinese digital culture, celebrity increasingly exceeds the boundaries of the human. Fictional characters circulate across platforms with recognisable personas, sustained authority, and intense affective investment, functioning as celebrities despite their lack of physical existence. Ye Xiu, the protagonist of the online novelThe King’s Avatar(《全职高手》), exemplifies this shift. First appearing as a professional e-sports player within web-based fiction, Ye Xiu has since become a widely recognised cultural figure through animation, live-action drama, voice performance, gaming discourse, and extensive fan production. The present article approaches Ye Xiu as a case of fictional celebrity, arguing that his celebrity status emerges through an assemblage of media infrastructures, platformed fandom, and affective labour. In doing so, it contributes to ongoing debates in celebrity studies concerning virtuality, relational fame, and the limits of Western star-centric models.

  11. Labubu: the ugly-cute toy celebrity of mainland China’s sang culture
    Luming Chen, Ning Zhang · pp. 487-495 · online 2026-07-01
    摘要

    This article examines the rise of Labubu, an ‘ugly-cute’ designer toy, as a non-human celebrity among young people and explores how fan practices of sang culture contribute to the establishment of its celebrity status in mainland China. Drawing on textual analysis, discourse analysis of videos, comments, and danmu on the platforms Bilibili, Douyin, and Rednote, this paper argues that Labubu is not just a consumer product but has instead emerged as a toy celebrity comparable to human stars. Its rise has been driven by celebrity endorsements, marketing strategies, and its resonance with sang culture among Chinese youth, while the government’s attitude towards this toy celebrity remains notably ambivalent. This research contributes to understanding the complex relationship between toy celebrities, sang culture, youth culture, and state authority in mainland China.

  1. Algorithms, bullet screens, and Chinese academic stars: the making of Bilibili’s influencer-professors
    Kezheng Chen, Xinzhong Chen · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    This study theorises the wanghong jiaoshou (internet-celebrity professor) as a distinct form of professional celebrity emerging from China’s platform ecology. Drawing on semi-structured interviews with 12 Bilibili scholar-creators, we investigate how academic authority is negotiated through the platform’s specific techno-cultural affordances: bullet-screen comments (danmu), the gamified ‘One-Click Triple’ economy, and state-endorsed science popularisation (kepu) norms. Through an integrated framework synthesising boundary work, Bourdieu’s capital theory, and celebrity studies, we identify a tripartite typology of digital boundary work – expansive, protective, and blurring – as the mechanism through which scholars accumulate composite platformised authority. This authority is conceptualised as a dynamic product of cross-field capital conversion among Institutional, Attention, and Affective Capital. Our findings reveal how calculated intimacy and algorithmic collegiality reshape scholarly persona, while highlighting tensions between rigour and virality, institutional autonomy and platform dependency. The article extends celebrity studies by contextualising professional celebrity within digital China and redefining celebrity capital as relational, platform-specific, and constantly renegotiated at the intersection of academic, commercial, and state logics.

  2. I Ain’t sorry: Beyoncé’s Lemonade and post-respectability black celebrity
    Kyle Stanley · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    This article examines Lemonade as a decisive intervention in the relationship between Black celebrity and respectability politics. Situating the album within a longer history of racial governance, it argues that Lemonade marks a withdrawal from the belief that disciplined performance can secure protection or recognition from institutions whose authority depends on exclusion. Rather than treating the album as a response to a discrete political moment, the analysis reads it as registering the exhaustion of respectability as an organising framework for Black public life. The article shows how emotional containment comes to signify coercion rather than virtue, while expressions previously managed or suppressed acquire interpretive weight. This argument extends across Beyoncé’s later work, including Renaissance and Cowboy Carter, where authority increasingly emerges through historical placement rather than immediate approval. Together, these projects articulate a post-aspirational mode of Black celebrity that no longer treats inclusion as evidence of progress. The article contends that Beyoncé’s work models a form of cultural authority oriented towards durability rather than consensus, offering insight into how refusal operates as an organising orientation under contemporary conditions of racialised power.

  3. The ‘20,000 year-old nymphette’: ageing without age as reflected in the French singer Brigitte Fontaine’s career
    Louis Pastor, Jeanne du Roure · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-19
    摘要

    Brigitte Fontaine is an 87-year-old French artist whose protean career spans nearly six decades across theatre, music and literature. Since the 1960s, she has cultivated a non-conformist persona, first through her lyrics and later through an increasingly eccentric public style. In her 2013 song ‘Delta’, she describes herself as a ‘20,000-year-old nymphette’, an oxymoronic expression capturing a central dimension of her trajectory: the paradox of ageing without age. Taking this seriously, this article examines how ageing has transformed Fontaine’s non-conformity into asserted transgression, enabling late-life stardom that counters age-related penalties widely documented in sociological research on female artists. After starting in theatre, Fontaine turned to music and released several albums, before a period of relative invisibility in the 1980s. Her return to prominence in the 1990s marked an unprecedented expansion of public recognition. This visibility was accompanied by media references to her age, alongside the construction of a persona that subverts normative representations of female ageing. Drawing on an original corpus of press articles and television interviews, this paper combines quantitative and qualitative analyses to examine her evolving regime of visibility and media framing.

  4. ‘Feeling very fit’: when an ‘Oriental’ star fades out
    Yiman Wang · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    This article delves into the thus-far understudied late career of Anna May Wong—the most celebrated Chinese American film pioneer.   5. Performing Southern postfeminism: authenticity and celebrity in Leanne Morgan’s comedy
    María Celina Bortolotto · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-15
    摘要

    ‘When you write to me on social media and say “I’m living your life”, I know it’s true’. This article examines how the American comedian Leanne Morgan constructs a commercially successful persona by transforming the contradictions of contemporary Southern womanhood into a shared emotional rhythm. Through close textual analysis of her memoir What in the World (2024) and her stand-up performances, I argue that Morgan performs what I term a Southern postfeminist square dance rhetoric: a patterned movement in which she steps towards feminist critique before pivoting back into the reassuring cadence of tradition, faith, and domestic femininity. This rhetorical strategy aligns with the construction of Morgan’s celebrity persona, showing how her confessional, maternal, and self-deprecating authenticity functions simultaneously as emotional intimacy and as a form of brand coherence across platforms. Her performances cultivate a sense of parasocial intimacy while enabling her to navigate ideological divides and appeal to a broad audience base. Rather than resolving postfeminist contradictions, Morgan’s comedy teaches audiences how to inhabit them, transforming ambivalence into a marketable and affective resource, validating conventional roles while offering audiences emotional recognition and comic relief. Her work exemplifies how contemporary celebrity comedians mobilise authenticity as both a cultural and commercial strategy.

  6. From boy-deity to queer spiritual icon: ageing, kyara accumulation, and maternal authority of Akihiro Miwa
    Kazumi Nagaike, Natthanai Prasannam · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-15
    摘要

    This article examines Akihiro Miwa (1935–2026) as a Japanese queer celebrity whose public persona has been constituted through the cumulative layering of kyara formations across post-war Japanese media culture. Rather than reconstructing Miwa’s private self, it analyses how his image has been discursively produced and circulated through biography, criticism, television, theatre, film, and popular discourse. Bringing Japanese kyara theory into dialogue with celebrity studies and age studies, the article argues that Miwa’s star persona is best understood not as a linear sequence of stages but as a recursive accumulation organised around three major formations: the sacred youth of the ‘boy-deity’, the onnagata-like persona, and the later-life queer spiritual icon grounded in maternal ethics. This structure reveals Miwa as both an age-specific and an age-transgressive celebrity whose authority is repeatedly reconstituted across different moments of life. Drawing on Tsunehiro Uno’s concept of ‘expanded motherhood’, the article argues that Miwa’s ageing authority emerges from a post-war cultural formation in which masculinity is mediated through maternal ethics rather than paternal power. It further suggests, through the lens of queer ageing, that authority in his later life is produced through the reorganisation of earlier aesthetic and affective forms.

  7. Do vocaloids dream of electric sleep? The death and ageing of virtual Idols 1
    Nick Prior · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    This article explores what growing old means when distinctions between biological ageing, social ageing and technological ageing are fuzzy and confusing. Focusing on the Japanese virtual idol, Hatsune Miku, it asks what a post-humanist approach to celebrity and ageing yields in a context where celebrity is a highly elaborate techno-cultural formation and celebritisation a process embedded in global circuits and platform dynamics: both of which shape celebrity’s surfaces. Despite her appearance as a forever young idol, the article shows how Miku is inherently precarious and tethered to the fragile infrastructures that sustain her. Just as Miku’s fanbase matures, so their modes of engagement and the platforms that host their content evolve and decline. The article draws on a mix of theoretical work informed by post-humanism, as well as primary research comprising ethnographic work, netnography and close textual analysis. The article makes the case for an approach to celebrity that foregrounds the idea that fans age with their idols, reflecting a broader evolution in the lifecycle of digital fandom and ageing assemblages.

  8. Keeping up with everybody else? Celebrity and visual comparability in social media beauty cultures
    Akane Kanai, Rosalind Gill, Amy Shields Dobson, Julia Coffey, Niamh White · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    Young people growing up in digitally mediated environments are increasingly ‘savvy’, literate and critical of the manufactured nature of celebrity beauty. Yet, beauty standards exemplified by celebrity are increasingly impossible to dismiss when such ideals have become sedimented into everyday social media practice. Drawing on a qualitative research project with 88 young social media users in Australia, this article argues that lateral, continuous, everyday comparison and comparability with acquaintances and peers have become significant modalities through which appearance pressures are experienced by young people. Importantly, this direct comparison with peers who can achieve a celebrity look does not replace, but becomes a principal means of reinforcing increasingly ‘higher standards’ set by celebrity. We explore the highly individualised, sometimes difficult and painful ways unfavourable comparison could register, while also looking at how these feelings were themselves shameful and had to be disavowed, kept secret or managed alone. Further, we also consider the experiences of those who felt ‘on the edge’ of comparability – somehow not straightforward participants in the competitive comparison culture by dint of particular racialised identities – exploring the paradoxical ways in which this could mark an intensification of comparison, or, by contrast, a space to operate outside it.

  9. Quitting ‘the substance’: her age anxiety and her resistance in the AI era
    Tianle Huang, Shutong Fan · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    In an industry that long privileges youth, generative AI is challenging Hollywood performers’ presence and turning their age anxiety into an existential and ontological threat through algorithmic classification and replacement. This article examines how Hollywood actresses embrace ageing and identifies multiple technologies of the self that help transform ageing from a taxing burden into narrative capital. By combining Vivian Sobchack’s phenomenological account of the actor’s four bodies with Don Ihde’s postphenomenological analysis of the technological body, this enquiry conceptualises how actresses resist age anxiety in the age of AI into a framework that spans material–physiological, cultural–representational, role–performative, and symbolic–capital dimensions. Taking (2024) as a cyborg allegory of rejuvenation, the study reveals the meanness and multi-(in)stabilities of curative yet toxic quasi-AI technologies. Demi Moore, as the film’s leading actress, extends the cautionary themes of ageing, technology, and fractured subjectivity through her own star body, career trajectory, and cultural capital. To quit the substance is not to renounce technologies, but to inhabit their multi-(in)stabilities critically, to re-imagine ageing, embodiment, and authenticity within an algorithmic culture, and to reconceptualise mobilising age anxiety into techno-ethical self-formation.

  10. Take a closet off of me: transformation of shame into social resource in Israeli celebrity disclosures
    Yael Gaulan, Michal Marmorstein, Zohar Kampf · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    This study examines how Israeli public figures transform shame disclosures on social media into strategic resources for identity construction and audience engagement. Through discursive psychological analysis of 25 posts published on Facebook and Instagram between 2017 and 2025, we identify consistent narrative components and positioning strategies that convert stigmatised experiences into forms of social and material capital. The analysis demonstrates how shame is reconstructed from a debilitating burden into a productive resource through metaphorical framing, temporal anchoring, and purposeful transformation. Strategic positioning practices – including professional identity management, audience engagement, and commercial integration – further this transformation. These disclosures reflect broader cultural trends in ‘emotional capitalism’ where vulnerability becomes valuable when properly framed. By establishing themselves as guides who have overcome stigma, these figures maintain authority while accessing cultural capital associated with authentic disclosure. The findings reveal how digital platforms enable new forms of parasocial relationships where celebrities leverage personal struggle to build community while generating professional opportunities. This research demonstrates how shame functions as social currency in contemporary digital environments, expanding beyond its traditional conceptualisation as primarily private and concealed. This first analysis of Israeli celebrity disclosures contributes to understanding evolving norms around vulnerability, authenticity, and identity in digital culture.

  13. Chasing fame, curating flow: logics of everyday celebrification on Instagram in Taiwan – a mixed-methods analysis of follower and following metrics
    Shih-Wei Tien · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-08-28
    摘要

    This study investigates how ordinary Taiwanese Instagram users enact celebrification – the everyday practices through which visibility, recognition and self-presentation become intertwined with digital metrics. By operationalising míng (名, fame), that is, follower count and liú (流, flow), that is, following count, as measurable indicators that project cultural meanings, this paper proposes a method for quantifying and interpreting the relational dynamics of fame in a digital platform environment. Using 4,066 account-level observations (8,132 metric values) and qualitative analyses of user profiles and hyperlinks, the study identifies three logics of celebrification: Business card, Flyer and Self. Rather than treating metrics as mere numbers, the study reads them as culturally encoded signs of visibility and recognition, signs that mediate how users imagine themselves and their place in the attention economy. The analysis suggests that míng and liú can be understood as mutually constitutive dynamics rather than opposing forces. Their correlations fluctuate with user visibility, revealing a layered structure of ordinary celebrification. By foregrounding míng and liú as cultural signs at the interface level, the study extends celebrity studies beyond Western perspectives, offering a model for observing fame where numbers meet cultural meaning.

  14. ‘I will live by my own policies. I will sleep with a clear conscience. I will sleep in peace.’: Calling out pejorative celebrity obituaries as an emergent digilante practice
    Marta Dynel · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-08-12
    摘要

    This paper furthers discourse-driven research on parasocial grieving by shedding light on a previously unexamined type of collective online reaction following celebrity deaths. Contributing to the literature on celebrity mourning online, this study addresses a new avenue of digilante practice involving multiple social media users denouncing obituaries they consider inflammatory. These obituaries are deemed to violate the moral principle of protecting the reputation and memory of the deceased. The study reported here presents a qualitative textual analysis of a dataset of relevant user tweets (1766) posted publicly to X in response to three obituaries of Sinéad O’Connor identified as derogatory to the subject, and hence inflammatory. Twelve rhetorical aims are thus synthesised to detail the enactment of the digilante practice conceptualised as calling out pejorative obituaries in the collective action of restoring the moral order.   16. Kawaii kayfabe: understanding the co-construction of Indonesian VTuber talents’ online personas
    Lee Choo Sum · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-07-10
    摘要

    In recent years, Virtual YouTubers (also known as VTubers) – human content creators who primarily perform and interact with online audiences as 2D or 3D avatars – have experienced a meteoric rise in prominence and popularity on YouTube and other livestreaming platforms. Existing literature on VTubers have characterised this new form of digitally mediated celebrity as perpetual and unsettled personas collectively enacted between online users. However, missing from these accounts is the role of VTuber agencies, as they do not meaningfully distinguish between independent VTubers and VTuber talents (VTubers who are employed under talent agencies). As such, this paper proposes a new analytical framework – kawaii kayfabe – for understanding the collective construction of VTuber talents’ online personas between agencies, talents, and fans.   17. Everything, everywhere, all at once: Michelle Yeoh’s Malaysian stardom in a global context
    Chrishandra Sebastiampillai · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-07-10
    摘要

    This article investigates how Yeoh’s celebrity is read within a Malaysian context, exploring her endorsement of Malaysian politics, and how her ‘Malaysianness’ contributes to rendering her image authentically ‘Asian’ to a global audience. Recent roles leverage Yeoh’s history of transnational mobility, Chinese Malaysian heritage, and global recognition, demonstrating a continuous morphing or migration of identity and location, always occupying a liminal identity – of being a Malaysian star, but something other; a Hong Kong star, but something other; a Starfleet captain, but something other.   18. Retiring against time: media narratives of Momoe Yamaguchi’s ‘ageless myth’ and the ‘aging body’
    Hao Gao, Yi Zheng, Jing Wu, Yijie Ye · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    This article examines the media narratives surrounding Momoe Yamaguchi, a Shōwa-era icon, focusing on her 1980 retirement and subsequent four decades of representation. Drawing on Dyer’s star theory, which conceptualises the star as an ideological sign, and integrating Foucault’s concept of disciplinary power and Bourdieu’s theory of symbolic capital, the study employs Fairclough’s critical discourse analysis to examine three corpora: the Asahi Shimbun microfilm (1974–1999), the Asahi Shimbun digital archive (1985–2025), and the women’s magazine Josei Jishin (2018–2025). Three themes emerge: First, retirement narratives shift from evaluating Yamaguchi’s appearance to her morality. Second, commemorative discourse freezes her youthful image as a timeless cultural archive. Third, women’s magazines continually reinterpret her role as the ‘good wife, wise mother’, making her a contemporary moral exemplar. The study argues that by retiring from public visibility, Yamaguchi removed the conditions for ageing discourse, shifting the focus from appearance to moral self-discipline, and accumulating symbolic capital in the moral domain. This article identifies a third pathway for visibility governance in Japanese idol culture, in which female celebrities manage ageing risk through structured withdrawal from visibility, challenging the assumption of continuous visibility for female idols.

Continuum

Vol. 40, No. 5 · 2026-09-03 · 12 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Media/Culture
  1. Encountering global media before Satellite and Internet: media practices on the periphery
    Joy Elizabeth Hayes · pp. 721-725 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    This special section offers a new perspective on global communication history by shifting focus from Western media practices to those in post-colonial, semi-colonial and peripheral settings. Four articles examine the cultural and political implications of transnational media and communication practices in Afghanistan, China, Kenya and Romania from the late 19th century through the 1980s. These articles are based on research presented at the Going Global before Satellite and Internet Preconference of the 2024 International Communication Association Conference. This collection fills a gap in the historical study of global media by providing diverse case studies that explore how publics and institutions encountered global media through transnational networks, infrastructures, and organizational and ideological frameworks. This Introduction provides brief overviews of these studies and situates them within the broader global media history scholarship.

  2. Print, power, and modernity: the political economy of newspapers in Afghanistan before 1979
    Somaya Shafiqi · pp. 726-745 · online 2026-05-11
    摘要

    This study offers a political economy analysis of Afghan newspapers before 1979, examining how print media mediated relations between state power, external actors and projects of modernization and nation-building. Focusing on five key outlets, Shams-u-Nahar, Siraj-ul-Akhbar, Aman-e-Afghan, Anis and The Kabul Times, it traces three phases: the emergence of court-centred print under Amir Sher Ali during the Great Game; the reformist, anti-colonial moment associated with Mahmud Tarzi and King Amanullah; and the selectively liberalized media environment under Zahir Shah, including the ‘Free Parliament’ and ‘Decade of Democracy’. Combining a political-economy of communication framework with qualitative discourse analysis of selected issues, the article shows that newspapers operated primarily as instruments of propaganda, legitimation and developmental nationalism, while at specific junctures also opening constrained spaces for criticism and debate. It argues that pre-1979 Afghan print cannot be understood simply as an externally imposed project: exiled elites and domestic reformers appropriated Ottoman, Iranian, Indian and broader global models to articulate distinct visions of Afghan modernity, even as low literacy, limited infrastructure and linguistic fragmentation sharply curtailed their social reach and impact.

  3. It moves through you – early Chinese Communist radio and the culture of technology
    Dayton Lekner · pp. 746-757 · online 2026-02-18
    摘要

    In December 1940, the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) launched its first radio station from Yan’an, Shaanxi. Through the practice of the announcers who gave voice to political texts, this article explores the formative years of CCP radio, and through this the relationship between technology and culture. While radio has been depicted as a technology that transformed China, put to use by first the Nationalists (KMT) and then the CCP, the practice of CCP announcers reveals two important diversions from this model. First, the CCP put radio to use not to replace but to enhance extant socio-political practices of community building. Second, this resulted in a distinct CCP broadcast culture during the 1940s, with unique conceptions and deployments of the new technology. The development of early CCP radio announcing practices reveals a mutual interaction between the promises of the new media, extant socio-political practices, and limiting factors that forced broadcasters to improvise with the new technology. Far from eroding cultural or national barriers, CCP adoption of the new media of radio would create a distinct Chinese media culture after 1949 and into the current era.

  4. International influence on radio and family planning communication in Kenya: an historical assessment
    Joy Elizabeth Hayes, David B. Lomoywara · pp. 758-773 · online 2025-08-03
    摘要

    This study investigates the history of family planning communication in Kenya and the role of international aid and radio broadcasting in that history. It focuses on family planning communication as a component of development communication that was woven into Kenyan nation policy shortly after Independence. It draws on the records of the Ford Foundation and other international agencies available at the Rockefeller Archive Center, along with radio programme recordings, government reports and other sources. This study identifies three cases that correspond to three historical periods of family planning communication in Kenya: the 1960s-1970s; the 1980s-1990s; and the 2000s-2010s. These cases allow us to explore how the Ford Foundation and other agencies worked with Kenyan organizations and state actors to develop large-scale family planning campaigns targeting different segments of society. This history provides an important context for understanding radio’s current role in reproductive health communication in Kenya.

  5. Before streaming media: the emergence of marathon viewing in Romania during the 1980s
    Alexandra Bardan · pp. 774-787 · online 2025-08-13
    摘要

    This study explores Eastern European media consumer cultures during late socialism. Focusing on uncharted TV viewing practices in 1980s Romania, it examines the videocassette recorder (VCR) as a technology that facilitated consumer agency. Through the analysis of three case studies – commercial shared screenings via cable TV, public commercial collective screenings, and private (non)commercial collective screenings – the research highlights the VCR’s role in shaping new viewing practices. The findings reveal limited consumer control, significant state involvement in the VCR economy and substandard screening quality as the norm, reflecting a ‘second-hand’ consumer culture.

  6. Modernity and indigeneity: Australian self-representation at the Osaka expositions of 1970 and 2025
    Tets Kimura, William Peterson · pp. 788-801 · online 2026-03-05
    摘要

    International Expositions or so-called ‘World’s Fairs’ have for over 150 years served as the most prominent stage in which nations have represented themselves at the world’s largest gatherings. Osaka is unique in having hosted two such events: once in 1970 and again in 2025, providing Australia with two unique platforms to reach millions of Japanese citizens through its presentations in their national pavilion. Over this 55-year period, how Australia has presented itself to a Japanese audience has changed significantly, from the projection of a modern, advanced, surf-loving White Australia in 1970, to a nation that recognizes and learns from 65,000 years of Indigenous culture and knowledge systems. These shifts run alongside the changing dynamic of Australia-Japan relations, moving from economic partnerships to a closer alliance and mutual friendship and the deepening of cross-cultural understandings. This article critically examines how Australia’s self-representation at these two expos has shifted over time and what the form and content of Australia’s country pavilions have offered the Japanese spectator/participant. This study also investigates how these shifts reflect Australia’s increasing desire to position itself in Asia as a reliable partner with Japan, rather than as a Western, colonial outlier nation floating in the Pacific.

  7. Restricting young people from digital platforms in Australia: the experience of digital harms and freedom of expression in community support for youth social media age bans
    Rob Cover, Joel Humphries, Ingrid Richardson, Dan Harris · pp. 802-819 · online 2025-12-05
    摘要

    In 2024, the Australian government introduced legislation requiring digital platforms to restrict Australian users under sixteen years from holding social media accounts to protect from digital harms such as abuse and disinformation. The first half of this paper analyses key discourses on the mandatory restriction, demonstrating an underlying assumption that young people’s use of digital platforms is about exposure to (sometimes problematic) content rather than participation, sharing, creativity, art, engagement and debate. The second half of the paper analyses the factors that condition widespread adult support for restriction. Drawing on survey data of Australian adults exposed to digital harms, we analyse how support for mandatory restriction may be shaped by (i) demographic factors, and (ii) experiencing or witnessing digital harms. We end by discussing how participants who gave a very high importance to free speech but sought to restrict young people from social media likewise ignores youth participatory culture online.

  8. Feminism Reboot and the evolution of Korean Boys’ Love
    YooJoung Nam, Eunhoo Lee, Ana Rita Baltazar Almeida · pp. 820-838 · online 2026-03-08
    摘要

    ‘Boys’ Love’ (BL) is a controversial genre portraying romantic homoerotic relationships between men largely consumed by women. This paper traces Korean BL’s adaptability and responsiveness to changing societal attitudes. In particular, it dives into how the genre has transformed since the emergence of the 2018 ‘t’alBL’ movement, which arose in the broader context of the 2015 ‘Feminism Reboot’. Through close readings of works produced after 2018—including works released immediately following the movement, a work that reconfigures conventional Omegaverse tropes, and works that either satirize established conventions or pursue more wholesome romantic dynamics—this paper explores the diversification of BL narratives. While these changes reflect the demand for greater feminist awareness, Korean BL continues to exhibit certain limitations. Ultimately, the genre offers a glimpse into the complexities of contemporary Korean society and how individuals negotiate their desires and fantasies in a world that often constrains them.

  9. Between the superhero and the supercrip: Deaf Gain, exceptionalism and normality in contemporary film and television
    Gemma King · pp. 839-852 · online 2026-03-08
    摘要

    The inspirational ‘supercrip’ has long been a staple of deaf film narrative, but the 2010s and 2020s have seen an explosion in new screen portrayals of superheroes whose powers operate as extreme examples of Deaf Gain. Deaf superheroes both challenge the supercrip trope by resituating their skills in a fantastical context, and perpetuate it through their continued focus on exceptional bodies. Confronting this paradox through analyses of several recent characterizations, this article investigates the deaf superhero corpus’ potential to make a radical contribution to screen representation, not only through its portrayal of deaf exceptionalism, but of deaf normality.

  10. “All that from two symbols?!”: an Adolescence roundtable
    Rachel Anna Billington, Rosemary Overell, Brett Nicholls, Michael S. Daubs, Catherine Fowler · pp. 853-868 · online 2026-02-19
    摘要

    Adolescence (2025) is a hit television show that has sparked widespread debate in both the press and in government houses. In this collaboratively written response, we consider how the show might index relatively new social phenomena – that is, involuntary celibate (incel) culture, social isolation, anxiety about young people’s engagement with social mediascapes – but also reproduce older social fears, such as threats to hegemonic institutions and the ‘power’ of media. In particular, we discuss the multiple ways that Stephen Graham et al.’s production at both the aesthetic and representational levels, grapples with structures, such as patriarchy, class and state institutional power (e.g. the prison, the school, the psychiatric discourse) through an attention to how ‘symbols’ are articulated through the programme, but also how Adolescence sparked more popular ‘hot takes’, hand wringing and celebration. We conclude that the show is compelling because it reproduces and problematizes through the immediacy of the one-take style the well-worn themes of vulnerable youth and family in peril.

  1. Would you like spice with that? social media spaces as the new gatekeepers of sexuality in young adult fantasy fiction
    Kristine Moruzi, Elizabeth Little · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    A recent internet phenomenon includes readers creating online reviews that identify the level and type of sexual content found in a particular book by grading the ‘spice’. Using chilli emojis, the ratings range from mild to extra spicy. As spice levels are increasingly used to discuss popular romance and fantasy novels, interrogation of how books aimed at young adult readers (typically understood to be between 13 and 18 years of age) are being rated and reviewed is needed. This article examines social media content focusing on discussions of spice levels in young adult fantasy and argues that this content defines rigid boundaries between appropriate and inappropriate representations of sexuality in these texts. It reflects concerns that young readers are being exposed to more explicit sexual content through their reading and attempts to assert that certain levels of spiciness are only appropriate in adult fantasy.

  2. The magic of investment podcasts: finfluencers as ideological intermediaries
    Merja Porttikivi, Ella Lillqvist · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-10-04
    摘要

    Investment podcasts have emerged as influential platforms where financial influencers, ‘finfluencers’, shape beliefs and normalize finance as part of everyday life. This study examines three popular Finnish investment podcasts through the lens of ‘magical capitalism’ to analyse how neoliberal ideology is propagated by finfluencers. Finfluencers act as ‘ideological intermediaries’, portraying investing as a route to magical transformation and protection enabled by the power of financial capitalism. Our results show that investment success is depicted as a magical transformation achieved through initiation rites, worshipping gurus and adopting the proper magical beliefs and aims. These podcasts function as mediatized rites, by developing discourses that merge practical investing guidance with compelling storytelling to frame financial capitalism as both accessible and aspirational. While making financial topics relatable, finfluencers contribute to the financialisation of everyday life by embedding investing into daily routines and discourses. This study advances research on podcasts and influencer culture by focusing on the role of finfluencers as ideological intermediaries and the ways they disseminate hegemonic neoliberal discourse.

  3. The missing sounds and voices of Asian American independent media: Chinese cable television (CCTV, 1975–1983)
    Klavier Jie-Ying Wang · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    This article examines Chinese Cable Television (CCTV, 中文電視台, 1975–1983), America’s first Asian American public-access television, as a crucial yet overlooked constituent of the first-wave Asian American independent media (AAIM). While previous scholarship focuses primarily on filmic representations and institutional filmmaking, this study shifts attention to the domestic medium of television and the democratising technology of portable video. Grounded in archival research encompassing digitised cablecast tapes and organisational ephemeral documents, I argue that CCTV represented a vital constituent of the broader Asian American media counterpublic sphere. Drawing on counterpublic sphere theory, this analysis examines the station’s mobilisation of political and media resources to reveal two key interventions. First, CCTV operated as a ‘voice’ in AAIM, utilising favourable broadcast policies and public arts funding to deliver community-centred programmes into domestic spaces. Second, it generated disruptive ‘noise’ by pioneering guerrilla television-style video activism, grounded on the aesthetic and material flexibilities of portable video. CCTV thereby challenged mainstream media representations of activism, especially their neglect of Asian Americans. Ultimately, this media case provides lessons for confronting contemporary challenges by demonstrating the fundamental importance of diversity and empowerment–oriented policies and media tools.

  4. Religious aesthetics and feminist containment in Rosalía: the disciplining of excess between cooptation and resistance
    Sonia Núñez Puente, Diana Fernández Romero · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-09-25
    摘要

    This article examines the disciplining of ‘feminist excess’ in public reception and Rosalía’s aesthetic repositioning in the context of the emergence of her album Lux (2025), situating her reluctance to explicitly identify as a feminist in a cultural scenario marked by the resurgence of religious youth movements, the advance of anti-feminist tendencies, and post-#MeToo disputes over the conditions of visibility and legitimacy of feminism. Based on a corpus of 40 journalistic pieces published in Spanish media, the article applies a model of critical discourse analysis based on the operationalization of the concept of excess in two analytical dimensions: as an epistemic device that organizes the politics of excess between discipline and resistance, and as a dynamic category linked to the redefinition of the thresholds of the intelligible. From this perspective, the article proposes to address containment not only as a technology of regulation, but as a space in which the limits of the intelligibility of feminist political activism and its discursive representation are renegotiated.

  5. Thirty years of running together: mothers who run with strollers in Runner’s World magazine 1993–2022
    Clarissa Carden, Kayla Mildren · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-09-23
    摘要

    The popularity of distance running has increased dramatically since the 1960s and 1970s. Alongside that popularity has been an increased potential for inclusion, with one especially visible cohort being parents who run with their infants or young children in strollers. Focusing on the popular lifestyle magazine Runner’s World over the 30-year period 1993–2022, this article seeks to understand the discursive construction of the stroller-pushing runner-mother. It argues that mothers who align with conventional conceptions of the ‘good mother’, managing their athletic engagement independently while parenting and making no demands on the running community, are valorized by the magazine. This article highlights the limits of inclusivity in running communities and the importance of recognizing mothers as a unique cohort in sporting cultures.

    Kexin Han, Haerin Shin · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-09-15
    摘要

    Recent AI horror narratives have emerged as privileged cultural sites wherein posthuman anxiety, racialized othering and parental affect converge with peculiar intensity. This essay examines M3GAN (2022) as a symptomatic text that organizes contemporary fears surrounding technological companionship, childhood vulnerability and what I term ‘AI Peril’ – a media-cultural formation operating through logics of hierarchical discrimination historically associated with Yellow Peril discourse. This analysis interrogates how the film’s horror aesthetics naturalize particular affective orientations towards nonhuman entities, producing a grammar of technological fear that rehearses colonial patterns while appearing ideologically neutral; it argues, further, that this racialized grammar is inseparable from the film’s gendering of menace, the displacement of a neoliberal crisis of feminine care onto an idealized, white-coded android.
