Convergence

Vol. 32, No. 5 · 2026-10 · 30 篇 · SAGE · Media
  1. Utopian media studies: Introduction
    Steve Jankowski, Jakko Kemper · pp. 1465-1482 · online 2026-07-30
    摘要

    This introduction to the special issue ‘Utopian Media Studies’ sketches a framework for how media studies can become a site of utopian praxis, research and experimentation. It first addresses three apprehensions media scholars might have about the notion of utopian media studies and its appeal to a loaded concept like ‘utopia’. The introduction then outlines five sensibilities to explicate the nature and significance of this special issue’s contribution to the field: a utopian media studies must adopt an ecological approach to media; recognize and be invigorated by limits; work toward the technological liberation of time; connect imagination with practice; and emphasize and enable conditions for transmitting hope. Lastly, the introduction considers Ruth Levitas’s distinction between utopia’s archaeological, ontological, and architectural mode to map some of the concrete ways in which existing media studies research is doing utopian work that aligns with these sensibilities. In connecting work that operates in these modes with the various contributions to this special issue, the introduction reveals the utopian currents that already run through media studies as a discipline and opens up new conceptual and methodological approaches to studying and modeling digital utopias.

  2. Digital Queers’ cyberutopia after the end of history
    Sam Hunter · pp. 1483-1500 · online 2026-04-08
    摘要

    A non-profit organization called Digital Queers was founded in 1992 in San Francisco, California, to organize queer tech workers’ capital, knowledge, and labor for queer activism. This article draws upon archival research to historically trace Digital Queers’ efforts to raise funds, conduct computer trainings for queer activists, and distribute computer equipment to other non-profit organizations including the National Gay and Lesbian Task Force and Gay and Lesbian Alliance Against Defamation. I argue that Digital Queers’ media activism demonstrated “cyberutopian” desires for digital computation to produce better living which integrated queer activist practices to the American technology industry’s political economy. This orientation towards liberal reformist accommodation with capitalist systems questions the automatic equation of queer utopianism with anti-capitalist futurity which has appeared in queer media studies after the influential work of José Esteban Muñoz. The example of Digital Queers shows how utopian media studies must fully account for utopia’s social functions rather than presuming that the presence of utopian traces is sufficient cause for celebration. Rather than dismissing utopian methodologies, this article calls for a historically materialist approach to utopian media studies which unpacks the utopian interests of Digital Queers’ cyberutopianism and considers how utopian desires can support capitalist hegemony.

  3. The vernacular web: A notion at the intersection of media theory and a utopian web imaginary
    Anya Shchetvina, Nathalie Fridzema · pp. 1501-1521 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    This article critically examines the vernacular web as a notion that exists in conceptual ambiguity between Media and Internet Studies, Web History, and artistic and activist practice. Drawing on Ruth Levitas’s argument that utopian implications are often embedded within the Social Sciences and can be critically excavated (2013), it argues that the vernacular web operates across two registers: an analytical concept describing amateur, non-institutional, and ostensibly autonomous forms of online practice, and a temporally grounded imaginary that invests the early web with utopian value. By analysing the reciprocity between these two registers, the article traces how utopian assumptions about autonomy, participation, and digital well-being circulate across scholarship, historical narratives, and activist practice. Through the cases of Olia Lialina's essays and The Yesterweb, it identifies three constituent values of functional autonomy, while arguing that this imaginary remains half-baked: oriented towards aesthetics and affect rather than a reflection on the feasibility, limitations and potential risks.

  4. Virtual enchantment: Rethinking engagement in social VR through the Better World Museum
    Mai Ibrahim · pp. 1522-1538 · online 2026-06-13
    摘要

    This article develops the concept of virtual enchantment to analyze how immersive digital environments generate affective experiences that both resist and are recuperated by platform capitalism’s extractive logics. Drawing on Bennett’s understanding of enchantment as wonder that exceeds habitual perception, virtual enchantment describes affective intensity that momentarily interrupts habitual patterns of interaction, creating conditions for contemplative engagement and speculative imagination. Through an autoethnographic phenomenological analysis of the Better World Museum: Angl the Artiste, a virtual museum within Meta’s Horizon Worlds celebrating Black artistic production, this study examines how enchanting encounters operate within and against corporate virtual reality platforms. The analysis unfolds in two parts. Part I demonstrates how the museum’s design generates genuine aesthetic encounters through curatorial strategies, spatial arrangements, and interactive affordances. Drawing on Levitas’s concepts of ontological and architectural utopia and Benjamin’s framework of radical imagination, this section analyzes how enchantment cultivates contemplative engagement and positions the museum within Afrofuturist traditions that reimagine technology as a site for Black creative agency. Part II examines the material infrastructures structuring these experiences. Drawing on platform studies and critical race scholarship, this section analyzes how Meta’s surveillance architecture, racialized labor divisions, and algorithmic selection mechanisms simultaneously produce and commodify enchantment, revealing that virtual enchantment operates within platform capitalism as a structural tension. The article argues that virtual enchantment must be understood dialectically: as simultaneously genuine and recuperated, resistant and extractive, liberatory and complicit. By refusing to resolve this tension, the analysis advances utopian media studies as an orientation treating digital infrastructures not solely as sites of critique but as spaces where enchantment can sustain hope, generate ephemeral collectivities, and cultivate speculative possibilities within extractive conditions. Virtual enchantment offers not a dismissal of platform critique but a strategy for sustaining communal engagement and imaginative possibility within and against digital capitalism.

  5. Popular digital sovereignty and digital real utopias: Lessons from the Homeless Workers’ Movement in Brazil
    Rafael Grohmann, Alexandre Costa Barbosa · pp. 1539-1556 · online 2026-05-29
    摘要

    This article traces the ongoing construction of “popular digital sovereignty” within Brazil’s Homeless Workers’ Movement (MTST), through its Tech Sector, arguing that the concept itself operates as a digital real utopia: an aspirational horizon made workable through imperfect and situated practice. Rather than treating digital sovereignty as a state-centric policy project, the movement frames popular digital sovereignty as a from-below definition forged through epistemic sovereignty, the collective control over how knowledge is produced, debated, and circulated inside and beyond the movement. Drawing on a workers’ inquiry approach conducted since 2021, including interviews, observations, workshops, and co-produced materials, the article analyzes how the MTST’s Tech Sector develops this concept through learning in struggle: iterative trial-and-error, learning by doing, and continuous revision in response to the material constraints of organizing. We show how popular digital sovereignty is assembled through territorial and class-based priorities, and how its meaning shifts as the movement grapples with the role of the state, infrastructural dependency, and the need to “meet workers where they are.” The article contributes to digital sovereignty studies and utopian media studies by conceptualizing popular digital sovereignty as an epistemic practice and a prefigurative, unfinished project, an effort to build tech otherwise from below under conditions of constraint.

  6. Playful green utopias: Analysing solarpunk imaginaries in independent video games
    Marta Fernández-Ruiz, Lasse Loepfe · pp. 1557-1583 · online 2026-04-18
    摘要

    This study assesses climate imaginaries and utopian proposals within solarpunk video games, understanding solarpunk as a subgenre of speculative fiction that mainly envisions sustainable futures in opposition to today’s prevailing dystopian narratives. Employing a mixed-methods approach that combines thematic and content analysis of 62 titles from the Itch.io and Steam platforms, the research identifies five recurring thematic categories organized along two principal axes: one emphasizing social welfare and community, and another focussing on energy transition and the relationship between urban environments and nature. The findings indicate that indie solarpunk video games predominantly articulate critical utopias, fostering reflection on identity, ethics, and morality in response to the climate crisis from decolonial and posthumanist perspectives. By incorporating Levitas’ concept of ‘utopianism as method’, it is argued that video games offer specific affordances to amplify utopianism, allowing one to transcend the reading of possible futures to actively perform and experience them. Through interactivity and agency, players can simulate alternative sociopolitical systems in real time, positioning the medium as a laboratory for utopian practice that facilitates rehearsal of desirable responses to ecological emergencies in ways unattainable by non-interactive media.

  7. From critique to hope: Infrawalking, defamiliarisation, transgressive infrastructuring and utopian engineering
    Fieke Jansen, Maxigas, Niels ten Oever · pp. 1584-1599 · online 2026-04-13
    摘要

    This article explores how methodological innovation can move media and infrastructure studies from critique toward hope. Building on the infrastructural turn in media studies, we argue that research must go beyond diagnosing power asymmetries to co-creating utopian alternatives that centre people and planet over capital and control. Drawing on experiments from the critical infrastructure lab, we reflect on four methods: infrastructure walks, transgressive infrastructuring, defamiliarisation, and utopian engineering. In these methods, we swap the pairs of infrastructure/governance, discourse/materiality, and values/objects in order to cultivate alternative technological trajectories, thereby unsettling assumptions that current technological developments are inevitable. Rather than treating infrastructures as closed systems, these approaches invite participants, including researchers, policymakers, activists, artists, and industry actors into collaborative processes of relational knowledge production. We argue that such methods embody ‘utopia as a method’ by creating spaces of uncertainty and experimentation that render infrastructures visible, contestable, and reconfigurable. In doing so, they generate not only critique but also openings for collective utopian agendas in an attempt to co-develop alternative infrastructural futures that centre people and planet over capital and control.

  8. The Futures Glossary: An experiment in utopic pedagogy
    Steven Logan, Kate Maddalena · pp. 1600-1620 · online 2026-05-18
    摘要

    In this essay, we describe a project that asks students to edit and contribute to a collaborative “Futures Glossary” across two undergraduate media studies courses about the future and futurisms. Both courses focus on critique and critical creative practice; that is, they require students to actively imagine various futures using words and images, while also considering theories of technology. Our glossary asks students to work as editors, curators, and contributors. The whole point is the doing of these things, not the end result—the glossary itself is a space for practice that also centers practices. We situate our project first as an intervention in the instrumentalization of the university as an institution, then in terms of other glossary projects. We then establish the keywording aspect of our project as a utopian practice that befits a class on futures, and we establish the artistic aspect as part of ongoing conversations about collective imagination work, especially Afro- and indigenous futurisms. Finally, we describe how the project works in our classrooms and offer some preliminary examples of work-in-progress.

  9. Digital ecologies of hope: A natural history of emerging media futures
    Abu Abdelbagi, Shell Avenant, Inara Bezerra, Meier Clark, Shoshana Gottesman, Julio Intriago Izquierdo et al. · pp. 1621-1647 · online 2026-07-08
    摘要

    Digital Ecologies of Hope is a collaboratively produced field guide that documents how people use media to sustain care, resilience, and imagination in the face of technological, political, and social constraints. Drawing inspiration from naturalist field guides and critical fabulation, the project reinterprets everyday practices as “specimens” of digital hope, each annotated with classifications, habitats, conservation statuses, field notes, and illustrations. These specimens span diverse contexts—from Sudanese youth defending theses during civil war to Afro-Brazilian students reclaiming identity in elite institutions, to transgender voice archives on TikTok and youth activist networks resisting surveillance in schools.   10. Digital spectacles, convergent realities: Deconstructing the sport-media-business nexus in an era of extended media
    Alexander Yu. Krouglov · pp. 1648-1665 · online 2025-12-22
    摘要

    This article explores the transformative effects of digital media on the sport-media-business nexus. The approach is an analytical focus on a wide range of sports, examined from a theoretical framework that integrates institutional and organizational perspectives on mediatization with critical theories of hyperreality and the society of the spectacle. Based on a deconstruction of contemporary global sport, it is demonstrated that digital platforms are a major concern across the entire sporting landscape and that a new wave of mediatization is reconfiguring the nature of spectatorship and fandom. Still, many aspects of this digitally inflected reality are in a state of flux, with emergent forms of community and engagement existing alongside new modalities of control and commercialization. Consequently, one of the significant effects of convergent media is the dispersion of the sporting experience across multiple platforms, involving a concurrent increase in the complexity of power dynamics between leagues, media corporations, and audiences. Mediatization is, therefore, a process operating at many levels and at various speeds, which also takes sport in diverse and often contradictory directions. Employing the theoretical lenses of Baudrillard, Debord, and Turner, updated through contemporary scholarship on convergence culture, it is argued and demonstrated that the fusion of sport and convergent media demands a critical examination of platform governance, datafication, and the very future of community in an increasingly hyperreal world. This research offers a new perspective by establishing a conceptual framework for sociological inquiries into sport’s technological and economic interface. This analysis engages directly with recent scholarship to update and sharpen the critique of commercialized sport, concluding that the evolving sportscape necessitates a critical interrogation of its intricate power relations.

  11. Beyond age marginalization: Understanding the success of older influencers on Douyin
    Yingwen Wang, Hui Lin · pp. 1666-1684 · online 2025-12-11
    摘要

    Older content creators gain popularity on Douyin and become a significant part of the platform economy. However, much existing research concentrates on young, beauty content creators with limited attention paid to older content creators. Drawing on the theoretical framework of digital labour and ageing studies, this article examines the reasons why and how older adults become influencers on Douyin, how these creators gain a follower base on such a youth-led platform as a marginalized group, and how they navigate precarity and challenges to achieve visibility on Douyin. Based on in-depth interviews and participant observation with older Chinese Wanghong creators, we identify four key motivations driving their participation in short-video production: personal fulfilment and expression, social engagement and community contribution, economic empowerment, and strategic account development. By foregrounding older Wanghong’s experiences in China’s platform economy, the paper extends theories of digital labour to a new demographic, showing how cultural values and generational context inflect the influencer phenomenon. This study contributes to ongoing debates surrounding the digital economy, self-representation, and ageing by illuminating specific mechanisms through which older Wanghong creators strategically blend entrepreneurial aspirations with communal goals in their online engagements. We conclude with implications for platform inclusivity and a call to recognize the diverse actors shaping influencer culture in the global digital era.

  12. ‘Simulating fan discourse shifts: A thematic coding and regression model of K-pop communities under China’s Korean Wave Ban (2016–2017)’
    Ronald Yi Xiu Wu, Tianguang Meng · pp. 1685-1710 · online 2025-11-11
    摘要

    This paper examines Chinese K-pop fan communities’ responses to the 2016–2017 Korean Wave Ban, which restricted the circulation of Korean cultural materials amid diplomatic tensions. The study asks whether fan discourse – previously shaped by South Korea’s more democratic and individualistic environment – would shift toward state ideology or preserve alternative orientations once outside influences were curtailed. Drawing on a dataset of Weibo posts before and after the ban, thematic coding and regression analysis track how online dialogue evolved. Findings show a sharp rise in debates about policy and governance, but no corresponding increase in nationalist or security-driven rhetoric; rather than embracing state narratives or engaging in overt resistance, fans adapted by reframing entertainment discussions into institutional or bureaucratic terms. This strategy of maneuvering reflects coping within constraints while maintaining group identity. The study thus conceptualizes fan communities as emotional and discursive enclaves, whose expressive strategies flexibly adjust to regulation. It highlights how cultural governance reshapes digital participation without reducing fandom to a passive vehicle of state ideology.

  13. Drawing practices as an interface for transnational collaboration: An expanded studio between Durban and Antwerp
    Janna Beck, Michelle Stewart, Bhavna Pather · pp. 1711-1731 · online 2026-06-03
    摘要

    This article explores how collective digital drawing can function as a minimal interface for building and sustaining transnational collaboration. Central to this collaboration is FRAMED a low-threshold, open-source platform for real-time collective drawing, designed to operate reliably under unequal infrastructural conditions. Over a multi-year period, drawing was deployed not primarily to produce images, but to rehearse collaboration: a shared performative act through which trust, shared authorship, and institutional alignment gradually emerged. The article examines the methodological, pedagogical, and infrastructural dimensions of this practice across diverse artistic and educational contexts, including public festivals, community-based workshops, off-grid environments, and large-scale transnational drawing sessions such as Poetry Africa, the Artfluence Human Rights Festival, AfrikaBurn, and the Royal Academy Drawing Marathon. Situated within frameworks of the nomadic subject, relational aesthetics, and critical approaches to infrastructure, the analysis argues that technological sophistication is not the primary driver of sustainable collaboration. Instead, autonomy, maintainability, and the ethics of shared traces prove more consequential than feature-rich systems. The article concludes by demonstrating how collective digital drawing enabled curricular integration and by outlining the transferability of lightweight, adaptable setups across educational, community-based, and off-grid environments.

  14. Recycling as ‘IP extractivism’ in the age of platformization: A comparative analysis of Netflix, Amazon Prime video, and Disney+
    Eduard Cuelenaere · pp. 1732-1752 · online 2026-01-29
    摘要

    Streamers have become central to the production, circulation, and consumption of audiovisual content, reshaping how cultural industries manage creative risk and exploit intellectual property (IP). I argue that, within this platformized environment, content recycling – including (re-)adaptations, remakes, sequels, spin-offs, and prequels – has emerged not as a residual practice, but as a core operational logic. The article interrogates how recycling practices are structurally embedded within the economic and infrastructural design of global video-on-demand (VOD) services. Building on an interdisciplinary theoretical framework, the study draws on a comparative, cross-platform dataset covering all ‘Original’ fictional feature films released by Netflix (2015–2024), Amazon Prime Video (2018–2024), and Disney+ (2019–2024). Through this dataset, the article identifies both convergence and variation in how major streamers operationalize recycled content as a form of ‘IP extractivism’ – that is, the systematic transformation of existing IP into modular, extensible, and data-optimized content assets. The analysis indicates that, while all three streamers rely heavily on recycled content, the forms this recycling takes vary in ways that align with their different corporate configurations. In the dataset, Netflix’s Original film slate is dominated by speculative, adaptation-driven recycling, Amazon Prime Video’s by a mix of acquisitions and selective franchise extensions, and Disney + by patterns consistent with an integrated, conglomerate IP portfolio. The article argues that, under conditions of platformization, recycled IP functions as a privileged asset class: its modularity, data legibility, and retention potential align closely with logics of algorithmic governance and assetization. Conceptualizing recycling as IP extractivism, the article reconsiders originality as controlled variation within pre-enclosed worlds and highlights the implications of this shift for cultural diversity, rights ownership, and the sustainability of smaller production ecosystems.

  15. Window-shopping the far-right: A comparative study of alt-tech social media homepages
    Jonathan Collins · pp. 1753-1774 · online 2026-01-31

  16. Defining pink games: Feminine aesthetic, play experience, and skillset presumptions of PEGI-3 girls
    Lucy Lloyd, Leighton Evans · pp. 1775-1793 · online 2026-04-08
    摘要

    This article defines and critically examines the ‘pink game’ as a gendered design category in commercial video games targeted at young girls. Drawing on feminist game studies, we analyse eight PEGI 3–7 titles published by Outright Games, using the MDA Framework, Schell’s Elemental Tetrad, and a visual analysis to identify how femininity is encoded through mechanics, aesthetics, narratives, and avatar design. Our findings show that pink games are structurally defined by minimal mechanical complexity, low risk play, and aesthetics rooted in relational behaviour, beauty ideals, and emotional labour. By contrast, games marketed to boys encourage mastery, autonomy, and competitive engagement. We argue that pink games reproduce a narrow vision of girlhood that limits agency and reinforces gender stereotypes through design rather than narrative alone. This article contributes an empirically grounded definition of the pink game and offers a structural critique of how gender is materialised in children’s play experiences.

  17. Trash or Treasure: A proposed typology of bad videogames
    Hollie Wistow, David Antognoli · pp. 1794-1813 · online 2026-03-14
    摘要

    Although critically maligned and popularly derided videogames are a persistent part of gaming culture, scholarly work has scarcely acknowledged the different ways in which games are understood as bad. This article disambiguates bad videogames by providing a critical overview of the various manifestations of badness in games. Through examining a series of case studies, we propose a framework for understanding how a videogame can be perceived as bad, focusing on presumed developer intentions and reception by players and game journalists. We chart four distinct yet interconnected categories of bad videogames: So-Bad-It’s-Bad, So-Bad-It’s-Good, Bad-in-Context, and Intentionally Bad. Rather than identifying specific sources of badness, these categories aim to clarify how badness impacts audience understanding and appreciation of videogames. At the same time, by focusing on the bad, uninspiring, and ugly side of videogames we take a political stance against the typical game studies canon by expanding the archive of what games and play experiences are conceived as legitimate and worthy of scholarly attention.

  18. Synchronising times: Waiting as a relational mechanism in simulative browser games
    Frederik Peper, Nico Wettmann · pp. 1814-1827 · online 2025-12-23
    摘要

    This article explores how waiting is an essential element in browser-based games. As a phenomenon that combines activity and passivity, waiting reflects the real-time mechanics of gameplay and illustrates the intertwining of simulative games with social and individual time structures. Drawing on a synthetic framework, the study demonstrates how players must synchronise their actions to align the game’s demands with their daily rhythms.   19. ‘This might sound like I’m wearing a tinfoil hat…’ – Knowledge workers’ perceptions of privacy and surveillance in group-based communicative AI
    Liisa A. Mäkinen, Minna Koivula, Mia Leppälä · pp. 1828-1847 · online 2025-12-23
    摘要

    This study examines knowledge workers’ perspectives on privacy implications and surveillant functionalities of group-based communicative AI in the context of work. The objective is to connect the ongoing examination of communicative AI in the workplace to research on privacy and employee surveillance, and to use the framework of privacy as contextual integrity as a theoretical lens through which some of the user perspectives on communicative AI tools are analyzed. Building on 33 qualitative interviews with Finnish knowledge workers, who were presented with scenarios of communicative AI functioning as a ‘team member’ in a work-related chat group, we find that knowledge workers recognize several privacy-related risks in using group-based communicative AI at work, and commonly use surveillant reasoning and logics to make sense of the suggested technology and its privacy implications. We present three distinct approaches knowledge workers had toward privacy in this setting: the detached, the compartmentalized, and the affective. Our findings suggest a wider need to consider communicative AI’s agency and role as an actor contributing to increasing privacy concerns. We highlight how, due to its human-like nature, data-reliant functionalities, and potential capabilities when operating in a group setting, the technology introduces negative affective responses and novel concerns related to privacy and employee surveillance. Specifically, privacy concerns relating to communicative AI expand beyond mere data protection issues to include concerns of how the technology is used by management, how the technology itself interacts within the organization, and how it will develop in the future.

  20. Memes, gatekeepers, and the dynamics of viral K-pop diffusion on YouTube: Analyzing user engagement and network structures
    Shin Haeng Lee, Daseul Oh · pp. 1848-1865 · online 2025-12-28
    摘要

    YouTube’s multilayered ecology enables K-pop virality to evolve from grassroots remix to corporate-driven promotion; however, how the meso-level interplay between gatekeepers and memes shapes user engagement remains under-explored. To bridge this gap, we applied an integrated media consumption/choice theoretical approach and a rhizome perspective to commenter-overlap networks for four COVID-era hits— Rollin’ , After LIKE , Attention , and Event Horizon. We then estimated exponential random graph models to contrast top-down promotion with bottom-up sharing. Three key findings emerged. First, mid-tier channels act as decisive brokers. Situated between entertainment mega-channels and grassroots creators, they fuse otherwise disconnected subnetworks, a capacity that scales with event magnitude and drives mass virality. Second, meme genres foster engagement, but do so homophilously: commenter overlaps concentrate within the same meme type, confirming that memes are catalysts of participatory rather than top-down, diffusion. Third, traffic is asymmetrical: links probabilities rise when users move from memes to mid-tier channels, but not vice versa, indicating that meme communities pull attention inward rather than gatekeepers pushing it outward. The results show that K-pop visibility is continuously negotiated between platform-level concentration anchored by elite hubs and rhizomatic diversification orchestrated by meme-driven mid-tier actors. By refining political-economy accounts of algorithmic dominance, the study offers empirical evidence for policy aimed at safeguarding cultural diversity within platform-dominated music industries.

    摘要

  22. Platform super user: Examining Google Arts & Culture’s cultural production
    Bethany Berard, Sarah E. K. Smith · pp. 1883-1900 · online 2026-01-02
    摘要

    Current scholarship on platforms does not fully account for how platforms produce content. To demonstrate this limitation, we examine the platform Google Arts and Culture (GAC) revealing distinct forms of cultural production. While GAC functions as a repository, allowing users access to objects, sites, and exhibitions, it is more than a digitization project. GAC is a content producer providing narrativized content, as well as games, and opportunities to curate digital collections and produce creative projects. Unpacking these dimensions, our study demonstrates how cultural production functions on GAC and the users that this enables. We argue that GAC facilitates four types of cultural production for distinct groups of cultural producers. Focussing on the cultural production of the platform, we further contend that GAC occupies a unique hybrid role as facilitator and author. We point to this authorial role because it is largely obscured. Assessing how GAC wields cultural control, we characterize its distinct functionality as a ‘super user’ on the platform: facilitating end-user engagement with content, moderating the content uploaded to the platform by institutions, and producing and promoting its own curated content. Analyzing GAC’s affordances as a super user extends our theorization of platformized cultural production with respect to non-monetized platforms. Based on these findings, we call for scholars to attend to the ways in which platforms can function as cultural actors, in addition to their role as technical intermediaries structuring the means of engagement.

  26. Beyond Bricks and Mortar: How CEO digital vision in smart cities impacts tourism resilience and digital heritage preservation
    Meng-Chen Zhang, Hongru Zhang, Ying Wei, Wen Wang · pp. 1965-1984 · online 2025-11-19
    摘要

    This study investigates the causal impact of CEO digital vision within smart cities on tourism enterprises’ resilience and digital heritage preservation. Employing a Difference-in-Differences quasi-experimental design, we find that firms led by digitally visionary CEOs in smart cities experience significantly greater increases in revenue stability and faster recovery times, alongside enhanced digital heritage digitization and engagement.   27. My love will be there forever: Ghostbots and the artificial continuing communication
    Bibo Lin · pp. 1985-2005 · online 2026-01-15
    摘要

    The advent of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, such as machine learning (ML) and large language models (LLMs), allows ordinary users to turn themselves or their loved ones into AI chatbots. Based on the analysis of media reporting on users’ experiences, the study identifies three major features that frequently appear in users’ interactions with ghostbots simulating their deceased loved ones: (1) the interactions often elicit intense emotional responses from the users; (2) the purposes of using ghostbots can be practical, including mitigating grief, seeking advices, looking for emotional support, or say a final goodbye; and (3) the interactions may generate new knowledge, ideas, and memories about the dead. The study also introduces the concept of artificial continuing communication , recognizing the interpretative asymmetry inherent in interactions with AI interlocutors, while emphasizing the continuing bonds between the living and the dead. I argue that although ghosts are reduced versions of the dead, this emerging form of communication challenges the normative hydraulic model of the psyche, which views grief as a negative emotion to be discharged and emphasizes emotional detachment from the deceased. I contend that a type of thin reciprocal love exists between the users and their ghostbots and suggest that the ghostbot use offers an alternative perspective on how we relate to love, loss, and emotional continuity, albeit its user base remains small.

  28. Vocal infrastructuring in the smart home
    Marie Ertner, Stina Hasse Jørgensen · pp. 2006-2024 · online 2026-01-16
    摘要

    This paper explores the phenomenon and practices of making vocal home automation through an ethnographic study of the emergent relations between domestic digital voice assistants (DVAs), older users and their homes.   1. ‘Flirting’ with the audience: Broadway Cinematheque’s participation, play, and affective economics on Threads
    Hannah Poon · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    This article presents Broadway Cinematheque, Hong Kong’s arthouse cinema, as a case study of a brand-participatory affective economics enabled by social media. Since the launch of Threads in 2023, Broadway Cinematheque has become an active participant in the local online film network with its cinephile-like persona and witty promotional strategies. Meanwhile, audiences frequently reference the cinema or appropriate its content for self-expression and play. Focussing on their engagement practices and the platform mediality of Threads, this article argues that Broadway Cinematheque does not simply exploit audiences but ‘flirts’ with them in intimate, playful, and performative ways. By delineating four forms of ‘flirting’ between the cinema and its audiences – participatory intimacy, nostalgic appropriation, myth-making play, and ironic self-roasting, it formulates ‘flirting’ as a highly networked, participatory, and contingent mode of engagement embedded in the platform mediality of Threads. Because of this contingency, it has the potential to open up ways of meaning making and affective patterning of the audience that redistribute, overspill, or destabilise the cinema’s economics.

  2. “Gender essentialism is cringe”: Brazilian and Ukrainian queer players’ strategies for queering character interactions
    Mark Maletska, Thais Arrias Weiller · online 2026-10-23
    摘要

    This article presents the strategies that Brazilian transgender and gender diverse (TGD) players use to queer character interactions and accommodate diverse gender identities into video games, and aims to show the differences in queering strategies between Brazilian and Ukrainian players. Reflexive thematic analysis was used on survey data gathered from Brazilian and Ukrainian players. The main finding reveals the strategies for queering character interactions distinguished in Brazilian players’ responses, namely, (1) playing with an idealised self, (2) queering binary player characters, and (3) rejecting non-queerness. The results show similar ways of approaching gender binaries and treating them as queer (able), with coinciding reasoning behind. Brazilian respondents also reflected on the significance of player characters as “additional” or “alternative” bodies to experiment with, which differed from Ukrainian players seeking queerness while doubting the potential of video game spaces to accommodate their identities. The results highlight the need for further culturally informed and geographically diverse research on TGD players’ experiences in video games. The findings reflect a similar approach to gender binaries in Brazilian and Ukrainian TGD players’ experience that differs from previous research. It is also shown how bodily focused Brazilian culture makes their experiences more focused on in-game bodies.

  3. Unfinished becoming: Seriality, temporality, and the making of literature on Wenxuecity
    Shaohua Guo · online 2026-10-23
    摘要

    This article examines Wenxuecity, a long-running website for overseas Chinese communities, as a site of diasporic literary production shaped by distinctive temporal practices. Drawing on the serialized fiction of two active contributors and sustained observation of forum discussions, it identifies three intertwined temporalities: narrative, processual, and archival. Narrative temporality foregrounds authors’ self-directed rhythms of storytelling; processual temporality traces how ongoing exchanges between authors and readers turn writing into a collective, open-ended practice; and archival temporality reveals how dispersed texts accumulate into a layered record of diasporic becoming. The article argues that these temporal practices sustain a mode of digital cultural production oriented toward continuity, participation, and lived experience—one that contests the extractive logics of platform economies rather than merely operating within them. As a fragile media archive, Wenxuecity holds cultural memory in the constitutive tension between accumulation and precarity.

  5. How platformisation transforms public service media: A new research agenda
    Catherine Johnson, Tim Raats, Hanne Bruun, Vilde Schanke Sundet · online 2026-10-18
    摘要

    The expansion of global digital platforms has significant implications for public service media (PSM), which now operate within a platform ecosystem in which a small number of transnational technology companies increasingly shape the conditions under which media are produced, distributed and consumed. In this introduction to a special issue of Convergence on ‘Comparative Approaches to PSM in the Age of Platforms’ we set out how platformisation fundamentally reshapes the institutional conditions under which PSM operate, challenges the public service remit, reconfigures core values and invites a reconsideration of PSM as a democratic institution. Arguing that this represents a profound transformation, we identify five key dimensions that distinguish platformisation from earlier processes of digitalisation: platforms have created a fundamentally different democratic environment; platforms operate at global scale; platforms create asymmetric dependencies; platforms alter the cultural logics of audiovisual production; platformisation challenges PSM’s normative foundations. Building on the Public Service Media in the Age of Platforms (PSM-AP) project, this introduction argues for the importance of comparative research for understanding this transformation and sets out an agenda for the future of PSM studies based around six core themes: values, ecosystem, audiences, portals, content and role. The introduction argues for the urgency in addressing these themes if we are to understand how PSM—as a systemic intervention designed to drive public and societal value—is to be preserved in the age of platforms.

    Aida Martori Munsant, Tania Fernández-Lombao, Azahara Cañedo · online 2026-10-15

  7. Ludo-Iconological Analysis: A new method for reading visual symbolism in games, with a case study on Blasphemous
    Baykar Demir · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    This article introduces Ludo-Iconological Analysis, a staged interpretive method for examining visual symbolism in digital games through an art historical perspective. Informed by Erwin Panofsky’s iconological framework, the method is adapted to the temporal and participatory conditions of games. While existing scholarship has demonstrated the value of analysing games through artistic sources and visual traditions, the present study develops a more explicitly structured framework for tracing how culturally coded imagery is activated and complicated within an interactive medium. The method is applied to Blasphemous (The Game Kitchen, 2019), a title distinguished by a dense religious visual language rooted in Spanish cultural history. Its symbolic vocabulary draws upon penitential garments, the crown of thorns, public devotional ritual, and historically situated works of sacred art without treating any single visual tradition as the privileged measure of symbolic complexity. This combination makes Blasphemous a particularly productive case for demonstrating Ludo-Iconological Analysis, revealing how historical, theological, and ideological meanings are reconfigured within the visual and interactive structures of the game. By introducing Ludo-Iconological Analysis, this study proposes a focused interpretive framework that integrates established principles of art historical research into the study of digital games. Rather than claiming universal applicability, the article demonstrates how the method can illuminate a game whose visual design is densely structured by historically and culturally coded motifs. The framework is intended as a basis for further comparative application to games in which symbolic imagery is meaningfully connected to narrative progression, player encounter, and interactive context.

  8. Immersive proximity to suffering and crisis: The democratic implications of distressing virtual reality content
    Rūta Kazlauskaitė · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    Non-fiction VR and 360-degree immersive video are widely used to depict the experiences of victimized, marginalized, and traumatized people, in both historical and contemporary contexts. Immersive VR content frequently engages topics of war and conflict, forced displacement, disability, precarity, incarceration, impact of climate change, and diverse forms of oppression and discrimination. Difficult social topics are introduced experientially, promising the audience an encounter with the lived realities of hardship, suffering, and injustice. The article argues that the democratic stakes of such uses of VR are best understood not by asking whether immersion produces empathy, but by asking what kinds of self–other relations and what kinds of agency are enacted and rehearsed through immersive proximity to suffering and crisis. Drawing on political psychology and indirect-trauma research, I show how immersive proximity to suffering and crisis can shape democratic dispositions via perceived control, threat salience, and victimhood framing. Immersive proximity to suffering and crisis may support solidarity and empathy, but it can also generate personal distress, feelings of helplessness, heightened threat sensitivity, and self-focused victimhood, which may undermine social trust, openness to pluralism, and a sense of civic efficacy. Engagement with distressing content in immersive media should enable reflective processing of difficult experiences, as well as support self–other differentiation, the sense of agency, hope, control, and efficacy, instead of leaving audiences feeling powerless and overwhelmed. The article advances a heuristic for evaluating and designing non-fiction VR depictions of suffering and crisis in ways that support democratic dispositions.

  11. Drawing boundaries: How creative practitioners navigate precarity in AI-embedded creative work
    Jie Huang, Graham Hitchen, Safak Dogan · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    As generative artificial intelligence (AI) continues to grow within creative industries, existing research has examined its implications for creative labour. However, much of this work emphasises AI’s impact on efficiency gains or creativity, offering limited insight into how practitioners adopt AI tools and negotiate human-AI boundaries in practical contexts under conditions of creative precarity. To bridge the gaps, this study applies boundary work theory to examine how creative practitioners redefine what remains distinctly human in AI-mediated creative production. Based on 21 interviews with practitioners in the UK film, gaming and live performance sectors, findings show that, in creative work, boundaries between human and AI systems are neither predefined nor passively adjusted to, but are actively shaped, expanded, and maintained in real-world settings. We argue that the adoption of AI is not a simple technical adjustment but a process through which professional value and the dynamic of creative work are reconsidered and re-constituted. Practitioners intentionally engage in boundary activities to navigate the unstable work environment associated with AI integration, enabling them to defend the role of creator. Theoretically, this study demonstrates the explanatory power of boundary work theory for understanding AI-embedded creative production and identifies that boundaries are not drawn by practitioners alone but through the interplay of technological capacities, project demands, and the legal environment. Practically, it suggests the need to redesign creative workflows and refresh professional skill sets to maintain meaningful human control while also facilitating efficient human-AI collaboration.

  12. Hydraen narrative: Conceptualising inclusive and resistant fiction
    R. Lyle Skains · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    Hélène Cixous reclaims the monstrous figure of the Medusa in l’écriture feminine . Discourse on feminist writing has widened to be more inclusive, though we do not yet have corresponding conceptualisation for “writing from without” that encapsulates marginalised authors and subversive texts as eloquently as Cixous does that of the feminine. This paper, arising from practice-based research in writing the hypernovel Seven Sisters Unmet , posits “Hydraen narrative” to refer to the depth and breadth of narratives written from outside and resisting the homogenising effects of dominant culture. Hydraen narratives are those that proliferate under suppression and reclaim demonised creative excess through previously termed “feminist” structures, styles, and/or content: polyvocality, lack of closure, repetition, ontological metelepsis, unnatural narrative techniques, embodiment, intra- and intertextuality, questions of family versus self, and explorations of sexuality and gender. Hydraen narrative using digital interfaces galvanises the dialectical, participatory relationship between creator and audience that can lead to social change and restored (and restorative) power.

  13. Global reach, local constraints: senior professionals on the streamers’ impact on creative power, autonomy, and authorship in the Flemish audiovisual industry
    Jens Van Landschoot, Daniel Biltereyst · online 2026-10-07
    摘要

    Streaming platforms have transformed opportunities for European filmmakers, providing global reach and essential additional funding. However, these platforms also challenge notions of creative autonomy and authorship. Insights derived from thirteen in-depth interviews with senior industry professionals from the Flemish film and audiovisual market – primarily filmmakers, producers, and other professional stakeholders – reveal the complexities of this evolving creative landscape. This offers deeper insights into the interrelated challenges of creative freedom and collaboration with streamers. Three key perspectives emerge. First, almost all interviewed professionals acknowledge streaming platforms’ importance regarding additional financing. Over the last decade, these have increasingly become important as additional investors in an ecosystem increasingly challenged by a strained economic environment. Second, stakeholders highlight concerns over creative control when working with streamers. Although these platforms offer vital resources, they often impose rigid guidelines to cater to algorithm-driven global audiences, limiting filmmakers’ autonomy and control over final cuts. The third perspective suggests a convergence of cinema, streaming formats, and creative practices. Many directors are drawn to the series format for its potential for extended storytelling and its capability to sustain audiences’ attention for longer periods. This interplay creates new creative opportunities but also blurs the lines between traditional cinematic and serialized narratives and content, while simultaneously fostering homogenization processes.

  14. Platformed nostalgia: Techlash and the commercialisation of historical social media nostalgia on Tumblr
    Briony Hannell · online 2026-10-07
    摘要

    This article conceptualises platformed nostalgia as a social media platform branding strategy that commodifies historical social media nostalgia to (re)gain social acceptance and legitimacy in response to popular techlash towards contemporary social media platforms. Drawing on a critical discourse analysis of 193 marketing and platform communication documents authored by and/or related to Tumblr under Automattic’s ownership (2019-), it argues that platformed nostalgia is cultivated through platform branding and communication strategies that instrumentalise users’ assumed nostalgic attachments to an imagined utopic past of early social media alongside normative platform values of transparency, democracy, and public good. The findings reveal that, in Tumblr’s case, the latter are invoked to position the platform as a nostalgic foil to real or imagined irresponsible or less responsible corporate social media platforms of the present, such as X (formerly Twitter). The analysis therefore explores how popular forms of historical social media nostalgia emerging from discontentment with and critique of the technocultural present are vulnerable to commercial co-optation by social media platforms.

  15. Expanded documentary and immersive technologies: Ludic logics and heritage mediation in ARTE’s interactive experiences
    Alba Anaya Domínguez, Ludovico Longhi · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article examines the transformation of contemporary documentary practices through immersive and interactive technologies, including virtual reality, augmented reality and game-based narrative structures. Positioned within debates on media convergence, the study argues that expanded documentary forms do not merely hybridise non-fiction with entertainment but reconfigure documentary epistemology through embodied immersion, procedural authorship and guided participation. Drawing on theories of expanded cinema, interactive documentary and game studies, the article clarifies the concept of ludic logics and distinguishes it from gamification and serious games. A qualitative comparative analysis of emblematic immersive documentary projects is conducted using a shared analytical grid focused on immersion, agency, narrative organisation and ethical positioning. The findings show that immersive non-fiction fosters situated and affective forms of knowledge while introducing new tensions between agency and authorial control. The article contributes to documentary and media convergence studies by providing a conceptually precise and methodologically replicable framework for analysing immersive non-fiction.

  1. Ethics of care and female sacrifice in Puella Magi Madoka Magica
    Sing Cheung · online 2026-10-30
    摘要

    Puella Magi Madoka Magica (2011) is an anime television series that imagines an alternate world in which teenage girls have served as magical girls, who fight against witches and stop them from cursing and killing people, for centuries. However, these girls’ sacrifices for their local communities will eventually drive them to despair and turn them into the witches. It is only through the titular character, Madoka, that the exploitative system can be disrupted. This paper engages with the ethics of care to interrogate the ways in which the anime show offers (re-)imaginations of female sacrifice, a notion that threads through the show. Care ethicists such as Nel Noddings (2013) and Inge van Nistelrooij (2014, 2017) have suggested that it is not necessarily a patriarchal idea for female subjects to sacrifice themselves; instead, it may be proper for one to sacrifice herself when it attests her identity and preserves her value. This paper argues that Puella Magi Madoka Magica simultaneously critiques the form of sacrifice that leads to the carer’s self-erasure and recasts sacrifice as a manifestation of the carer’s relational identity, self-care, and resistance to any systems that exploit (girls’) care.

  3. Television’s scalar play: Invitations to scalar thinking across the intimate and the global
    Sofia Bull · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    This article examines how contemporary television engages with scale, arguing that the medium routinely prompts viewers to navigate conceptually between micro and macro perspectives. Early Popular Visual Culture

Vol. 24, No. 1 · 2026-01-02 · 7 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Visual culture/Film history
  1. Movie halls and cinema-theatres. A failed ‘second birth’ of film exhibition in 1910s non-urban Lombardy
    Virgil Darelli · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-08-25
    摘要

    This article uses the Lombardy region as a case study to examine the emergence of cinemas in non-urban Italian areas between 1905 and 1920. The main hypothesis is that the so-called ‘second birth of cinema’ (the establishment of dedicated commercial movie halls with daily screenings, which followed the initial birth of film exhibition in non-dedicated venues) was unsuccessful in rural and small-town Italy, leading to the development of cinema-theatres instead. Unlike dedicated movie halls, cinema-theatres (often under the control of associations, including mutual-aid societies) were politicised, mainly active on weekends and open to different forms of entertainment. The persistence of cinema-theatres lasted for decades and indicates a slower commercialisation and autonomisation of leisure time. The article also addresses the methodological importance of accounting for geographical differences in the spread of cinemas of different types. The historical materials consulted are varied and come from state archives, the local press, and municipal archives, the latter of which have been underused thus far.

  2. Introducing and Institutionalizing the Projection Lantern into University Teaching in England and Scotland (1880-1914)*
    Jason Bate · pp. 20-46 · online 2026-08-17
    摘要

    While historians of the optical lantern in academic teaching at universities have rarely discussed the financial aspects of slide lectures, departmental funding was critical in accommodating the projected image as a teaching tool in the service of this educational project. Between 1880 and 1914, the governors and financial committees provided subsidies that were approved by administrators and departments, allowing the projection lantern to become institutionalized in some English and Scottish universities. During the 1880s, lecturers struggled to incorporate lanterns into their daily activities because the apparatus and slide making facilities were expensive and time-consuming. Between the 1890s and 1910s, funding for the sciences and photography departments resulted in the expansion and diversification of the academic lantern and slide use across multiple departments. Reading lantern practices through the analysis of financial records, manuals, and photography journals reveals that the practicalities of transitioning from benchtop demonstration to gas-lit lanterns to electrification stood in stark contrast to individual lecturer innovation and inert university infrastructure. This analysis sheds light on the development of the projection lantern as a teaching tool in England and Scotland, as well as why educational institutions like Yorkshire College, Edinburgh University, University of Glasgow, and University College Bristol were able to produce and store large quantities of lantern slides. Examining the integration of projection lanterns in academic teaching from a funding perspective uncovers the pedagogical, sociopolitical, and bureaucratic reasons that influenced their development and institutionalization in English and Scottish universities. This analysis also argues for a business and economics approach to the optical lantern as an academic innovation, seeking to identify the various factors that created a conducive environment for accommodating projected images as teaching tools in this educational initiative.

  3. Animalizing the political other: Qian Binghe’s caricatures of Yuan Shikai and revolutionary propaganda in early Republican China
    Muyang Zhuang · pp. 47-65 · online 2026-08-18
    摘要

    This article examines Chinese political caricature in the early 1910s, focusing on Qian Binghe (1879–1944) and his caricatured depictions of Yuan Shikai (1859–1916), the first president of the Republic of China. A staunch revolutionary aligned with Sun Yat-sen, Qian opposed both the Manchu dynasty and Yuan’s authoritarian rule. Despite his contributions, his political caricatures remain underexamined. This article investigates how Qian Binghe’s revolutionary engagements intersected with his deployment of animalized imagery to visually construct political otherness in early Republican China. Central to this analysis is Qian’s use of animal imagery, particularly the homophonic association between ‘ape’ (yuan) and Yuan Shikai’s surname, in his collections A Pictorial History of the Yuan Shikai Government (1913) and A Hundred Performances of Old Ape (1913). I argue that Qian’s caricatures, by animalizing Yuan Shikai and circulating this representation through revolutionary propaganda networks, constructed the president as a political other. His work reflects broader political struggles between pro-Sun revolutionaries and Yuan’s government, as well as tensions among various political and ethnic factions in early Republican China. By bestializing Yuan Shikai, Qian’s drawings offer a dissident perspective on Republican politics, particularly opposition to Yuan’s imperial ambitions. As one of the earliest political cartoon collections of the Republican era, Qian’s works provide a critical lens for understanding the entanglements between visual culture, print media, and politics in China’s transitional years.

    Xiaomeng Jin, Sandro Jung · pp. 66-82 · online 2026-07-07
    摘要

    Offering the first text- and media-analytical study of the set of Robinson Crusoe ABC plates the Staffordshire Brownhills Pottery produced in the late Victorian period, this article will trace the transmedial use of transfer-printed illustrations for the literacy-formational purpose of these ceramic objects.   5. The art and science of the nineteenth-century lantern show: towards an intersectional media history of images
    Sonya Petersson · pp. 83-102 · online 2026-08-18
    摘要

    The late nineteenth-century lantern shows were public platforms for intermixing the arts and sciences. Their display of still images spanned various spheres of knowledge and culture, from art history to astronomy to the actualities of the day, and the events could take the form of stage art performances, media spectacles or science lectures. The aim of this article is to examine how the nineteenth-century lantern show intersected the arts and sciences and what this could imply for what I propose to call an intersectional media history of images. To that end, the study sets out to bridge the two research traditions of image studies and media history. In three example cases the article shows: 1) how lantern images of world exhibitions, which played on a double representation of extensive sites and sight as the sense of extension, were part of a set of conceptual interlinks between the media, arts and sciences of vision; 2) how a series of astronomy lectures embedded scientific photographs of celestial bodies in a staging of the sublime; 3) how lectures on a South Polar expedition intertwined art-historical image conventions with a photographic slide series documenting, among other things, cartographic findings. These examples also align Swedish materials with the recent discussions of international lantern practices. Finally, the article considers how the case of the lantern shows can inform a broader intersectional media history of images.

  8. Cosmoramas, immersive viewing and the evolution of peep practice
    John Plunkett · pp. 1-26 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    The cosmorama was one of the most common forms of visual show between 1820 and 1860. Exhibited widely across Europe and the Americas, it has nonetheless been overshadowed by scholarship on the more large-scale formats of the panorama, diorama and magic lantern. This is partly because it is often conflated with the peepshow due to them sharing very similar features: both formats engrossed and entranced by having audiences view painted scenes through large convex lenses. Yet the cosmorama (which itself had a number of cognate names) is important as a distinct genus, part of a family of immersive lens-based viewing formats. The cosmorama demonstrates how ‘peep practice’ diversified in response in response to new audience constituencies and display opportunities in the first half of the nineteenth century. It was a vital agent in taking pictures to the people, engaging bourgeois viewers in cities across Europe. Through a detailed study of cosmorama exhibition in Britain, this article demonstrates that the cosmorama was a key part of the proliferation of popular shows beyond established and formal venues. It was in the vanguard of pop-up shows that could open in all types of city centre locations, moving between these and established fairground circuits. Finally, the cosmorama illuminates the hitherto overlooked development of transnational networks of exhibition, with a number of examples of European showpeople visiting Britain.

  9. ‘Smile or weep even in the social circle’: secrecy, sharing, and the circulation of affect with Stanhope microphotographic viewers
    Meegan Kennedy · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-06-25
    摘要

    ‘Stanhope’ viewers are optical bijoux most popular in the 1860s, when they were first mass-produced in France, although they were widely available in Britain. These small novelty items contained a microscopic photograph (microphotograph) that could only be viewed or indeed seen through a tiny cylindrical magnifying lens, which was often hidden in the design of the object. Stanhopes were developed in the context of a rich media ecology. They draw on the work of John Benjamin Dancer and René Dagron in microphotography. They join kaleidoscopes, stereoscopes, and peep eggs as domestic peep media, and they stand with cosmoramas and microscopes themselves as peep practices dependent on magnification for effect. Although Dagron marketed them with hints of luxury and mystery, they were inexpensive mementoes available to purchasers for only a shilling. Stanhopes were often sold as souvenirs, fitted with microphotographs of notable places, but they could also be customized with personal images, such as photographs of friends or family members. This essay argues that Stanhopes were a uniquely affordable, adaptable, accessible, and portable way to reanimate and circulate memory and affect. They promised both secrecy and sociality. Stanhopes offer a unique perspective on Victorian tourism and souvenirs, fashion and jewelry, religious sentiment and death culture, and even erotica. With a Stanhope in hand, Victorians could readily access a hidden photographic image and stir up or share recollected love, friendship, faith, nostalgia, or desire.

  10. Multilayered enactments of A Christmas Carol in magic lantern shows
    Li Liu · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    This article examines the multilayered enactments of the visual, auditory, and dramatic features of Charles Dickens’s A Christmas Carol (1843) through the medium of the magic lantern show in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. It maps the complex processes by which the familiar story is translated into optical spectacles and reveals how they generate new interpretations. To illustrate these processes, the study analyses seven lantern versions of the story’s book illustrations, especially John Leech’s ‘Mr. Fezziwig’s Ball’. It shows how the removal of characters, shifts in perspective, and modifications to accompanying readings re-perform and transform the tale, imbuing it with authenticity and immediacy. Building on these pictorial transformations, life model slides (enabled by advances in photography) emulate the intra-iconic elements of illustrations, merge them with the extra-iconic embodied performances of models, and thereby layer these re-enactments. Artists, stage managers, photographers, and colouring assistants each contribute their own interpretations through interlocking practices, which blur the boundaries between extratextual and intratextual realms and engage audiences in immersive narrative experiences. To deliver a multimedia, multisensory experience, magic lantern performances of the fictional story were often clustered with other disparate attractions under unifying themes such as An Evening with Dickens. These optical presentations of Dickens’s biography and real-world locations, together with other pictorial, musical, and theatrical performances, reinscribe the medium’s polyvocal nature. By examining these performances, this article charts the iconic, photographic, and polyvocal enactments to demonstrate the evolution of the Christmas tale in Victorian and Edwardian lantern culture.

  11. Spellbound: encounters between the spectatorial regimes of spiritualism, hypnosis, and cinema in nineteenth-century Barcelona
    Judit Vidiella-Pagès, Aurelio Castro-Varela · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    This article explores the continuity, kinship, and process of substitution between supernatural spectacles and cinema in Barcelona from the second half of the nineteenth century onward. More specifically, it examines the turning-tables fad and the reception of hypnotists’ shows in the city in order to understand how cinema later appealed, often in the same venues, to bodies and gazes equally eager for marvellous experiences. The filmic medium is thus approached as a late agent of the modern supernatural, although this spectatorial connection would be blurred by early popular films depicting practices of spiritism and hypnosis in a parodic or pejorative manner. Nevertheless, the entanglement between cinematic wonder and the legacies of mesmerism rested on a shared field of knowledge and practices, including parallels between scenic apparatuses and a mode of reception apparently based on contagion among spellbound audiences.

  12. Itinerant cosmorama practices and exhibition networks in Northwestern Europe: Carl Georg Enslen’s travelling ‘room journeys’ (1810s–1860s)
    Bart G. Moens · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-05-14
    摘要

    This article explores the development and circulation of itinerant cosmoramas in Northwestern Europe between the 1810s and 1860s, relating them to the vibrant fairground culture of the period. Fairs are recognized as venues for showcasing visual and optical media, including cosmorama displays. Drawing parallels with travelling entertainment like ballad singing and peep box exhibitions, this study posits that cosmoramas quickly emerged as ambulant variants of stationary ‘room journeys’, following established fairground circuits across Northwestern Europe. Re-examining the itinerant nature of these largely forgotten attractions, which appeared under a wide variety of names, this study elaborates on the cosmorama attraction of Carl Georg Enslen (1792–1866), a renowned artist in his time who toured extensively across Europe between 1816 and 1866. The analysis of his circulation and display strategies highlights that while fairs were major public exhibition venues, they represent only part of the broader exhibition networks and practices of itinerant cosmorama attractions.

  13. Cosmoramas and the emergence of bourgeois spectatorship in nineteenth-century Spain
    Cèlia Cuenca · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-05-12
    摘要

    This paper examines the emergence of cosmoramas in Spain during the 1830s as a key element in the formation of new modes of spectatorship, developing in parallel to the consolidation of the liberal society under the reign of Isabel II (1833–1868). It traces the proliferation of such spectacles through an analysis of newspaper advertisements and archival materials related to cosmorama shows held in Madrid and Barcelona. Initially, itinerant performances dominated the scene. However, by the late 1830s, permanent venues operated by local figures gained prominence, such as the Galería Topográfica in Madrid and the optical shows organized by Felipe Maglia and Francesc Dalmau in Barcelona. Central to this analysis is a material case study of the cosmorama dispositif based on the uniquely surviving example preserved in the Museu del Cinema in Girona. This new form of entertainment ensured spatial separation and order through its very dispositif. Unlike earlier optical boxes, cosmoramas invited viewers to physically move from lens to lens, simulating the act of traveling through the images themselves. The aim of this study is to investigate how cosmoramas shaped the visual landscape, redefining agents, networks, and social spaces in both major cities.

  14. ‘Amputating the sick man’: metaphorical representations of Ottoman and Chinese weakness in Western political cartoons, 1876–1909
    Alice Wang · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-03-30
    摘要

    This article examines the parallel usage of the ‘Sick Man of Europe’ metaphor and its extension, the ‘Sick Man of Asia’, as monikers for the Ottoman Empire and China, respectively, in the Euro-American illustrated press between 1876 and 1909. Often referenced, this phenomenon is little analyzed. Political cartoons published in popular periodicals portrayed the Ottoman Empire and China as ‘sick men’, supplementing a prolific textual construction of parity between the Ottoman Empire and China. These directly compared the Ottoman Empire and China and utilized similar motifs of Orientalization and weakness to discuss individual states. While this metaphor illustrated the condescending attitude of Western observers towards each empire’s ‘decline’, its visual flexibility also facilitated the portrayal of ‘recovery’ following perceived political and military accomplishments. The intimate link between metaphors of sickness and metaphors for partitioning the Ottoman and Chinese states is established, and the thematic characteristics of, commonalities, and differences between British, American, and French cartoons portraying the metaphorical partitions of the Ottoman Empire and China are systematically examined in this light. Finally, it is shown that the designation of the Ottoman Empire and China as ‘sick men’ was internalized by Ottoman and Chinese constitutionalist reformers who themselves perpetuated discourses of Ottoman and Chinese weaknesses, respectively, in the domestic publication of reformist cartoons. These demonstrate an inchoate reciprocal admiration between Ottoman and Chinese reformists, the latter of which were particularly cognizant of the usage of the ‘sick man’ metaphor to describe both states.

  15. The cosmorama as republican experimentation in nineteenth-century Latin America
    Brendan Lanctot · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-03-16
    摘要

    This article examines the place of the cosmorama in public life in postrevolutionary Latin America. It argues that, as both a social practice and a recurring rhetorical figure, the cosmorama served to imagine and configure collective social actors during a time marked by what Hilda Sábato has called ‘republican experimentation’, when the new nations of the region struggled to identify and define the pueblo, or people, in order to secure legitimacy. Cosmoramas began appearing with regularity during the early 1830s, and the advertisements in local print media consistently promoted these spectacles with a cosmopolitan discourse that imagined a rapprochement between Latin America and the societies of the north Atlantic. Examining how letrados, or men of letters, such as the Chilean Mariano Egaña and the Venezuelan Simón Rodríguez incorporated references to the cosmorama in their writings indicates how they regarded its techniques of exhibition, namely the ordered, collective viewing of select landscapes, as potentially productive in the service of a republican pedagogy for (in)forming citizens. Last, an analysis of the frontispiece of the costumbrista anthology Los cubanos pintados por sí mismos, which depicts a crowd gathering around a tutilimundi, or portable peepshow box, reinforces the linkages between spectacle and literature, reading and seeing. In synthesis, the cosmorama helped shape the experience of political modernity in Latin America.

  16. Imperial visions on tour: cosmoramas, neoramas, and Eurocentrism in Andorfer’s Grand Optical Gallery
    Victor Flores · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-02-04
    摘要

    This article explores the international journey of the Grand Optical Gallery (1828–1854), operated by Austrian showman Thomas Karl Andorfer, with a particular focus on the unique combination of optical attractions it offered, including cosmoramas, neoramas, dioramas, and a new solar microscope. It investigates the Eurocentric perspective embedded in Andorfer’s exhibitions and the cultural impact of these shows, especially in Brazil, his first overseas destination. Drawing on historical press records, the article traces Andorfer’s itinerary from the early fairs in Italy, where he showcased his Grand Cosmorama, through the Iberian Peninsula and Brazil, culminating on Broadway, where the Grand Optical Gallery was met with acclaim. In Brazil, Eurocentrism became especially pronounced as local audiences began to demand specific iconographic adjustments to the exhibitions. This article argues that, much like panoramas, cosmoramas were entangled with imperialist discourses from their inception, fostering an imperially oriented worldview among spectators. This mindset is starkly illustrated in Brazilian newspapers, where advertisements for cosmorama shows appeared alongside slave sale notices, revealing how such spectacles were embedded in broader colonial economies. By situating cosmoramas within this context, the article analyses how they functioned as cultural commodities that reinforced colonial identities and shaped their viewers’ cultural imagination. Finally, it considers the extent to which the cosmorama, as a global phenomenon, was influenced by and responsive to local cultural contexts.
