Vol. 29, No. 5 · 2026-10 · 8 篇 · SAGE · Media/Culture
  1. Autosociobiography and social mobility: An introduction to the special issue
    Mike Savage, Sam Friedman, Carlos Spoerhase · pp. 837-843 · online 2026-07-24
    摘要

    This introduction explains why the autosociobiography genre offers the potential for an innovative and important way to link sociological research on social mobility with literary scholarship. It introduces and contextualises the papers included in this special issue.

  2. Beyond the ‘Scholarship Boy’ paradigm: Autosociobiography and social mobility
    Sam Friedman, Mike Savage, Carlos Spoerhase · pp. 844-861 · online 2025-12-24
    摘要

    This article reflects on the potential of the rapidly emerging literary genre of autosociobiography to extend analyses of the personal experience and feelings associated with social mobility. We reflect on the congruence of its themes regarding the dislocating and isolating effects of upward social mobility with important recent research in qualitative sociology, which have exposed the weakness of quantitative social scientific studies for understanding the ‘social mobility malaise’. We argue that the theme of dislocation, sometimes encapsulated in the phrase that the upwardly mobile are ‘fish out of water’, can be rooted back to a paradigm of the ‘scholarship boy’, originating in the 1950s. We discuss how autosociobiography is a vital genre because it radicalises this framing and provides more critical and provocative perspectives which are more attuned to the period of intensifying economic inequalities in the early 21st century. Drawing on Jaquet’s arguments regarding ‘transclasses’, and reflecting on numerous autosociobiographical accounts, and recent sociological contributions, our article demonstrates the interdisciplinary reach of this genre.

  3. ‘I’d like to thank the academy’ – Social mobility, education and literary form
    Philipp Lammers · pp. 862-878 · online 2026-03-20
    摘要

    This article analyses the entanglements of sociological research on social mobility and autosociobiographies (class-transition narratives) in France. This emerging and very successful genre has, since Didier Eribon’s Returning to Reims , established itself as a literary form. Drawing on recent autosociobiographies from sociologists, I discuss the critical contribution made by those that came in the wake of Eribon to the ongoing debate on upward mobility via education and ‘widening participation’ campaigns within elite French universities. The article demonstrates that central elements of the debate, such as the figure of the ‘scholarship boy/girl’ and the costs of such efforts – most notably, psychosocial uprooting – are shaped both by sociology and literature. While literature’s renewed interest in class and social inequality demonstrates the critical potential and impact of the genre, my study also interrogates the consequences and risks of the boom in autosociobiography: namely the showcasing of individual success-through-education and the danger of the genre being perceived as an academic one, since it is mainly academics (sociologists inclusive) who feel such a narrative ‘transclass’ desire.

  4. Before the educated and individualised transfuge de classe : Autobiographical narratives of social advancement in the 19th and early 20th centuries
    Alexander Mayer · pp. 879-898 · online 2025-12-26
    摘要

    Research and public debate on social mobility is often based on a specific conception of social mobility. We typically think of long-distance intergenerational mobility of individuals, paradigmatically between the working class and the educated middle class. Autosociobiographies, although critical of optimistic assessments of social mobility, are based on the same paradigm. This article explores the historical diversity of understandings of social mobility, aiming to historicise our current concept. It presents a small number of typical examples drawn from a sample of 530 German-language autobiographies and life-story interviews with men and women born into the working or lower middle classes between 1830 and 1950. I analyse which concepts of social mobility these people use to narrate their social trajectories. I also ask whether they attribute social mobility more to individual effort and merit or to external factors, and how this shapes the personal identities crafted in these autobiographical accounts. This article argues that our current understanding is the result of a long-term shift: There is a trend towards individualised conceptions of social mobility. Abstract notions of achievement have replaced older morally charged concepts when it comes to explanations of social mobility. Education has only gradually come to the centre of popular ideas about social advancement.

  5. Zombie states, adrenalised academics and spectral shadows: A transclass perspective on social mobility
    Diane Reay · pp. 899-916 · online 2025-12-18
    摘要

    The over-riding theme of autobiographical social mobility narratives is the celebration of individual success. However, the analysis in Chantel Jacquet’s book Transclasses develops a very different understanding. In this article, I draw on Jacquet’s interpretation, and Bourdieu’s concept of habitus, to weave into a common thread social mobility strands from writing from across the 20th and 21st centuries. First, I look at Education and the Working Class , written in the 1960s, followed by Pierre Bourdieu’s Sketch for a Self-Analysis . I interlace both texts with contemporary writing by Cynthia Cruz and Fran Lock, poets and essayists from US and UK working-class backgrounds, and my own autobiographical writing, in order to develop a perspective that recognises the salience of gender for social mobility journeys. Throughout, I draw on the concept of habitus developed in my own work, and that of Skeggs, to make links between individuals’ inner emotional worlds, their affective dispositions, and external social and structural processes.

  6. Social mobility is a joke: Working-class women and British TV comedy on ‘the social floor’
    Jo Littler, Beverley Skeggs · pp. 917-934 · online 2026-02-19
    摘要

    This article examines a range of recent autosociobiographical representations of and by working-class women in the contemporary UK, focusing on Rain Dogs , Chewing Gum and Alma’s Not Normal . It argues that what unites these different representations – all of which are TV comedy-dramas, some with connecting iterations as memoir and play – is a vigorous critique and rejection of the neoliberal meritocratic dream. These representations show that the idea of a level playing field in which working hard to activate talent results in success is simply not a possibility for most working-class women: upwards social mobility is a joke. Yet crucially, this situation is not simply portrayed as a thwarted tragedy of the downtrodden, or as poverty porn in the tradition of reality TV. Instead, through life-affirming exuberant comedy, they show how the wider socio-political landscape is unjust while energetically refusing to accept its limits or internalise its stigma: they ‘reject respectability’. Unlike the majority of autosociobiographies, these representations primarily use a comedic tone. Their focus is not on ‘escape’, ‘transcendence’ or the aspiration for a middle-class life, but on the complexities of working-class lives as lived in context, and on critiquing institutional structures. They do value collective community support and crave the security of putting a ‘social floor’ on their circumstances, of not having to constantly worry about losing everything. Considering why the televisual is a useful vehicle for these narratives, the article asks: what do these women’s exuberant rejections of neoliberal meritocracy and bourgeois standards of judgement indicate about the wider cultural, social and political context, or current conjuncture?

  7. ‘Words were needed . . .’ Poetics and politics of the voiceover in contemporary autosociobiographical filmmaking
    Elena Meilicke · pp. 935-954 · online 2026-01-31
    摘要

    The article examines autosociobiographical film practices within contemporary European experimental and essay film, and specifically focusses on their use of voiceover narration. Comparing and contrasting three recent examples of autosociobiographical filmmaking from France, Austria and Germany, the article explores both the poetics and the politics of autosociobiographical voiceover, and asks how they relate to some of the fundamental ethico-political questions and problems associated with the genre of autosociobiography.

  8. Talking back: Social mobility, class and reflexivity in Darren McGarvey’s ‘Poverty Safari’
    Flora Petrik, Markus Rieger-Ladich · pp. 955-973 · online 2026-07-09
    摘要

    This article examines Darren McGarvey’s Poverty Safari as a meaningful yet underexplored contribution to the emerging field of autosociobiographies—a genre where authors intertwine personal narratives with critical reflections on the social and cultural structures that shape their lives. We argue that McGarvey disrupts dominant, idealised accounts of social mobility by interrogating the classed dynamics of the book market, offering a distinctive form of reflexivity within the genre. Through an analysis of his narrative strategies, we explore two key questions: How does Poverty Safari narrate social mobility in ways that foreground the conditions, constraints, and expectations shaping autosociobiographical storytelling? How does McGarvey’s mode of reflexivity (re-)configure the analytical potential of autosociobiography for studying social class and mobility? We demonstrate that McGarvey critiques the expectations placed on authors of autosociobiographies, revealing the power structures that both allow and limit their success. His self-reflexive approach acts as a form of ‘talking back’—an act of reclaiming the right to critically narrate one’s struggles. By situating McGarvey’s work within current debates on mobility narratives and class representation, this study highlights the genre’s potential to reframe discussions of class and education.

  1. Can organic vegetables convert an urban and conservative Catholic movement to political ecology?
    Josselin Tricou · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    This article explores whether concrete practices—such as participation in an Association for the Maintenance of Peasant Agriculture (AMAP)—can reshape the imaginaries and lifestyles of conservative Catholics toward a more systemic and political ecology. It focuses on the Altercathos , a group of young Lyonnais Catholics, through the experience of their community café, Le Simone . The question is especially relevant given that, while Anglophone studies often highlight a negative correlation between conservative Christianity and ecological concern, the French Catholic context is more nuanced. Since the 1970s, French Catholicism has nurtured a tradition of ecological commitment. However, the Vatican has often used the “ecologization of religion” to reinforce conservative positions on gender and sexuality, particularly through the concept of “human ecology.” The Altercathos , initially active in protests against same-sex marriage, come from a conservative background but underwent a “militant disillusionment” that gradually distanced them from the Church’s reactionary stance. They embraced the notion of integral ecology from Pope Francis’s Laudato si’ , seeking to reconnect faith with civic and ecological engagement in urban life. Yet, the analysis of Le Simone ’s AMAP reveals a more complex reality. Despite aspirations toward “ecological conversion,” the initiative did not lead to a strong collective transformation or spiritualization. Participants’ motivations were diverse—ranging from practicality to social ties—reflecting a dynamic of individual engagement rather than collective commitment. This case illustrates the gap between papal discourse on ecological conversion and lived Catholic practice. Rather than a unified movement, Le Simone reflects slow, fragmented transformations and a pluralism of motivations, calling for a more nuanced understanding of faith-based ecological engagement.

  2. Political representation of displacement and escape in the neoliberal city: Körfez and Arada in Turkish independent cinema
    Alper Kasımoğlu, Murat Taşdan · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    Focusing on two independent Turkish films, Körfez (2017) and Arada (2018), this article examines how displacement and escape are politically represented in the neoliberal city. It argues that neoliberal urban transformation in Turkey generates not only physical displacement but also alienation and a drive to escape. Using qualitative narrative analysis and close readings, the study shows how both films stage the erosion of belonging in urban life. Körfez portrays a suffocating, stagnant city through an introspective protagonist; Arada tracks the chaotic nocturnal journey of a young man planning to leave the country. Despite their aesthetic differences, the two films politicise displacement and urban alienation through two distinct forms of escape: Körfez figures escape as inward withdrawal, while Arada stages it as kinetic boundary-crossing. This distinction shows how these independent films make the human costs of neoliberal urban transformation legible through contrasting forms of belonging and non-belonging.

  3. ‘Alexa, dinner time’: Exploring how connected devices shape domestic atmospheres, intimacy, and power
    Helene Fiane Teigen, Henry Mainsah · online 2026-10-17
    摘要

    This paper explores how people use smart home technologies in home-making practices. Employing the concept of atmospheres, we examine how smart home technologies contribute to producing the feeling of home and give texture to everyday activities. The concept of atmosphere denotes shared feelings and moods in a particular space or environment that are produced and experienced among different configurations of objects, technologies, and (human and non-human) bodies. The article is based on in-depth interviews with 12 people living with smart home technologies in Norway. The analysis shows different ways people use the technology to make the home feel right through setting different moods and scenes, engaging in playful improvisations and renegotiating the felt boundaries of the home.   4. Memory as decoloniality
    Meghan Tinsley · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    European colonialism is an epistemic project in which collective memory plays a crucial role. Memory creates linear histories and consolidates cohesive identities while erasing difficult or marginalised histories and projecting contemporary Others across history. Memory, however, is also never singular: the dominant narrative of the past constantly interacts with, and is contested by, grassroots counter-narratives. As such, counter-narratives of national memory in postcolonial nations may enact memory as decoloniality. Drawing from decolonial and postcolonial theory, queer theory, cultural studies and memory studies, I argue that memory as decoloniality plays out in three ways. First, it entails delinking from official narratives of the past as false or incomplete. Second, it involves unsanctioned acts of mourning and melancholic reiterations of the unsettled past. Third, it writes new narratives and invents new traditions, replacing monumental history with a pluriverse of fragmented memories. This paper engages with a transnational range of cases of memory as decoloniality, reflecting critically on their promises and limitations. I conclude by reflecting on the implications of memory for larger political and cultural processes of decoloniality.

  5. Moving images, moved audiences? A focus group study into audience interpretations of distant suffering in The Impossible (2012)
    Valerie Vanhove, Stijn Joye, David Ongenaert · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    While distant suffering has been thoroughly explored in factual media such as news and documentaries, this article examines Western audiences’ interpretations of distant suffering and non-Western ‘Others’ in the format of fiction films. Through five focus groups ( N = 22) in Flanders, the northern Dutch-speaking part of Belgium, this explorative study examines whether the film The Impossible (2012) fosters empathy, raises awareness and encourages action towards the portrayed distant suffering. The film tells the story of a British family during the tsunami of 2004 and is largely based on true events. Findings show that the film proves effective in increasing Western viewers’ awareness of distant suffering. However, this sense of closeness mostly resulted in passivity and rarely translated into concrete action. A proper distance from the portrayed Other appears crucial for Western viewers to be motivated to show compassion and consider action. Nevertheless, the study also suggests that audiences’ emotional connection is heavily influenced by individual film consumption preferences.

  6. Scrolling for sanity, performing for the algorithm: Theratainment and the governance of care on TikTok
    Krysten Stein · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    Drawing on 42 semi-structured interviews with US-based clinicians, this study examines how licensed mental health professionals navigate TikTok’s governing infrastructures while attempting to educate and care for audiences within an algorithmically curated attention economy. It identifies three interlocking pressures: (a) engagement-optimisation systems that reward affectively charged, trend-aligned posts, making clinically nuanced communication difficult to sustain; (b) the emergence of ambient caregiving, an always-on, low-intensity form of audience support that generates emotional labour without institutional scaffolding; and (c) opaque, automated moderation practices that disproportionately suppress content addressing trauma, structural inequality and marginalised identities. Conceptually, the article develops theratainment to describe the fusion of therapeutic language with entertainment aesthetics and entrepreneurial self-branding that clinicians must negotiate under platform logics and elaborates ambient caregiving as a domain-specific extension of affective labour in short-form video. The findings reframe clinicians not only as educators but also as subjects of platform governance who continually balance visibility, credibility and ethical responsibility within engagement-first infrastructures. The article concludes with policy and design implications, including verification, trauma-informed and anti-bias moderation practices, and potential public-platform partnerships to support evidence-based care. This article forms part of the European Journal of Cultural Studies Special Issue on Digital Mental Health.

  7. Cute, goofy, and wholesome: Inventing social media otherwise in virtual reality
    Ilker Bahar · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    Recently, the hype around the Metaverse has faded, with artificial intelligence taking the spotlight.   8. Haunting data: Mental health, data-hacking and digital temporalities
    Marjo Kolehmainen · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    This article maps the experiences of victims of the Vastaamo psychotherapy centre data hack by analysing their stories through a lens provided by the digital hauntology framework. The article provides insights into the ways in which digital temporalities entangle with the affective experiences of mental (ill-)health. It argues that the digital age provides entangled and networked sites for recurrent wounding, thus ultimately generating a form of affective inequality. Many victims register the Vastaamo data breach and its aftermath as a loss of the ontological and epistemological certainty and security that previously provided an experience of being psychically held.

  10. When the guess becomes a default: Imagined audiences as production constraints
    Chul Heo · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    Taking up a question opened by Siao Yuong Fong’s recent Cultural Commons essay, ‘Imagining audiences in the age of generative AI’, this essay asks how audience imaginaries acquire operational force inside production and take aesthetic form. Fong’s palimpsest foregrounds how heterogeneous audience assumptions accumulate; the default names which of them returns with the least resistance and which becomes costly to refuse. Drawing on an AI-enhanced virtual-production archive in South Korea, and on a history running from radio’s absent listener to the mechanical storage of audience response in 1950s television, the essay argues that generative AI does not make actual audiences more knowable. It makes the audience position sedimented in the apparatus partially reconstructible – through recurrent outputs and the labour required to undo them. Model defaults meet budgets, workflows and cultural expertise, leaving formal traces in framing, movement, atmosphere and signage. Following those traces shows who pays when cultural specificity does not arrive by default.

  11. Between concrete and current: Econostalgia and the reimagining of urban-nature relations along Tbilisi’s Mtkvari river
    Mariam Urdia · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    This article examines how the Mtkvari river in Tbilisi is remembered, narrated and politically reimagined after decades of infrastructural enclosure. Based on multi-method anthropological research, including participant observation, interviews, digital materials and archival sources, it traces the river’s transformation from a sacred, social and ecological presence into an increasingly marginalized urban watercourse shaped by embankments, highways, pollution and concrete. The article conceptualises these damaged relations through ecological nostalgia, understood not as passive longing for a lost past but as a situated civic repertoire through which memories of riverine life become claims for repair, access and responsibility. The analysis focuses especially on Dighomi Meadows, one of Tbilisi’s last semi-wild riverside ecosystems, where activists mobilise memories and imaginaries of floodplain forest, riverine sacredness and more-than-human life against extractive development and new cycles of concrete urbanisation. By linking ecological nostalgia to riverine citizenship, the article shows how care for a damaged river can expand urban belonging beyond property, residence or administrative citizenship towards responsibility for shared human and more-than-human worlds. Finally, it situates the Mtkvari/Kura as a transboundary river, inviting comparative attention to river memory, attachment and ecological vulnerability across the wider Kura-Aras basin.

  12. Artificial intelligence as a ‘dictionary of accepted ideas’
    David Morley · online 2026-10-09
    摘要

    Contemporary large language models function as word-pattern-recognition systems crunching Big Data to identify the most commonly associated interdiscursive patterns in contemporary language and thought. Similarly, in the late 19th century, Gustave Flaubert, using rather simpler, artisanal techniques, produced a set of condensations of the linguistic patterns and received wisdoms of his own age in his ‘Dictionary of Accepted Ideas’. This article examines some of the analogies between artificial intelligence’s methodologies and Flaubert’s literary approach. By linking these issues to Stuart Hall’s arguments about the constitutive role of common sense in providing the horizons of thought, we can then clarify artificial intelligence’s (inevitably ideological) role in policing those horizons in our own time.

  1. From margins to mainstream: a critical network analysis of the #MosqueMeToo hashtag
    Shoaa Almalki · pp. 1987-2005 · online 2025-08-17
    摘要

    This study critically examines the discourse around sexual assault in religious places within the Muslim community, as facilitated by the #MosqueMeToo hashtag on Twitter. Initiated as part of the broader #MeToo movement, #MosqueMeToo offers Muslim women a platform to share their experiences and raise awareness of the problem. The researcher used critical methods combining computational analysis and qualitative analysis to examine 6,790 tweets shared on the hashtag at its peak. The findings highlight the narratives of the compelling need to expose sexual assault in religious places, debunking modesty myth, and the dynamics of suppression and voice. This study challenges the idea that social media has a transformative potential in amplifying marginalized voices. The findings suggest that certain topics, taboos, and cultures might find it more challenging than others to address deep-rooted issues within their communities. This research contributes to the broader conversation around the #MeToo movement and its impact on raising awareness and empowering women to seek justice.

  2. From secularism to freedom: The hijab debate in the 2024 Olympics and its reflections in Türkiye
    Melis Karakuş · pp. 2006-2023 · online 2025-08-10
    摘要

    In recent years, the issue of Muslim women wearing hijabs has become a controversial topic in Western societies, particularly within the realm of sports. This study examines the reactions to France’s ban on Muslim athletes wearing hijabs during the 2024 Paris Olympic Games, with a focus on Türkiye’s discussions on the X platform (formerly Twitter) between July 26 and August 11 2024. The study analyzes the themes and trends in public discourse, exploring how the ban was perceived in terms of gender, religious freedom, and cultural differences.     Monica Crawford · pp. 2024-2040 · online 2025-10-09
    摘要

  4. Defining premium advocacy documentary TV: analyzing the coexistence of popular feminism and care-based feminist advocacy in Hulu’s Untouchable: The Rise and Fall of Harvey Weinstein (2019)
    Amber Hardiman · pp. 2041-2057 · online 2025-08-22
    摘要

    This article identifies an emergent television genre that has developed with the dissolution of traditional genre distinctions at a time of amplified cultural, industrial, and technological media convergence. I call this genre premium advocacy documentary TV. Through a close analysis of Hulu’s Untouchable: The Rise and Fall of Harvey Weinstein (2019), I explore how the streaming industry’s acceleration of media convergence across digital platforms permits competing and coexisting discursive attitudes toward feminist advocacy. I argue that changes in the configuration of genre have enabled the production of post-#MeToo television documentaries that claim to uphold care-based feminist ethics and advocacy practices, even as they are mutually underpinned by the values of popular and neoliberal feminism.   5. Rhetorical strategies in the speeches of Mrs America
    Margarita-Esther Sánchez-Cuervo, Luisa Sánchez-Rivas · pp. 2058-2074 · online 2025-07-03
    摘要

    Mrs America, a 2020 TV miniseries created by Dahvi Waller, examined the history of the Equal Rights Amendment (ERA) in the USA, highlighting the struggles between feminist and anti-feminist groups that affected the amendment’s failure to gain ratification in the 1970s. The protagonist, portrayed by Cate Blanchett, was Phyllis Schlafly, a conservative political activist who worked tirelessly to prevent the ratification of the amendment by means of an active discreditation campaign. Historically, Schlafly utilised dissociation of certain aspects of the amendment text to favour her anti-ERA interests and employed a variety of rhetorical strategies to persuade her listeners of the correctness of her ideas. This article analyses how Schlafly’s rhetorical strategies, particularly her use of dissociation, are dramatized to shape the portrayal within the miniseries, with a focus on three key rhetorical figures. These figures, choice, presence, and communion are presented as devices used to enhance dramatic tension while nevertheless remaining rooted in Schlafly’s real-life writing and public statements. While the series dramatized many aspects of Schlafly’s personal life, it can thus be seen to provide a relatively nuanced representation of her role in shaping conservative activism, offering an invitation to viewers to critically assess the cultural tensions of the period.

  6. Have a cup of coffee: the techno-spatial tactics of female urbanites and the production of “mobile publicness” in digital urban China
    Jian Xu, Wei Wang · pp. 2075-2094 · online 2025-09-13
    摘要

    Female urbanites play a particularly visible role in the burgeoning coffee culture of megacities like Shanghai. The remarkable visibility of female coffee house consumers contrasts with the dominance of the male bourgeoisie in Jürgen Habermas’ public sphere, making it a phenomenon worth investigation. Meanwhile, female participation in coffee houses is inseparable from digital culture. However, existing research has not connected women’s digital technology use to urban space production. To bridge this gap, this paper adopts an approach derived from “everyday life” to conceptualize how women use social media to produce urban space in Shanghai. Building upon Michel de Certeau’s conceptual framework, we propose to understand female urbanites’ techno-spatial tactics as an extension of his concept of tactics. In the case of Shanghai’s coffee houses, we argue that the techno-spatial tactics of female urbanites include hybrid appropriation, participatory storytelling, shared coding, and place collage. With these tactics, female urbanites have reconstructed the “publicness” of unconventional public spaces, which we term “mobile publicness.” The mobile publicness of coffee houses can translate into a rejuvenated public life in twenty-first-century Shanghai, shaped and embraced by women’s assertive digital power.

  7. Vertical representation of gender in the Ghanaian broadcast media
    Samuel Danso, Gifty Appiah-Adjei, Theodora Dame Adjin-Tettey · pp. 2095-2113 · online 2025-07-16
    摘要

    The gender gap in media organizations has drawn research attention in recent years. This study, through the lens of the glass ceiling concept, draws on interview data to explore how gender is represented in managerial positions within Ghanaian broadcast media. The study’s findings indicate that, despite progress towards gender equality, men continue to dominate top managerial positions in Ghana’s broadcast media. The absence of institutional policies specifically targeting gender representation perpetuates these inequalities. Further, informal efforts by some media firms to ensure fair representation lack the effectiveness of formal guidelines and accountability mechanisms. Thus, women in the Ghanaian broadcast media experience the “glass ceiling” effect and are excluded from managerial decision-making processes. The study highlights the importance of promoting gender equity and diversity in leadership within the Ghanaian broadcast media.

  8. Postfeminist branding: masculinity and nationalist sentiment in Li Jiaqi’s beauty livestreaming
    Gao-Huan Chen · pp. 2114-2134 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

  9. Women don’t look up? Female roles in climate change cartoons
    Sara Moreno-Tarín, Tatiana Pina, Martí Domínguez · pp. 2135-2154 · online 2025-07-29
    摘要

    In this article, we analyze the representation of women in cartoons related to climate change published over the last 40 years. A sample of 3,770 cartoons, drawn by professional cartoonists, was obtained from the CartoonStock database. Our results show that only 25% (N = 973) of the cartoons studied include at least one female figure. Moreover, when women do appear, they are often depicted in secondary and stereotyped roles, with minimal representation in leadership positions, particularly in scientific and technical fields. Beyond their underrepresentation, the study identifies four biases in climate change communication through editorial cartoons: omission, trivialization, credibility, and androcentrism. These biases not only undermine the legitimacy of women’s contributions to climate discourse but also shape public perceptions of the environmental crisis, diminishing its urgency when framed through feminized narratives. These findings highlight the need to redefine the representation of women in editorial cartoons, especially in the climate movement, where their role is more active and significant than depicted by cartoonists.

  10. Gold diggers in the digital age: unraveling the “Lao Nü” stereotype in contemporary Chinese social media
    Weiqi Tian, Jingshen Ge, Haotian Guan · pp. 2155-2172 · online 2025-08-07
    摘要

    This study investigates the construction and perpetuation of the “lao nü” (gold digger) stereotype within Chinese digital culture, focusing on the “Fat Cat” incident that garnered significant social media attention. Utilizing Feminist Critical Discourse Analysis (FCDA) within the broader framework of Critical Discourse Analysis (CDA), the research critically examines how gender ideologies and power dynamics are articulated and reinforced in digital discourses. The analysis identifies the coexistence of feminist and anti-feminist discourses surrounding the “lao nü” stereotype. This duality underscores the complex, contested nature of gendered narratives in digital spaces and their implications for understanding gender dynamics in contemporary China.

  11. Can action heroines age? The return of Sarah Connor in Terminator: Dark Fate (2019)
    Marianne Kac-Vergne · pp. 2173-2187 · online 2025-07-28
    摘要

    This article examines the return of Sarah Connor in Terminator: Dark Fate as an aged action heroine played by the 63-year-old Linda Hamilton. Her return is a very rare example of an older action heroine coming back to be the main protagonist of a sci-fi/action franchise, in contrast with older male action heroes, whose return to the screens has led to a whole subgenre of “geri-action” films. Furthermore, contrary to Victoria (Helen Mirren) in the RED movies and Princess Leia (Carrie Fisher) in the latest Star Wars trilogy, Sarah Connor challenges conventional femininity and occupies a central place in the marketing and narrative of the film. She is a source of regeneration rather than degeneration, leading a team of younger women for whom she becomes a mentor. However, a comparison with the older Terminator played by Arnold Schwarzenegger reveals that ageing comes at a greater cost for women than for men. While the Terminator is fully integrated into society and continues to benefit from spectacular action scenes and witty dialogue, Sarah Connor appears as a much more tragic figure of ageing, marred by loss, loneliness and bitterness.

  12. The interface gaze: on the (sexual) objectification of domestic workers in the gig economy
    Laura Wiesböck, Julia Radlherr · pp. 2188-2205 · online 2025-08-08
    摘要

    In contemporary capitalist societies, domestic work is increasingly externalised and marketized, allowing profit-oriented gig-economy platforms to emerge as new actors in this field. Considering that such digital marketplaces shape the interactions and relationships between clients and workers, it is crucial to examine the experiences of household workers in relation to the norms, beliefs and ideas embedded in the functionalities and design of the platform. This is particularly relevant concerning the historically prevalent (sexual) objectification of domestic cleaners. Therefore, the present paper presents 15 problem-centred interviews with female domestic cleaners working in the gig-economy in Vienna. The results are complemented by applying the walkthrough method and discursive interface analysis on the website of Betreut.at, one of Austria’s largest platforms for domestic services. Our study shows that the interviewees’ experiences reflect the culturally embedded objectification of domestic workers. Recurrent themes identified include clients’ practices related to lookism and the prioritization of attractiveness, unwanted dating requests, and sexual harassment, all of which are, to some degree, shaped by the platform’s technological design. Additionally, the platform’s website interface lacks essential safety features, exacerbating the vulnerability of household cleaners, who are at high risk of sexual abuse.

  13. From collective struggle to individual hustle: gig work and neoliberal feminism in Working Mother magazine
    Jessica K. Crowell · pp. 2206-2221 · online 2026-03-04
    摘要

    This article traces coverage of gig work in the U.S. magazine Working Mother from the 1970s to the 2020s. The study uses critical discourse analysis to conduct a comparative historical examination of 289 articles, focusing on how coverage of gig work and feminist themes evolves. Whereas early issues of the magazine align closely with second-wave feminist principles, such as encouraging women to pursue collective solutions for social change and compete with men for jobs, later issues embrace neoliberal themes of self-reliance, hustle, and gendered entrepreneurialism.   14. The endless project of the feminine self: ‘locked-down femininity’ and becoming #ThatGirl on TikTok
    Stephanie Patouras, Megan Sharp · pp. 2222-2236 · online 2025-08-17
    摘要

    Encouraged by the solitude of COVID-19 lockdowns, the social media platform TikTok gained prominent cultural attention. These ideals are entrenched within both hegemonic and critical expressions on TikTok, as feminist resistance in the form of feedback occurs in tandem with the discourse it attempts to challenge.

    This article examines the polymediated performances and political humor of women comedians in Turkey’s emerging comedy landscape.   2. Trapped in the simulation: Tradwife culture, fake allyship, and the exhaustion of the domestic dystopia in Don’t Worry Darling (2022)
    Rodrigo Ernesto Fernández Schütz · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-10-24
    摘要

    This article examines Olivia Wilde’s Don’t Worry Darling (2022) as a critical impasse in popular feminist cinema’s deployment of the domestic dystopia. Rather than offering another comparative reading with Bryan Forbes’s The Stepford Wives (1975) or its 2004 remake (dir. Frank Oz), the article situates Wilde’s film within three interconnected contemporary contexts: the tradwife movement’s appropriation of Stepford aesthetics; post-#MeToo discourse around fake male allyship; and the exhaustion of the ‘fiftiesness’ that the nostalgia film recycles as a substitute for historical engagement with the postwar period. It further examines how the film’s troubled production and reception, including Florence Pugh’s public distancing and the casting of Harry Styles, symptomatically reproduce the contradictions the film ostensibly critiques.   3. “ This data is really sensitive ” – the making of non-commercial menstruapps as productive resistance within Femtech
    Aino Koskenniemi · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    Digital menstrual cycle tracking is a booming business and a burgeoning field of study. Although often framed as empowering, researchers have recognized serious problems in menstrual cycle tracking apps, or “menstruapps.” These include the lack of accuracy in predictions, the enforcement of harmful stereotypes, and above all, the ways in which sensitive user data is collected and used for profit-making. However, in recent years, a number of non-commercial apps have become available. In this study, nine makers of non-commercial menstruapps were interviewed.   4. Unleashing menstrual blood: a qualitative study of free-bleeding activism on Instagram
    Estefanía Reyes · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    Free-bleeding practices gained visibility on Instagram during the 2010s, when a series of posts became emblematic moments of digital menstrual activism. Yet little scholarship has examined how menstrual blood operates as a site of power and resistance in everyday digital contexts. Through a critical discourse and semiotic analysis of six high-profile Instagram posts published between March 2015 and September 2019, this study explores how menstrual blood is visually and discursively represented on the platform and how these representations challenge and reconfigure dominant discourses of menstruation among audiences.   5. Lover’s (Dis)comfort: considering the feminist potentials of romance in the digital game
    Amy Brierley-Beare · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    Romance in mainstream gaming has rapidly increased in visibility over the last decade. Recent titles like Baldur’s Gate 3, Starfield and Dragon Age: The Veilguard have included optional romance features as selling points in promotional campaigns. Despite this rise in popularity, discursively romance is still a confused and contested term amongst scholars, players, developers, and games journalists. It is used as a catch-all term to refer to a diverse range of social interactions and gameplay mechanics. Some scholars have argued that romance has been a gateway for transgressive, feminine, and pro-social play. As Anastasia Salter states, the decision to feature romance in play can be viewed as an act of “feminist game design”. However, without a discerning understanding of the different presentations of romance within gaming it is challenging to recognise where feminisms are happening and where they are distinctly not. Through an exploration of how romance mechanics navigate issues of consent, objectification, and “pick-up” ideologies, I argue romance in the digital game is not inherently feminist, though still maintains transgressive potentials. Recognising where feminism is potentially happening is especially important as “romance” is becoming increasingly visible as a feature of the AAA game. Without recognising the potentially harmful and troubling mechanics baked into prominent examples (some of which are considered templates for their development teams) there is a risk that damaging presentations will continue to be normalised and expected within these styles of romantic representation.

  6. Level up, ladies: Femininity as a total project and the rise of Queen feminism
    Heini Salminen, Juulia Heikkinen · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-09
    摘要

    Critiques of liberal feminisms are increasingly emerging from the women-led “femosphere,” calling for a revised understanding of feminism through a revaluation of femininity. This article examines one such voice: a group of social media influencers who cultivate and commercialize “queen” femininity as a lifestyle, advising women on how to “level up” their lives and attract provider-minded men. We follow 14 Finnish “queen” influencer–entrepreneurs and ask how they define and practice femininity. Drawing on digital fieldwork and content analysis of 303 Instagram captions, we show how femininity is constructed as a total and infinite project—one that extends across all fronts of life. The study further demonstrates that the cultivation of a “feminine lifestyle” is not merely a set of personal preferences but also implies a broader vision of social order. We term this ideological project “Queen feminism”: an essentialized, spiritualized, and platformed articulation of neoliberal feminism, in which the entrepreneurial ethos is (re)located in the total, perpetual, and crucially impossible project of the feminine self. Beyond analyzing a (counter)movement within feminism, this paper contributes to discussions on influencer politics by showing how the entanglement of ideological work and influencer economics may hamper collective feminist projects yet contribute to wider ideological shifts.

  7. Mobilising motherhood: feminist tactical media and platform struggles against environmental injustice in Central Java
    Dewi Candraningrum · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-09
    摘要

    This article examines how rural women in Nguter, Central Java, mobilise environmental resistance through WhatsApp in response to toxic emissions from a rayon factory, PT. Rayon Utama Makmur (PT. RUM). Drawing on feminist political ecology, ecofeminism, and feminist media studies, the study conceptualises digital activism as a situated gendered practice embedded in everyday life. Based on seven years of participatory ethnography (2017–2024), the analysis shows how WhatsApp operates not simply as a communication tool but as a feminist tactical medium through which care, environmental witnessing, and collective action are organised, sustaining a class-action lawsuit that in 2024 won a binding Supreme Court ruling. Mobilisation emerged through domestic and informal networks before extending into digital spaces, where video sharing, voice messaging, and real-time coordination formed counter-publics challenging corporate narratives and institutional inaction. Participation was shaped by intersecting inequalities, limited digital literacy, infrastructural constraints, and exposure to surveillance and gendered risk, so digital activism was both enabling and precarious, relying on affective and communicative labour disproportionately borne by women. The article advances “WhatsApp ecofeminism” to describe a low-tech, high-impact activism grounded in care, relationality, and tactical improvisation, arguing for a rethinking of digital inclusion beyond access toward relational participation and situated counter-power.

  1. A Space for Women: Gender, Class, and Women's Lounges in 1930s Chinese Movie Palaces
    Yumo Yan · pp. 1-30
    摘要

    This paper analyzes the design and spatial planning of women's lounges in three Chinese movie palaces in the 1930s: Shanghai's Nanking Theater, Nanjing's Xindu Theater, and Nanjing's Dahua Theater.   2. On Aesthetic Re-education: Newsreels in the Aftermath of World War II
    John Hoffmann · pp. 31-55
    摘要

    This essay argues that re-education films made in the wake of World War II reorient the historical and political stakes of education filmmaking, thereby contributing to broader film-historical debates about visual propaganda and the ideology of nonfic tion cinema. The essay concentrates on the newsreel series Welt im Film [World in Film], which became a key instrument for re-educating the German public after twelve years of nationalist indoctrination. As fixtures of the postwar screen in Allied zones of occupation, these films reveal how the theatrical distribution of nonfiction films was instrumentalized for an anti-nationalist and globalist agenda.

  3. The Sun Tribe films: Moral panic over the taiyōzoku during Japan's long and hot summer of 1956
    Mats Karlsson · pp. 56-78
    摘要

    During the summer of 1956, a series of films based on novels by Ishihara Shintarō sparked a nationwide sensation in Japan. Their portrayal of a decadent, hedonistic lifestyle sent shockwaves through society and provoked widespread concern. Critics quickly called for the films to be banned, fearing that the antisocial behavior of their affluent protagonists might corrupt unblemished youth across Japan. Drawing on con temporary Japanese sources, this article examines the ensuing moral panic, focusing on the films' reception and the public debate that followed. It further reveals how close Japan came to reinstating film censorship—despite lingering memories of the notorious wartime Film Law of 1939.

  4. A Cinema of Paper: Relocating the place of the written word in early British educational film
    Anushrut Ramakrishnan Agrwaal · pp. 79-104
    摘要

    From the film medium's earliest years, the written word has been integral in establishing cinema's educational role. Yet historians have largely studied the writings by film companies as "paratexts" to their films. Text was used to integrate panoramas/magic lanterns into knowledge-making, and film was no different. The prose in catalogues, pamphlets, and journalism produced by British filmmakers, served to highlight cinema's pedagogical utility. This article foregrounds the forgotten figure of Thomas Clegg, arguing that Clegg's innovative writings positioned the film producer, Charles Urban Trading Com pany, as an educator. Through Clegg's example, I reveal that, more than image, text was integral to selling the promise of the moving image's unique contribution to instruction, knowledge, and media history.

  5. What is an American ?: Tracking the Paths of Immigrants in Early Cinema
    Richard Abel · pp. 105-131
    摘要

    This essay investigates the portrayal of immigrants and immigration in short American films, both nonfiction and fiction, in the decade before the Great War. That portrayal is based on surviving film prints as well as synopses and articles in the trade press. Several questions guide this investigation. Where did immigrants tend to come from, particularly if their ethnicity, as Italians and Jews, could make them "alien" and not always accepted as white ? In the nonfiction films, how were immigrants helped or hindered by government agencies, businesses, and other organizations? In the fiction films, how often do their narrative arcs allow immigrants to confront and overcome all kinds of difficulties, ending in success? In either kind of film, how were immigrants prepared, or not prepared, to become new Americans and even poised to rise socially and economically, as my own ancestors slowly did? The following pages take up these questions in several stages, with emphasis on changes in American society that occurred in the early 1910s. How does this analysis reveal what it meant more than one hundred years ago, in comparison to the fraught experience of current immigrants, to be or become an American ?

Film Quarterly

Vol. 80, No. 1 · 2026-09-01 · 16 篇 · U of California Press · Film
  1. Editor’s Notebook
    J. M. Tyree · pp. 5-7
  2. “It Is in Our House Now”
    Justus Nieland · pp. 8-21
    摘要

    This essay describes the 2.3-acre Hollywood Hills estate of the late director David Lynch—known as “the Compound” to his collaborators and fans—as a unique film studio that played a crucial role in Lynch’s creative practice. Assembled over the course of the 1990s as Lynch purchased and remodeled a series of contiguous properties, the Compound was the decisive environment of Lynch’s multimedia experimentation over nearly three decades of work in Los Angeles. Nieland explores the Compound as a material space and an assemblage of technologies and personnel that shaped key feature films like Lost Highway (1997), Mulholland Drive (2001) and Inland Empire (2006). The Compound became increasingly visible in documentaries about Lynch’s “art life,” in later media experiments like his YouTube channel “David Lynch Theater,” and in its 2026 sale after Lynch’s death. In but not of Hollywood, the Compound served as both home base for the construction of Lynch’s auteur brand and the studio infrastructure of his late modernism.

  3. The Birth of the Electrical Boy
    Amy Herzog · pp. 22-25
    摘要

    This article maps the connections between David Lynch’s unproduced script, Ronnie Rocket, and his 2017 series, Twin Peaks: The Return (aka Twin Peaks season 3). Focusing on the “Dougie Jones” sequences set in Las Vegas, Nevada, this short article draws parallels between Lynch and Jacques Tati, particularly in their use of slapstick and modern built environments to create physical humor.

  4. Uncanny Intimacies, Abject Desires
    Eileen G’Sell · pp. 26-29
    摘要

    This article introduces the dossier “Mulholland Drive at 25.” What did Mulholland Drive (2001) say about female fantasy, suffering, and desire at the turn of the twenty-first century, and how might that matter today? Rather than debate Mulholland Drive’s degree of feminism or the extent to which David Lynch himself proved able to create feminist cinema, the authors of this dossier approach the film as an intricate text that frustrates traditional interpretive approaches based on the specter of the “male gaze” and the assumption of coherent, consistent character subjectivities. Consulting canonical feminist and philosophical texts as well as contemporary queer theory, sound studies, and star studies, the dossier authors write from the perspective of differing generations and disciplines, offering a lively array of critical reads.

  5. The Radical Intimacy of Rot
    Laura Westengard · pp. 30-35
    摘要

    This article by Laura Westengard, part of the dossier “Mulholland Drive at 25,” uses the lens of decomposition to show how Mulholland Drive (2001) demonstrates the multiplicity of life that always already exists within individuals that are falsely perceived to be singular. Turning toward rot as a model for queering an understanding of life, death, and self, this article provides a framework for analyzing the film's overlap and divergence of characters, the jumble of temporality that challenges a linear narrative, and the recursive merging, gaps, and doubles, offering us a vision of a kind of reproduction that occurs within, outside, and beyond normative understandings of self/other, life/death, gay/straight. Positing the film itself as decomposing corpse, Westengard suggests that the queer proliferations of rot open up not only a new reading but a queer way of engaging life, death, and the messy entanglements of existence.

  6. Sweet Dreams (Are Made of This)
    Lori Jo Marso · pp. 36-41
    摘要

    This article, part of the dossier “Mulholland Drive at 25,” argues that the “sweet dreams” of the women characters in David Lynch’s Mulholland Drive (2001) invite viewers to “feel like feminists.” Engaging the writings of Simone de Beauvoir in The Second Sex alongside Beauvoir’s 1959 essay on the myths of Brigitte Bardot, Marso finds subversive feminist dreamscapes in Lynch’s film. Feminist sweet dreams exist within the onscreen interactions of Betty/Diane with Rita/Camilla as the two women characters simultaneously navigate the demands of patriarchy and the mythic expectations for “becoming woman.” Lynch’s film demonstrates both gaps and overlap between gendered fantasies and ugly realities, inviting disorienting and uncomfortable feelings for viewers. These feelings, Marso suggests, can be interpreted and mobilized towards “feeling like feminists” such that viewers seek and find a feminist future within current conditions.

  7. “That’s Not My Voice”
    Temperance Aghamohammadi · pp. 42-47
    摘要

    This article, part of the dossier “Mulholland Drive at 25,” argues that the telephone plays a critical role in David Lynch’s Mulholland Drive (2001) by disturbing concepts of identity and placing telephonic technology near the heart of the film’s meaning. Drawing on the theories in Avital Ronell’s The Telephone Book: Technology, Schizophrenia, Electric Speech, and other sources, Aghamohammadi limns how the images of telephones in the film disrupt communication and pluralize identities rather than securing them.

  8. “This Is the Girl”
    Eileen G’Sell · pp. 48-56
    摘要

    Adopting film critic Melissa Anderson’s “acteurist” approach to Laura Dern in Lynch’s Inland Empire (2009), this essay explores Naomi Watts’s role in Mulholland Drive (2001), putting media scholar Mary R. Desjardins’s ideas around the “recyclable” nature of the female film star in dialogue with Naomi Klein’s theory of the “digital double.” If the Hollywood dream factory recycles, and alienates, female stars whose visage appear onscreen, what to make of the selves curated today for social media—especially among young women? Providing a close analysis of the prophetic declarative “This is the girl,” a revealing motif throughout the film linking Betty’s imagined Hollywood “dream place” to the film’s lackluster LA backdrop, this essay unpacks how Watts’s dual roles as Betty Elms and Diane Selwyn reflect the ways in which the abjection of the would-be Hollywood ingenue, either endured or witnessed from behind a screen, can warp a woman’s sense of self.

  9. How George A. Romero Inspired the Horror Studies Center
    Adam Lowenstein · pp. 57-62
    摘要

    This article recounts the genesis of the Horror Studies Center at the University of Pittsburgh. Relationships with the independent horror filmmaker Romero, the non-profit foundation and archival collection created in his name, and the city of Pittsburgh that Romero called home for most of his career are all explored. Romero’s landmark feature Night of the Living Dead (1968) plays an important role in Lowenstein’s personal as well as professional life: he screens it at his bar mitzvah party, and he relies on its lessons to give him hope in the wake of the anti-Semitic killings at Pittsburgh’s Tree of Life synagogue in 2018. Lowenstein’s experience hosting a screening of Romero’s Bruiser (2000) in Pittsburgh with the director in attendance is juxtaposed with a screening of Queens of the Dead (2025) in Pittsburgh with its own director: Tina Romero, George’s daughter.

  10. “To Film a Society Is Very Difficult”
    Jaimey Fisher · pp. 63-72
    摘要

    This article pertains to the new film, Miroirs No. 3, by the Berlin School stalwart, Christian Petzold, one of today’s most celebrated German-language filmmakers. It comprises a lengthy interview with Petzold about Miroirs, with discussion of its location in former East Germany, its re-shot ending, and its numerous influences, along with memories of his erstwhile teacher and collaborator, Harun Farocki. Petzold recounts how the new film returns to a family milieu some 26 years after his breakthrough The State I am In (Die innere Sicherheit, 2000) as well as to the Grimm’s fairy tale “The Burial Shroud,” the basis for Ghosts (Gespenster, 2005). The interview includes a discussion of Petzold’s own refugee family and its relationship to Nazism. The piece also comprises an introduction about the titles of Petzold’s films, especially his interest in double figures under the influence of Hitchcock’s Vertigo (1958) and Rebecca (1940).

  11. The Call Is Coming from Inside the Cottage
    Ramzi Fawaz · pp. 73-87
    摘要

    This essay offers a bold counter-reading to the charmed popular reception of Heated Rivalry, a 2025 HBO television series whose passionate depiction of explicit gay male sex and long-term romantic longing has been hailed as a revolutionary installment in the history of queer media representation. Fawaz identifies how the show’s central formal conceits—including its association of gay sex with competitive sports, the plot’s rapid-fire leaps across time that heighten sexual longing, the eroticization of romantic miscommunication, and the depiction of gay sex in the absence of gay cultural contexts—actually work to transform queer eroticism into a mirror image of heterosexual desire in its most dysfunctional expressions. Fawaz argues that audiences’ fanatical obsession with the show’s central romantic relationship between two closeted professional hockey rivals might demand a collective reckoning with the romance genre as a popular formation that can no longer adequately compensate for the real failures of the contemporary sex and dating scene, gay and straight alike, to provide meaningful or sustainable intimacy.

Film-Philosophy

Vol. 30, No. 2-3 · 2026-06 · 22 篇 · Edinburgh UP · Film/Philosophy
  1. Introduction: Existentialist Film-Philosophy
    David Sorfa, Lucy Bolton · pp. 179-184
  2. Whit Stillman's Cinema and the Existentialist Freedom of Bourgeois Nothingness
    David Sorfa · pp. 185-208
    摘要

    In this article I consider the films of Whit Stillman through the lens of existentialism to highlight ideas around freedom in contemporary society. I concentrate on the films of the Doomed Bourgeois in Love trilogy, Metropolitan (1990), Barcelona (1994) and The Last Days of Disco (1998) to show that these films offer us sympathetic understanding of a privileged class who struggle with questions of purpose, identity and absurdity in a world that seems to have no meaning. Jean-Paul Sartre's conception of nothingness underwrites the possibility of radical freedom and Stillman's films show how such freedom might look in the real world. I also offer a novel way of considering the difference between Sartre and Albert Camus's conceptions of the absurd.

  3. L’Avenir and Simone de Beauvoir on Independence, Freedom and Happiness
    Marguerite La Caze · pp. 209-228
    摘要

    Mia Hansen-Løve's film L’Avenir depicts a philosophy teacher, Nathalie Chazeaux, undergoing a serious existential crisis. Her husband Heinz ends their long marriage, her mother dies, and her book series is cancelled. Rather than despairing, Nathalie continues to pursue her projects and claims to be experiencing total freedom for the first time. Simone de Beauvoir contends in The Second Sex that in considering women's emancipation, we should not take happiness to be the aim, as it is a confining emotion. Instead, using the perspective of existentialist ethics, we should aspire to freedom. I examine how Beauvoir's distinctions between happiness, independence and freedom illuminate L’Avenir and how L’Avenir's portrayal of Nathalie's life crisis enhances understanding of their potential. We might see Nathalie as an example of what Beauvoir calls the independent woman and possibly even a free woman able to live without her ex-husband. I argue that closer analysis of the film does not support the view of Nathalie as completely free and happy. She herself says that older women are not valued, and she is harassed. Furthermore, the songs of lost love in the final scenes and the last shot of her living room with no-one in sight suggest her situation is ambiguous and that her Stoicism belies a loneliness and dread of the future.

  4. Éric Rohmer with Kierkegaard and Nietzsche: Desire, Chance and Freedom
    Sara Delshad · pp. 229-246
    摘要

    This article examines Éric Rohmer's cinema through the perspective of existential reflection, showing how his films engage with morality, faith and chance in everyday life. With particular attention to Søren Kierkegaard and Friedrich Nietzsche, I trace how these perspectives unfold across his major cycles. In the series of films known as the “Six Moral Tales” (1962–1972), Rohmer's protagonists embody a Kantian concern with self-imposed moral law, testing principles against desire and circumstance. In the later “Comedies and Proverbs” (1981–1987) and “Tales of the Four Seasons” (1990–1998), this framework widens: characters confront contingency and coincidence, evoking Kierkegaard's notion of faith as a leap beyond rational proof and Nietzsche's affirmation of chance as necessity. Through chance encounters and games of probability, Rohmer transforms ordinary experiences into existential moments, showing freedom not as certainty but as a negotiation between desire, chance and belief.

  5. Existentialism and Freedom in WWI Films: The Soldier as Being-for-Itself
    Xi Lu · pp. 247-268
    摘要

    Drawing on Jean-Paul Sartre's concepts of being-in-itself and being-for-itself, this article examines how war reduces soldiers to cogs in a war machine and transforms individuals into soldiers whose sole function is to fight, thereby reducing them from what Sartre terms free beings- for-themselves to limited beings- in-themselves. However, despite such attempts to impose constraints on human freedom, individuals can still exercise their freedom through acts of choice. I will examine two films set during World War I, Wooden Crosses (Raymond Bernard, 1932) and Paths of Glory (Stanley Kubrick, 1957), to explore the problem of radical freedom in existentialism. This article ultimately suggests that Sartre's existentialism enables a reading of war films that recognises that individuals assert their freedom and shape themselves even under the most constraining circumstances.

  6. Djibril Diop Mambéty's Decolonial Existentialism: Sartrean Praxis and the Critique of Bad Faith Humanism
    Eddy Troy · pp. 269-294
    摘要

    This article argues that Jean-Paul Sartre's writings on existentialism and political praxis illuminate the critical decolonial work of Djibril Diop Mambéty's cinema. The article analyses Touki Bouki (1973), Hyenas (1992), Le Franc (1994) and The Little Girl Who Sold the Sun (1999) in terms of Sartre's notions of bad faith, praxis and his critique of bourgeois humanism. This Sartrean reading of Mambéty's cinema foregrounds the relevance of the philosopher's thinking for decolonial film analysis. In drawing out the existentialist dimensions of Mambéty's film-philosophy, the article links the filmmaker's critiques of neocoloniality and universal humanism to adjacent conversations in Africana existentialism and Black studies.

  7. Bad Faith, Death and Neoliberal Funny Business in Weekend at Bernie's : The Working Dead
    Kaitlin Lake · pp. 295-315
    摘要

    This article reads Weekend at Bernie's (Ted Kotcheff, 1989) as not merely a throwaway farce, but an elucidation of the ways in which the logic of late capitalism both cultivates and relies on what Jean-Paul Sartre terms bad faith. Situated within this context, the film crystalises neoliberal logic through a highly specific rendering of 1980s corporate culture, from which the central conceit of the plot emerges: the absurd spectacle of a dead man dragged around, his corpse manipulated and posed to sustain the illusion of life. I triangulate the concepts of bad faith, death and neoliberalism by reading Weekend at Bernie's as a commentary on the cultural logic of late capitalism.

  8. Freedom, Anguish and Bad Faith in Mr. Nobody : Sartrean Existentialism in Cinema
    Thaís de Sá Oliveira · pp. 316-339
    摘要

    This article explores how the film Mr. Nobody (2009), directed by Jaco Van Dormael, illustrates key concepts of Sartrean existentialism. The film exemplifies an existential condition through the character of Nemo Nobody (Jared Leto), whose life unfolds across multiple possible futures. His indecision and avoidance of choice reveal existential anguish and bad faith. The film's structure, which fragments time and narrative, mirrors the weight of responsibility inherent in every decision. Through detailed examination of key scenes and choices, I argue that the film not only illustrates Sartrean thought but also deepens it by dramatizing philosophical dilemmas in a concrete, affective form. Ultimately, Mr. Nobody affirms that freedom, though distressing, is inescapable and constitutive of human existence.

  9. Kore-eda with and against Sartre: Existential Education in Nobody Knows
    Haotian Wu · pp. 340-363
    摘要

    This article makes a case for Hirokazu Kore-eda as an existential director. By reading his film Nobody Knows (2004) against Sartre's anti-essentialist existentialism, I will show that the film disabuses us of several Sartrean doctrines and reorients us towards an existential education in Kore-eda's cinema. Contrasting the despair and disappearance of Keiko, a Sartrean existentialist heroine par excellence, with the development of Akira, Nobody Knows moves us away from the freedom and transcendence in Sartrean existentialism and emphasises the formation of the self and the growth in self-knowledge through navigating the division between existence and essence. Portrayed by suggestive dialogue, tactile close-ups, and spontaneous performances, this existential education is advanced through a series of mirror images that invite comparison and reflection. The film peaks in a tragic wisdom before death and opens the transformed self to community.

  10. Iris Murdoch's Existentialism in French Exit : The Difficulty of Thinking Perpetually of Oneself
    Lucy Bolton · pp. 364-379
    摘要

    This article argues that the popular perception of Sartrean existential bleakness permeates French Exit (Azazel Jacobs, 2020), and its publicity, and yet Iris Murdoch's take on Sartre emerges as the film's overriding perspective. I propose that the film stages an encounter between Sartrean and Murdochian understandings of existentialism. The film is about a cold, cynical, widowed woman, who is longing for death to solve all her problems. She is in a state of paralysis, ignoring reality, and emotionally dominating her son, who is similarly inert. Neither of them seem concerned with creating meaningful lives, and – when faced with the shame of their impending penury – they run away to Paris. In the capital city of existential ennui, however, their inertia is invaded by people who represent their pasts, presents and futures, ensuring that they are not able to stay alone wallowing in their familial purposelessness. Protagonist Frances Price learns to see beyond her egocentrism and to release her son to pursue love. Murdoch's vision of our loving interconnectedness trumps Sartre's commitment to our suffering at the judgement of others.

  11. Indigenous Existentialism in the Films of Warwick Thornton
    Oscar Davis, Damian Cox · pp. 380-400
    摘要

    Existentialist philosophy, in particular the work of Franz Fanon and Jean-Paul Sartre, was crucial to early postcolonial theorising and, as demonstrated in the work of Brendan Hokowhitu, continues to be an important perspective for understanding Indigenous postcolonial experience. Existentialism is a valuable tool in the on-going attempt to gain clarity about the nature of racist oppression and ways of overcoming it. We focus on two films by Warwick Thornton: Samson and Delilah (2009) and Sweet Country (2017) that together form a study of an Indigenous existentialism, and in contrasting ways illustrate both the difficulty of, and possibility of, emergent transcendent agency in postcolonial environments.

  12. Stan Brakhage, Jean-Paul Sartre and Existentialism: Cinema de trop
    Byron Davies, Addison Ellis · pp. 401-426
    摘要

    In 1961, Stan Brakhage made Sartre's Nausea for a public television introduction to existentialism hosted by Sartre scholar and translator Hazel Barnes. Brakhage later returned to that work, editing and intervening on a copy of it to make Black Vision (1965). We argue that despite Brakhage's ambivalence towards Sartrean existentialism, these works illuminate Sartre by demonstrating important continuities between hypnagogic consciousness and nausea. We additionally argue that Brakhage was likely the most appropriate filmmaker for adapting Nausea, owing to his careerlong grasp of how the experience of radical contingency could permeate not only individual consciousness, but also the filmmaking medium itself.

  13. Existential Failure and Utopian Collapse in Megalopolis : A Philosophy of Imperfection
    Emad Abouata Amlashi · pp. 427-447
    摘要

    This article examines Megalopolis (Francis Ford Coppola, 2024) as a cinematic enactment of existential imperfection – where failure is not merely depicted but structurally embedded through narrative collapse, temporal disjunction and the erosion of authorial coherence. Drawing on the existential philosophies of Albert Camus, Søren Kierkegaard and Samuel Beckett, alongside critical frameworks from Trinh T. Minh-ha, I argue that Coppola does not simply risk failure but that he formalizes it as a philosophical operation. Through close analysis of recursive structures, visual dissonance and mise-en-scène destabilization, the article explores how Megalopolis renders revolt, paradox and collapse as aesthetic logic. Yet the film also interrogates the ethical limits of this wager: when failure is mediated by auteurist discourse, it risks becoming stylized spectacle rather than ontological exposure. Rather than framing Megalopolis as a misunderstood masterpiece or failed epic, I position it as a contested site where cinematic imperfection confronts the limits of sincerity and philosophical legibility in contemporary screen-philosophy.

  14. What Can Cinema Do? Saint Omer and French Existentialist Disclosure as Film Theory
    Kate Ince · pp. 448-465
    摘要

    France's leading existential phenomenologists Simone de Beauvoir and Jean-Paul Sartre both wrote extensively about literature and rather less about film. Concentrating on Beauvoir and especially on a lecture she gave at a televised event in Paris in December 1964, I ask whether the lecture, “What Can Literature Do?”, can also apply to the medium of film, answering this question in the affirmative. It then considers the resulting Beauvoirean film theory alongside a contemporary French film, Alice Diop's Saint Omer (2022), analysing the film's visual style as is required by reading the theory alongside its fictional presentation of real events. Diop is one of France's few successful black female directors and since the two protagonists of Saint Omer are also black women, the article concludes by asking what the writings of Frantz Fanon have to offer to an existentialist screen theory seeking to build the racialisation of looking into the spectatorial dynamics of film.

  15. The Existential Ambiguity of Authentic Love in Alice Diop's Saint Omer
    Kelli Fuery · pp. 466-487
    摘要

    Using Simone de Beauvoir's and Frantz Fanon's existentialist phenomenology, this article examines an existentialist ethics of recognition as an act of authentic love in Alice Diop's Saint Omer (2022). Loosely based on a true story, Saint Omer tells a story of filicide: Laurence Coly leaves her baby on a beach to die and we follow the subsequent courtroom drama as it unfolds. The film also follows Rama, a literature professor intending to write up the case as a contemporary version of the Medea myth. I suggest Saint Omer presents an ethics of recognition in its avoidance of the superficial question of what is ethical (the act of filicide); instead it shows how cinema might express an ethics that is central to reciprocal recognition, an expression of authentic love for the other. It asks whether such an ethics of recognition is even possible if it cannot be authentic, that is, if it cannot escape the prescription of recognition already embedded in gendered and colonial relations. Through Beauvoir's philosophy of love and Fanon's notion of ankylosis, I consider how an ethics of recognition can serve as an actional aspect of ambiguity, valorizing the unrealizable of the Other helping to determine the possibility of authentic love.

  16. Magical Emotions: Jean-Paul Sartre and The Headless Woman
    Ruochen Bo · pp. 488-510
    摘要

    This article reexamines Jean-Paul Sartre's early theory of emotion in light of his reflections on ego, consciousness, language and narrative, clarifying his often-overlooked but critical conception of magic. I first show how magical emotional consciousness transforms the instrumental world by disrupting deterministic logic and then trace a parallel reversal in The Transcendence of the Ego, where spontaneity is displaced onto the ego and its states. I argue that these forms of magic converge with Sartre's concern about narration and expression – how meaning is retroactively conferred on events and how representation both reveals and degrades lived experience – thereby sharpening Sartre's enigmatic suggestion that the social world itself has a magical structure. To assess and concretize these claims, I turn to Lucrecia Martel's The Headless Woman (2008), whose protagonist's headlessness uncannily illustrates, yet also puts pressure on, a Sartrean account of emotional transformation as conscious but unreflective, as purposive yet spontaneous. I show that Martel's attention to framing echoes Sartre's suspicion of language's hold on things, exposing the limits of expressive mastery and thereby revealing the stubborn autonomy of objects and others. The film thus illuminates how emotional magic implicates freedom, bad faith and social complicity.

  17. Screening Existentialist Narcissism in Todd Field's Tár
    Tarja Laine · pp. 511-533
    摘要

    Inspired by the writings of Jean-Paul Sartre, this article approaches Todd Field's Tár (2022) as an existentialist satire, in which humour is borne out by a conflict between bad faith and the way in which people can miss the truth about themselves by self-delusion. Commonly classified as a psychological backstage drama, many of the scenes in Tár rely on darkly humorous delight, deliberatively indulging in vindictive attitude that clashes with our basic moral feelings. This article analyses the aesthetic-affective conflicts in Tár manifesting themselves along the axis of seeing and being seen, as much of its protagonist's efforts are defined by how she wants to appear in the eyes of others and her sense of self based on narcissistic delusions. But she cannot control the field of vision organized by the others’ perceptions, and as soon as Tár starts recognizing herself in these various perceptions, her mental stability dissolves while her disavowed guilt takes the upper hand. The final part of the article discusses the ethical implications that result from the film positioning the spectators within the very experience of a morally inscrutable character.

  18. Existential Feelings in the Cinema of Ingmar Bergman
    Robert Sinnerbrink · pp. 534-554
    摘要

    In this article I propose that films typically described as existential are not only concerned with classical existential themes – alienation or anxiety, solitude and despair – but with affective states that Matthew Ratcliffe calls “existential feelings” or “feelings of being.” These terms designate various bodily feelings – the bodily feeling of being, of existence, of belonging - that express a relationship towards the world understood as a space of possibilities. To explore this idea, I turn to a discussion of Ingmar Bergman's Silence of God trilogy, comprising Through a Glass Darkly (1961), Winter Light (1963) and The Silence (1963), and explore negative existential feelings that underlie more familiar existential moods or emotions such as anxiety or dread. This consideration of the important role of existential feelings in the work of a canonical existentialist auteur opens new ways of understanding existential moods in contemporary cinema.
