Games and Culture

Vol. 21, No. 7 · 2026-11 · 15 篇 · SAGE · Games
  1. Beyond Game Blocks: Adolescent Girl Players' Perspectives During COVID-19
    Katherine J. E. Hewett, Bethanie C. Pletcher · pp. 1511-1538 · online 2025-05-13
    摘要

    The Entertainment Software Association (ESA) found that women accounted for 46% of U.S. gamers in 2024. However, women players (especially girl players) remain an under-researched demographic in game studies. The aim of this qualitative case study was to expand upon previous research discoveries and explore the 21st-century skills, design processes, perspectives, and behaviors of adolescent girl players as they played, created, and survived in a video game ( Minecraft ). The data analysis involved triangulating eleven qualitative case study interviews with visual artifacts and observations from twenty-one girl players. A hundred and fifty students collaborated with the participants throughout various stages of the study's three phases. The qualitative coding process generated five themes: (1) Father Figures , (2) Community House , (3) Gamer Girl , (4) Lockdown , and (5) Trial and Error . Each theme organizes and shares how girls viewed, theorized, and interpreted their video game experiences during the COVID-19 pandemic.

  2. Minecraft as a Pedagogical Tool: A Systematic Literature Review (2014–2024)
    Javier García-Álvarez, Jesús Acevedo-Borrega · pp. 1539-1561 · online 2025-05-15
    摘要

    On the 15th anniversary of Minecraft's release (2009), this systematic review of 106 articles has been conducted to outline the scientific production of the past decade regarding the use of Minecraft in education. Among the most notable findings is the relationship between the most frequently employed game mechanics and the most recurrent educational objectives, determining that construction and programming/problem-solving are the primary mechanics utilized in educational interventions, while the analysis of Minecraft as a tool and its influence on cognitive development constitute the most extensively studied research objectives. Some of the main identified limitations highlight the need to analyse in-game activities through standardized methodological frameworks to enhance comparability with other studies, thereby facilitating the association between game mechanics and learning processes. Finally, further exploration of underdeveloped research areas is encouraged to expand the understanding of Minecraft's impact across diverse educational settings.

  3. ‘Born of the Blood, Made Men by the Blood, Undone by the Blood’: Bloodborne as a Critical Reflection of Ghosts of the Anthropocene
    Sara Skott, Karl-Fredrik Skott Bengtson, Michael Fiddler · pp. 1562-1582 · online 2025-05-19
    摘要

    Drawing on critical frameworks in criminology, including Popular Criminology and Ghost Criminology, this paper aims to explore how Bloodborne offers a critical reflection of the haunting nature of the harms inflicted by humans in the age of the Anthropocene. More specifically, we aim to examine how the cultural text of Bloodborne illustrates humanity's corruption of the natural and the harms this inflicts, arguing that this can be understood as a reflection of ghosts of the Anthropocene. Using ethnographic content analysis, three main themes were uncovered in Bloodborne that related to humanity's corruption of the natural, the harms this inflict, as well as how this can be understood as a reflection of ghosts of the Anthropocene: Sanguine Exploitation, Horrific Creation , and Monstrous Extinction . We conclude by arguing that Bloodborne , in evoking the horrors of climate catastrophe, is an important cultural script that responds to McBrien’s necrocene and Haraway’s Chthulucene.

  4. The Business of Play: Financialization and Optimization in World of Warcraft
    Daniel Nielsen · pp. 1583-1600 · online 2025-05-27
    摘要

    In World of Warcraft (WOW) , thousands of players work together to analyze the game's rules and maximize their rewards. Organized boosting—a paid player service—is widely seen as undermining the meritocratic ideal that ties rewards to effort and skill. Drawing on semistructured interviews with six former providers of boosting services, this study argues that optimization in digital games is not solely a strategic choice, but often a necessity shaped by financial pressures. The introduction of token-based economic elements, such as the WoW token, nudges players into financial speculation and wealth accumulation, transforming optimization into a form of labor. This study considers boosting within the framework of financialization, highlighting how optimization mirrors broader tensions in a game culture shaped by corporate commodification practices.

  5. Do Video Games Matter? Examining Video Games’ Pathways to Legitimacy and Cultural Value Through Cultural Intermediaries
    Charlotte Gislam, Garry Crawford, Gaynor Bagnall, Victoria Gosling, Neta Yodovich · pp. 1601-1620 · online 2025-06-04
    摘要

    After years on the fringe of our cultural and social life, video games are becoming more recognized as legitimate and culturally valuable. This paper examines the process of their legitimation through the role of ‘cultural intermediaries’, who frame video games as meaningful cultural artifacts. Drawing on Bourdieu's theories, this paper explores how external cultural institutions legitimize video games and how cross-media pollination further reinforces their cultural status. By analysing how broader industries have integrated and contextualized video games within their own domains, this paper highlights how video games contribute to cultural production and are gradually being positioned within established cultural hierarchies.

  6. Queer(ed) Avatars: Exploring Visual Identity Construction in Final Fantasy XIV Online Gaymers
    Karl van Heerden · pp. 1621-1644 · online 2025-06-09
    摘要

    I explore gaymers’ use of virtual photography to construct their avatar persona in Final Fantasy XIV Online . The study of game photography is a field that has seen relatively sparse engagement from scholars in the past decade. In this article, I discuss virtual photography's potential in gaymers’ visual construction of avatar personas in massively multiplayer online role-playing games. I focus on “gaymers” as they seek alternative identity affordances, expressing dissatisfaction with the narrow range of possibilities in games. In contextualizing the phenomenon, I engage with theories on virtual photography, persona studies, and social networking. I apply this theoretical framework to a series of Instagram accounts dedicated to FFXIV Online avatars. I incorporate a queer methodology in my research to expose how gaymers resist heteronormativity in games. My findings indicate that gaymers queer FFXIV Online 's mechanics and visuals through modding and shaders to visually co-construct a digital self.

  7. Emotions in Game Data Work
    Taina Myöhänen, Heikki Tyni, Olli Sotamaa · pp. 1645-1663 · online 2025-06-16
    摘要

    This study explores the emotional dimensions of data work within the game industry. Through interviews with Finnish game industry professionals, the research reveals that data-driven work practices evoke a wide range of emotions, ranging from pleasure and satisfaction to insecurity and frustration. Data can act both as a helpful workmate and as a source of professional conflict and frustration, impacting the worker's sense of professionalism. Data-driven work also compels workers to individually confront ethical questions. Our findings highlight the interplay between creative work processes and data analytics, emphasizing the emotional labor that game industry professionals need to handle in a data-driven work culture. Additionally, the research addresses ethical considerations and the emotional work required to reconcile public perceptions of data usage. The study contributes to the broader understanding of emotional work in new data-intensive professions and advocates for more emotionally sustainable practices in data-driven game development.

  8. Understanding How Marginal National Video Game Industries Differ
    Jacqueline Burgess, Elina Roinoti, Alexander Muscat, Anthony Grace, Christian Jones · pp. 1664-1679 · online 2025-06-16
    摘要

    This article compares small (19 employees or fewer) video game developers’ commercial and creative mindsets in three marginal video game industries, Australia, New Zealand, and Greece, to understand how these industries differ. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with 62 participants across the three countries and analysed using thematic analysis. Despite all three national industries’ marginal status, there were similarities and clear differences in how the participant developers approached video game production. In particular, the lack of institutional support available for the Greek industry resulted in developers utilising networks and reputation. The research highlights how marginal, national video game industries cannot be simply grouped together because the developers within them display differences, as a result of their industry contexts, which influence and impact their video game development. These findings have implications for support from governments and education.

  9. Trains, Stations, and Railways as Harbingers of Biopolitics in Digital Games: From Governance to Hauntology
    Michał Kłosiński, Agata Zarzycka · pp. 1680-1697 · online 2025-06-27
    摘要

    This article presents research on the digital game representations of trains, stations, and railways from the perspective of the biopolitical theory. We aimed to analyze and depict different elements of life governance mediated by games that use rail infrastructure in their main narrative arcs, mechanics, and esthetics. We chose rail because of its well-documented role in colonial and imperial projects of modernity which have become the objects of game representations. Analyzing how games mediate rail infrastructure is therefore important for understanding how they mediate biopolitical projects, which elements can be traced as manifestations of biopolitics in these games, and what role they play in representations of ideas such as nationality, progress, history, and modernity.

  10. Datafication of Digital Intimacy: The Dual Logic of Empowerment and Commodification
    Tingting Hu, Liang Ge · pp. 1698-1722 · online 2025-07-21
    摘要

    This study explores the datafication of digital intimacy in female-oriented otome games, analyzing how these games construct databases of idealized masculinities and romantic narratives, balancing empowerment and commodification. Through digital ethnography, walkthrough, and interviews with Chinese women players between 2023 and 2024, we reveal how otome games mediate intimacies between women players and virtual male characters, reflecting sociocultural values and shaping affective experiences. These women-oriented games collect intimate data to optimize design and profitability, offering immersive, customizable romantic experiences while commodifying emotional investments. This dual dynamic underscores a tension in emotional capitalism, where intimacy is both enriched and exploited. By examining the interplay between digital intimacy, datafication, and emotional capitalism, our study further contributes to the critical discourse on how role-playing games monetize personal and emotional experiences, emphasizing the need to safeguard user sovereignty as digital platforms capitalize on affective labor.

  11. Rhyming Rudely: From Medieval Flyting to Invective Poetry in Video Games
    Agata Waszkiewicz, Kristian A. Bjørkelo · pp. 1723-1738 · online 2025-07-25
    摘要

    This article examines how games continue and represent the historical rituals of invective poetry exchanges. The article juxtaposes the analog, multiplayer card game Cards Against Humanity , known for its transgressive, profanity-based humor, with two single-player video games that present the invective poetry competition as (more or less) historically accurate activity. The former is argued to be a continuation of the same tradition that encompasses various historical practices such as Germanic flyting or African sounding, while the latter is an attempt at representation, without inviting the players to participate in the same practices. The difference between the card game, which makes a freer use of profanities, and the video game representation, which is devoid of it, are used to discuss to investigate the importance of the rituals involving invective poetry in the contemporary culture, as well as theorize about the differences seen in the analog and video games.

  12. The Dullahan, From Fairy to Undead Tracing the Cultural Hybridization of Irish Folklore in Japanese Games and Their Paratexts
    Jessy Escande · pp. 1739-1760 · online 2025-08-03
    摘要

    This study investigates how the Irish Dullahan—a headless fairy death messenger—came to be recast as an undead monster in Japanese popular culture. Contrary to the trend of Western tabletop role-playing games playing a central role in similar Japanese reinventions, our analysis reveals that the undead Dullahan was an exception. Instead, we demonstrate how it was shaped by the convergence of foreign influences during the 1980s and 1990s, with a central role played by the Japanese Celtic boom in that period. By examining early video games, fantasy dictionaries, and other paratexts, we highlight how cultural brokers (e.g., game artists and editors) actively reshaped imported folklore. Our findings contribute to broader discussions on cultural hybridization and underscore how games and related media powerfully influence the global perception of folkloric motifs across the broader mediascape.

  13. Exploring Online Gaming Communities Through Documentary Filmmaking
    Elina Roinioti, Renard Gluzman · pp. 1761-1782 · online 2025-08-26
    摘要

    This paper explores video game documentaries as a method for researching online gaming communities. We begin by framing documentary filmmaking as a practice-based approach, a creative form of video ethnography, with the film serving as the focal output. Our analysis draws on a close reading of four recent video game documentaries that highlight European voices, alongside insights from our own fieldwork. We focus on the affordances of this approach in capturing embodied, affective, and performative aspects of play that text-based ethnography may struggle to convey. The selected films also offer different stances on the agency of both participant-protagonists and researcher-director.   14. Tomes and Tombs: Toward a Phenomenology of Interiors in Dungeons & Dragons
    Cameron Macdonell · pp. 1783-1806 · online 2025-09-03
    摘要

    This article examines the evolution of immersive spatial design in Dungeons & Dragons through phenomenological analysis of interiors in Tomb of Horrors , the only module recurrent for all the game's major editions. Drawing on game studies, phenomenology, and historiography, I compare the module's textual and visual assets to assess how spatial immersion is constructed, disrupted, and reimagined. The analysis shows that the first edition's visual–textual dissonance produces interpretive ambiguity that might enhance embodied player engagement. Later editions increasingly removed visual assets, but the rise of mixed-media platforming via virtual tabletops (VTTs) brings the visuality of spatial immersion back into focus. By reading the dissonance of visual and textual assets through Heideggerian and Merleau-Pontian models of space, I argue that VTT designers can benefit from intentional ambiguities to enrich experiential depth. Tomb of Horrors thus becomes a key site for understanding how tabletop and digital design practices shape ludic interiority.

  15. Geopolitical Implications of E-Sports
    Austin Wontepaga Luguterah · pp. 1807-1833 · online 2025-09-08
    摘要

    The meteoric rise of electronic sports (e-sports) has transformed competitive gaming into a platform for geopolitical influence, cultural diplomacy, and digital governance. This article explores how e-sports reshape international relations and soft power, using frameworks from international relations, cultural studies, and political economy. It highlights how states like South Korea, China, Russia, and Arabian Peninsula leverage e-sports to project cultural capital, while Western markets promote corporate-led models that extend platform capitalism. It examines tensions between state-driven cyber sovereignty and corporate control over digital infrastructure. Regional dynamics from Southeast Asian cooperation to U.S.–China tech rivalries highlight e-sports’ role in economic positioning and international relations. As AI and blockchain reshape gaming's future, the article calls for deeper inquiry into e-sports’ implications for global governance, digital citizenship, and cultural diplomacy.

Historical Journal of Film Radio and Television

Vol. 46, No. 3 · 2026-07-03 · 27 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Film/Media history
  1. ‘The Movies and You’: COMPO, the MPAA, and the Branding of the Film Industry in the Mid-Twentieth Century
    Jennifer Porst · pp. 539-565 · online 2025-12-16
    摘要

    In the late 1940s, when faced with a declining box office and seemingly relentless negative publicity and legal challenges, Hollywood embarked on perhaps the largest publicity campaign in its history. The series of shorts, ‘The Movies and You’, which was produced between 1949 and 1951, represents an extremely rare attempt by all factions of the film industry to work together during a time of great industrial stress and disruption to rebrand itself as an industry. An analysis of these shorts and their production and distribution provides valuable insight into the anxieties, objectives, and behaviours of the film industry during a time of incredible change, as well as presenting an early case study of industry branding.

  2. In Tune with Changing Times: Radio’s Role in Colonisation and Decolonisation in British West Africa, 1935–1960
    Abdullahi Tasiu Abubakar · pp. 566-590 · online 2025-12-26
    摘要

    First introduced in West Africa by Europeans as an instrument of colonisation and western-modelled modernisation, radio later became a tool of decolonisation but also of surveillance and subversion. This article examines the role of radio in British West Africa, focusing mainly on Nigeria and the Gold Coast (Ghana) and briefly on Sierra Leone. It draws on original archival research to illuminate the multifaceted functions of radio in colonisation and decolonisation processes, the struggle for its control, and the limits of its performance. It traces the convoluted journey of radio broadcasting and reception in the region, from wired to wireless broadcasting, and from communal listening to private reception. The article also looks at the decolonisation of broadcasting itself, unpacking the changes witnessed in its administrative structure, staffing, training and programming.

  3. Reclaiming Şerif Gören’s Legacy: Correcting the Historical Record on Yol ( the Road , 1982)
    Tayfun Tezel-Luxembourgeus · pp. 591-612 · online 2025-12-16
    摘要

    The article challenges the long-standing misattribution of Turkey’s first Palme d’Or-winning film to Yılmaz Güney, arguing that the contributions of the film’s actual director, Şerif Gören, were systematically erased. Despite Gören overseeing the entire production under martial law, Güney—incarcerated during the entirety of filming—was falsely credited as the film’s director or co-director, a narrative perpetuated by Cannes and later scholarship even to this day. The article exposes how political symbolism, Cold War dynamics, and Güney’s calculated amplification as a dissident political figure overshadowed Gören’s directorial labour. Through archival research, eyewitness testimonies, and textual analysis, the study reveals Gören’s creative agency, including his radical restructuring of the script and logistical ingenuity. It also critiques Güney’s post-production edits, such as the unauthorised insertion of the title ‘Kürdistan’, which distorted Gören’s vision and exposed him to legal peril. The article provides a case study that reveals biases in global cinema, where institutional and ideological forces often privilege mythic narratives over collaborative authorship. By centring Gören’s legacy, the article calls for a re-evaluation of The Road’s history and a reckoning with the politics of recognition in film historiography.

  4. From Red Sorghum to Main Melody: Contemporary Chinese Language Film Theatrical Distribution in Germany Since 1989
    Yongli Li · pp. 613-629 · online 2026-01-09
    摘要

    In the post-Cold War era, the two formerly divided German film industries merged, reorganised, and transformed, setting Germany on a complex path within transnational film flows amid accelerating cultural globalisation, neoliberalism and shifting geopolitics. This paper traces the turning points over the course of this transformation since 1989, and argues that several social, political, and industrial factors have contributed to changes in Chinese language film distribution in the territory. By analysing two distributors, Rapid Eye Movies and Guang Hua Media, I highlight the diverse programming strategies that have emerged since the unification, with the former specialises in arthouse cinemas, the latter in main melody films and hesuipian. My paper adopts critical media industry research that moves beyond political economy frameworks that highlight the role of media institutions to shed light on the programming and marketing practices and the agents involved in the transnational film circulation.

  5. Interwar Romanies in the British Pathé (1922–1939)
    Rafael Buhigas Jiménez, Lidia Merás · pp. 630-652 · online 2026-01-22
    摘要

    This article aims to analyse the representation of the Roma people in the films preserved by the British Pathé online archive.   6. Domesticating the U.S. Communications Satellite System: Hawai’i and Television Synchronicity
    Selena A. Dickey · pp. 653-676 · online 2026-01-30
    摘要

    The arrival of the ‘satellite age’ in the 1960s revolutionised television by enabling signal transmission over vast distances in seconds. Yet, Hawai’i—one of the U.S.’s most remote TV markets—remained disconnected from full network interconnection until the late 1970s. This article explores the reasons behind this delay, focusing on Hawai’i’s unique ‘foreign domestic’ status shaped by settler colonialism and U.S. imperialism. Prior to satellites, the Islands were physically and televisually isolated, relying on delayed film (kinescope) shipments for content from the mainland. The emergence of domestic satellite systems proposed by ABC and NBC, and later Western Union and RCA, disrupted traditional distribution network topologies and sparked debates over national inclusion and service equity. State advocacy and shifting federal policies gradually paved the way for Hawai’i’s integration, though it eventually required creative business strategies that navigated and exploited regulatory loopholes. Ultimately, this article demonstrates how the pursuit of a unified national television community exposed the infrastructural and regulatory barriers that marginalised Hawai’i, revealing the intricate relationship between geography, technology, policy, and national identity.

  7. Perpetuating Fascism: Propaganda and the Temporality of Phonography
    Jonathan Thomas · pp. 677-699 · online 2026-01-04
    摘要

    In this article, I analyse how Italian fascism aimed to perpetuate itself through the use of sound media, especially phonography. I examine how propaganda expressed and was shaped by the articulation between fascism’s regime of historicity - its relationship to time - and the regimes of temporality of phonography and radio - the temporal framework in which they frame the actions that mobilise them. To do so, I have chosen to investigate, after presenting a brief political history of Italian phonography, two practical and social fields crucial to the perpetuation of fascism: education and war. The former prepared the way for the latter, and both were tasked with propagating the ideal of national regeneration placed at the heart of this political project, thereby offering an ideal field of investigation to better understand how the regime of historicity determined its manifestation in the field of propaganda and the dissemination of its imaginary. I shall refer to sources from state archives, the general and specialist press, and a discography of around 900 propaganda records produced for a research project concerning the recorded sound propaganda of the Italian fascist regime.

  8. Revisiting the financial interest & syndication rules: A discursive and industrial analysis of U.S. Television production, 1971–1990
    Peter Arne Johnson · pp. 700-725 · online 2026-02-05
    摘要

    For over half a century, industrial discourses have positioned the prospect of financial regulation in U.S. television—specifically the (re)introduction of the Financial Interest and Syndication (Fin-Syn) Rules—as a catalyst for free enterprise, creative freedom, and program diversity. In this article, I discuss Fin-Syn and the rise of independent production in the 1970s, juxtaposing progressive discourses against the material history of the period. I argue that while the Fin-Syn era is often celebrated as a “golden age” for independent television and program diversity, several macroeconomic and industry factors beyond Fin-Syn, particularly the major Hollywood studios’ retrenchment from production, facilitated the rise of independent producers like Tandem, MTM, Spelling, and Lorimar in 1970s. By the 1980s, prior to Fin-Syn’s repeal, network television’s deficit financing model, a global crisis, and the economic attributes of television ultimately reinforced the dominance of the vertically integrated studios and limited the long-term viability of smaller production companies. This revisionist history sheds light on current industrial crises, as Fin-Syn is once again presented as a solution to industrial blight. By closely examining the intersection of discourse and material practice around financing, this article illustrates how economic narratives can shape the trajectory of media history, including the contemporary moment.

  9. Transnational Histories of Radio Genres: The Formation and Development of Serial Radio Fiction in Finland
    Heidi Keinonen · pp. 726-740 · online 2026-02-05
    摘要

    While public service broadcasters are typically regarded as national institutions, their histories are profoundly transnational. Ideas, programmes, and models have crossed national borders since the earliest days of radio transmission. Yet the transnational histories of radio genres have remained largely unexplored. This article seeks to address that gap by analysing the formation and development of three serial fiction genres—family drama, crime series, and radio comedy—in the context of the Finnish public broadcasting company Yleisradio. Series representing these genres were among the most popular and longest-running radio shows in Finland. Drawing on an extensive collection of empirical material—including Yleisradio’s Annual reports, programming metadata, newspaper clippings, and radio programmes—the research reveals a variety of transnational exchange in programme production. It also underscores the need for further research that places the transnational dynamics at the forefront.

  10. Infrastructures of Influence: The British Federation of Film Societies and the Circulation of Soft Power, 1960–2000
    Matthew Rule-Jones · pp. 741-766 · online 2026-02-06
    摘要

    This article examines the role of the British Federation of Film Societies (BFFS) in the international circulation of film culture from 1960 to 2000, arguing that the Federation functioned as an infrastructural conduit for cultural diplomacy through which soft power could be generated. Drawing on newly catalogued archival materials from the recently established BFFS Archive in Sheffield, the article analyses seven case studies of film societies affiliated with the BFFS between the 1960s and 1990s, in contexts ranging from postcolonial India and post-Soviet Poland to the pre-millennium Faroe Islands, Switzerland, and London. While Joseph Nye’s concept of soft power has typically been applied to state-led cultural diplomacy, this article extends recent critiques by showing how cultural influence can emerge from the logistical and bureaucratic structures of non-governmental institutions. It proposes a four-part typology—coincidental, strategic, logistical, and negated soft power—to account for the varied ways in which the BFFS created the conditions for influence to be accrued depending on local conditions, institutional design, and audience access. In doing so, the article repositions the BFFS not as a purely domestic cultural body, but as part of a wider transnational infrastructure that linked national film cultures with international publics in often unexpected ways.

  1. Off-Air Recording, Tricontinental Cinema and University Libraries in the UK
    Richard MacDonald · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    This article examines the historical conjuncture in which off-air recording of television broadcasts became embedded within UK universities, diversifying film collections and expanding access to films from the global south. The article traces the emergence of off-air recording and its development as a long-term, decentralised practice enabled by changes to copyright legislation belatedly catching up with the affordances of the videocassette recorder (VCR). In a parallel development, innovation and experiment in the UK broadcast landscape, the establishment of Channel 4 and its pioneering film programming dedicated to global cinema, provided a regular supply of previously inaccessible films for recording. On this basis university libraries and film departments built substantial permanent film collections for teaching and research. Prompted by the persistence of hard to access titles as off-air recordings on the shelves of many university libraries, this article activates the history of off-air recording as a decentralised capacity to record, collect and curate, cultivated in-house by university libraries.

  2. Structural Friction: Reginald Eyre and the Limits of British Film Propaganda in Wartime Japan, 1916–1919
    Satoru Fukamachi · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    This article reconstructs the British effort to distribute official war films in Japan between 1916 and 1919 through the career of Reginald Eyre, the agent who managed it, drawing on the largely untapped Foreign Office and Ministry of Information records at The National Archives read against contemporary Japanese film journalism. It argues that the campaign’s uneven fortunes are best understood through the concept of structural friction: the resistance generated when a media flow encoded within one industrial, institutional, and cultural system enters a reception space organised by different codes. Three distinct layers of friction—industrial, formal, and affective—are distinguished and weighed. Britain’s marginal film industry and the episodic, benshi-unfriendly form of its actuality footage, rather than any simple absence of shared war experience, emerge as the decisive obstacles in commercial exhibition. Yet the campaign is shown to have been no uniform failure: it secured Imperial patronage and elite diplomatic visibility, reached very large audiences through a non-commercial circuit of schools, YMCA halls, and factories, and produced a bespoke royal film tailored to local values. Eyre appears less as a functionary than as a cultural intermediary, and the case illuminates the reception, not merely the production, of transnational wartime propaganda.

  3. ‘Artist Mill-Hand in Film Studio’: Harold Blackburn, Post-War British Film Production and The Making of The Master of Bankdam (1947)
    Richard Farmer · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-09-26
    摘要

    When The Master of Bankdam went into production at the Nettlefold film studios at Walton-on-Thames, Surrey, in the summer of 1946, Harold Blackburn was in attendance. Blackburn was a millhand-cum-artist from Huddersfield, Yorkshire – where location shooting for The Master of Bankdam had previously taken place – and had been engaged to act as a technical advisor on the film. Blackburn helped producers create an ‘authentic’ vision of the woollen industry in Victorian Yorkshire using his knowledge of working practices, costume and accent. But Blackburn also passed information from Walton back to Huddersfield, in the form of a series of articles written for the town’s Daily Examiner newspaper which explained the role he played in The Master of Bankdam’s production. When viewed in conjunction with a collection of sketches Blackburn made during his time at Nettlefold, the articles also paint a detailed picture of a British film studio in the immediate post-war period, and especially of the people who worked in it. As a sympathetic and observant outsider granted prolonged access to the spaces of cinematic production, Blackburn’s record provides fascinating insight into the nature of studio labour and operations while also demonstrating how ideas about filmmaking circulated in contemporary British film culture.

  6. The Last of the Pirates: Contesting Videotape Screening Right in Taiwan in the Late Cold War
    Raymond Kun-Xian Shen · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-09-24
    摘要

    This article examines late Cold War Taiwan as an underexplored site of copyright conflict, where videotape piracy became a site of contested sovereignty, media governance, and neoliberal transition. Focusing on disputes over videotape screening rights in the 1980s and early 1990s, it analyzes how Taiwanese video businesses clashed with Hollywood studios and U.S. trade representatives over the definition of ‘public screening’ and ‘video piracy.’ Rather than treating piracy as a deviation, the article understands it as the outcome of Taiwan’s hybrid political economy of party-state capitalism, authoritarian deregulation, and post–Martial Law liberalisation. It argues that the government’s failure to recognise videotape’s medium specificity produced an unstable media environment in which informal viewing practices initially flourished but were later sacrificed to corporate consolidation and U.S. pressure. Particular attention is given to MTV (Movie Television) audiovisual centres, semi-private screening spaces that operated first legally yet were later criminalised through a redefinition of ‘public screening.’ Recasting these ‘pirates’ as casualties of geopolitical realignment rather than violators of legal norms, the article reframes videotape piracy as a historically contingent outcome of Cold War client politics and uneven globalisation, challenging universalist accounts of intellectual property and foregrounding suppressed alternative viewing cultures in Taiwan and beyond.

  9. Fifty Years of Reviewing Films About Homosexuality: Discursive Shifts in the Moral Evaluations Within the Belgian Catholic Film Periodical Film en Televisie (1956–2005)
    Frederik Dhaenens, Mendel Missorten · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-09-12
    摘要

    In twentieth-century Western Europe, film periodicals played an important role in people’s lives and their decision-making process regarding film attendance. This symbolic power was recognised early on by the Belgian Catholic Film League, resulting in the foundation of the monthly periodical Film en Televisie. The periodical published the moral ratings issued by the Catholic Board of Film Classification and film reviews. This article turns to reviews of films about homosexuality and explores how reviewers evaluated these films and to what extent their moral evaluations changed throughout the twentieth century. Drawing on the results from a historical discourse analysis of 174 reviews, spanning fifty years (1956–2005), we observed multiple discursive shifts. Although moral condemnation, practices of othering, and heteronormative lenses to discuss homosexuality were present, we also observed neutral and inclusive discourses early on, contradicting the Catholic Church’s prevailing views on homosexuality.

International Journal of Cultural Studies

Vol. 29, No. 6 · 2026-11 · 13 篇 · SAGE · Media/Culture
  1. Beyond the cards: Exploring youth motives and practices in Moscow's tarot culture
    Polina Afanasyeva, Anna Dayneko, Yulia Efremova, Alexandra Malneva, Polina Sergeeva, Oxana Mikhaylova · pp. 961-983 · online 2026-05-30
    摘要

    This study uncovers why tarot has become an integral—and often surprising—part of everyday life for Moscow's youth. Using a mixed-methods approach combining content analysis of 15,530 YouTube comments with in-depth interviews of 21 young tarot users, we show that tarot's allure stems less from magical beliefs and more from psychological, social, hedonistic, and aesthetic motives. The findings challenge stereotypes of ritualized esotericism: most youth view tarot as entertainment, emotional comfort, and a tool for community-building and self-understanding rather than mystical pursuit. Our analysis highlights online tarot practices’ dominance and identifies a novel aesthetic motive—fascination with ritual atmosphere and visual appeal distinctive to this generation. These insights argue that tarot's vitality in secular urban Russia derives precisely from its de-magicification: stripped of supernatural claims, it functions as a portable meaning-making system meeting psychological, social, and aesthetic needs simultaneously.

  2. Voice for justice: Hybrid cultural resistance of hip-hop songs and memes in July mass uprising, Bangladesh
    Tareq Hossain · pp. 984-1002 · online 2026-05-22
    摘要

    This study investigates how hip-hop songs and memes operated as cultural and political alternative expressions during the July 2024 mass uprising in Bangladesh. The student-led movement, initially focused on quota fairness and accountability, evolved into the Chattro-Jonata Obbhothan (Student-People Uprising) after widespread state violence and the killing of over a thousand protesters. Using a qualitative interpretive approach that combines textual and visual analysis, the study examines 10 hip-hop songs and 77 memes from five meme groups shared across Facebook and YouTube. The findings reveal that these cultural forms served as tools of hybrid cultural resistance , merging digital creativity with street activism, and as mechanisms for constructing collective identity and solidarity among protesters. The study argues that hip-hop and memes transformed everyday digital practices into critical political participation, challenging authoritarian control and inspiring civic engagement in protest.

  3. Songs of resistance and identity: The role of Telangana folk songs in political mobilization and cultural memory
    Mekala Chiranjeevi, Ritu Sharma · pp. 1003-1021 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    This study explores how the Telangana folk songs were used as a launch pad in political agitation, cultural memory, and statehood shaping of identities in the period (1998–2014). It uses ethnographic fieldwork, interviews with performers, and study of lyrics as it tracks the growth of oral tradition that started in rural communities into the interface of digital worlds from mobile media to YouTube. The results reveal how these songs gave expression to marginal voices, how they rallied the grassroots into activism, and how they bridged the continuity of cultural tradition with the latest technology into a voice of rhythm-based resistance. Unlike supplying a background information to the protests, folk songs were used as a part of the active construction of the discourse, symbolism, and consciousness of the movement. Drawing on analytical work at the juncture of folklore, performance, and digital humanities, this scholarship shows the ways regional Indian oral traditions adapt to new media ecologies as part of maintaining political agency. It introduces a paradigm of intersection between storytelling, technology, and grassroots activism, in postcolonial contexts.

  4. Who gets to be independent?: Labelling and boundary work of China's indie game scene
    Haoxuan Xiong · pp. 1022-1039 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork and interviews, this article examines independence as a contested label sustained through boundary work rather than a fixed structural condition. In the absence of formal institutional definitions, developers mobilize the indie label to signal mutual recognition, draw moral boundaries against commercial logics, and negotiate what counts as legitimate participation. This boundary work unfolds across three registers: scene-building identity, institutionally entangled ‘relative independence’, and culturally situated expression within transnational markets. By analysing how labelling and boundary-making operate in China's hybrid production ecology – where platformization, state-backed infrastructures, and cultural governance overlap – the study contributes to game production research by shifting attention from classifications to the communicative practices through which indie autonomy is enacted and legitimized.

  5. Governing diversity through AI: DEI and procedural trust in public service media
    Sarita Malik, Sukhmani Khorana, Tanja Dreher · pp. 1040-1061 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    Public service media are adopting artificial intelligence at a moment when diversity, equity and inclusion policies are increasingly politicised. Drawing on comparative qualitative research in the United Kingdom and Australia, this article examines how AI is embedded within Public Service Media governance and platformised audience infrastructures. We argue that AI operates not merely as a technical tool but as a cultural and institutional logic through which diversity, risk and legitimacy are reconfigured. In this shift, trust in diversity moves from relational practices grounded in representation and accountability toward procedural confidence embedded in automated systems, standards and compliance regimes. We conceptualise this transformation as the rise of procedural trust : a reorganisation of institutional legitimacy through technical infrastructures rather than social relations. Our thematic analysis shows how platform capitalism reshapes public service media by depoliticising diversity and redistributing responsibility across governance systems, with significant implications for democratic legitimacy.

  6. The cultural trend driven by short videos: Cross-cultural narratives and global audience participation
    Yu Liang · pp. 1062-1083 · online 2026-08-04
    摘要

    This study explores how short-video platforms reshape global cultural dissemination through cross-cultural narrative mechanisms. Analyzing over 5 million video metadata entries from Kaggle, We Are Social, and Harvard Dataverse (2020–2024), this mixed-methods research employs thematic modeling, time-series analysis, and comparative analysis. The study proposes “algorithm-mediated cross-cultural narratives”—a framework where cultural content transcends linguistic and geographical boundaries through platform algorithms while preserving cultural distinctiveness. Findings reveal three interacting dimensions: algorithmic recommendation mechanisms, visual translation strategies, and meme propagation patterns that form short-video platforms’ unique cultural dissemination system. Global audiences function as cultural production agents through hierarchical participation and cross-cultural co-creation. Mainstream platforms establish a “global framework-local expression” model through differentiated strategies. The research demonstrates that short-video era cultural transmission exhibits fluidity, participation, and hybridity, providing theoretical insights for digital age cultural transformation and empirical evidence for policy and industry development.

  7. Interpretive incompleteness in global cultural flows: Ne Zha 2 and Labubu between visibility and decentralization
    Sizhou Pan · pp. 1084-1108 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    This study examines the structural asymmetry between cultural visibility and interpretive power. Through a critical discourse analysis of 349 media articles focusing on two Chinese cultural icons— Ne Zha 2 and Labubu —the study identifies a layered discursive defense system with selective visibility as its filtering precondition, followed by three core strategies: economic narrative strategies that erase cultural subjecthood, political narrative strategies that construct symbolic boundaries, and security narrative strategies that recode popularity as social risk. These layers operate as a structured mechanism of selection, evaluation, and management, collectively ensuring that Chinese icons remain subordinate to Western epistemic frameworks despite their market visibility. The study introduces the concept of interpretive incompleteness as a structural condition of global cultural flows, emphasizing that incomplete understanding persists as an inherent feature of cultural exchange, where cultural plurality is preserved through persistent negotiation and co-presence amid asymmetry.

  8. From page to screen: A comparative analysis of Ghassan Kanafani's Men in the Sun and Tawfiq Saleh's The Dupes
    Asma Hussein · pp. 1109-1125 · online 2026-06-29
    摘要

    This paper examines the adaptation of Kanafani's seminal novella Men in the Sun into Saleh's The Dupes (also known as The Duped and The Deceived ). It explores how this transformation walks the line between literary symbolism and cinematic realism, all while preserving the work's profound political critique. The significance of this case study transcends aesthetic considerations to encompass broader questions about how narratives of Palestinian identity, displacement, and exile translate across media in the context of the Arab cinema's evolution. Transforming Kanafani's sparse, symbolically dense prose into Saleh's visually expansive cinematic interpretation raises fundamental questions about fidelity, creative transformation, and the role of adaptation in shaping collective memory.

  9. The affective epistemic labor of trans people: Sharing knowledge in media spaces
    Mona Heimonen, Mervi Pantti · pp. 1126-1143 · online 2026-07-10
    摘要

    The global anti-trans backlash, which seeks to erase knowledge about trans lives and perpetuates epistemic violence, is well-documented. This article examines trans people's efforts to counter epistemic violence by analyzing their experiences of sharing trans knowledge in the media . We propose the concept of affective epistemic labor , which encompasses the emotional resources required to communicate alternative knowledge claims and the communicative strategies that leverage emotions to resist dehumanization. We demonstrate that a sense of responsibility to counter epistemic violence compels trans individuals to act as representatives in the media and share experiential knowledge as an act of solidarity, with the hope of making life easier for others, particularly for trans and queer youth. While trans individuals employ empathy and openness in their communicative practice to foster cisgender solidarity, this article also highlights the limitations of such virtuous epistemic practices in media spaces where transphobic hostility is widespread.

  10. The AI-mediated gamification of affective labor: The reconfiguration of participatory fan production in human–community–machine interactions
    Jie Cui · pp. 1144-1163 · online 2026-05-08
    摘要

    This study examines the life-cycle of LiPu, a Chinese ACGN (Anime, Comics, Games, and Novels) AI-powered fandom platform that integrates AI-generated fan creation and automated chatbots, to analyze the reconfiguration of participatory fan production in human–community–machine interactions. Employing the walkthrough method and interviews, this study introduces the concept of “AI-mediated gamification of affective labor” (AGAL) which encompasses the gamification of fan creation into a memetic replication of AI-generated content templates (content creation), the quantification and incentivization of emotional investment via algorithmic governance (labor management), and the simulation of reciprocal parasociality to sustain engagement (engagement sustainability). The AGAL reveals a shift in fan production, from platforms that manage fan activities to platforms that generate the very content and relational fabric of those activities, enabling governance not only through algorithmic extension but also through generative infrastructure. This study offers an account of early possibilities, user practices, and structural challenges in this emerging field.

  11. More than selling food: The affective labor of rural and small cities female creators in China
    Yuqi Yang · pp. 1164-1181 · online 2026-07-24
    摘要

    This paper draws on semi-structured interviews with 12 rural and small-city-based women creators on the short video and livestreaming platform Kuaishou to examine how these women actively transform unpaid domestic labor into a double form of creator work: affective labor performed through food-related content to build and sustain audience relationships and material-commercial labor enacted through offline street food vending as micro-entrepreneurship. The paper argues that the conditions motivating these women to create, including platform and state opportunities, domestic skills, and the need to claim recognition inside and beyond the home, also reproduce the precarity they navigate, as platform algorithmic uncertainty, the undervaluation of affective labor, persistent family expectations, and gendered harassment remain constant features of their creator lives. Through three dimensions of analysis, “strategic money-making,” “recognition and sisterhood,” and “the navigation of authenticity, public–private boundaries, and gendered harassment,” the paper shows that these women generate real income, social presence, and communities of solidarity while exercising agency that remains contingent and precarious. The paper contributes to feminist labor theory by moving beyond extraction frameworks, and to creator studies by showing that offline micro-entrepreneurship and online affective labor are mutually sustaining, bringing these frameworks into dialogue with the specific conditions of platform labor in China.

  12. Affective (non)sense-making: Digital nonsense, precarity, and the affective rhetoric of “Crazy Literature”
    Zixuan Zhang, Jingxuan Liu · pp. 1182-1204 · online 2026-06-29
    摘要

    This study investigates “Crazy Literature” (Fāfēng Wénxué 发疯文学) as a form of digital nonsense through which young people engage in affective sense-making about work-related precarity in China. Emerging from earlier E’gao spoof culture and subaltern tropes such as Diaosi and Sang Culture, Crazy Literature marks a shift from ironic self-deprecation of marginalized youth toward an active nihilism of an emerging precariat class often known as Dagongren (wage laborer) or Niuma (cow and horse) in the workplace. Drawing on affective rhetoric, this study tracks the circulation of work-related Crazy Literature across popular online platforms, showing how absurd language, schematic figures, and routinized performances materialize precarity and stabilize an ambient “craziness” that renders precarity an affective mode of self-making for youth in the workplace. Through circulation and commercial recuperation, “Crazy Literature” transforms venting into everyday affective resilience, turning “nonsense” into participatory and routinized forms of livability under precarity.

  3. Film Songs and the Split Theory of Soviet Cinema
    Maria Belodubrovskaya · pp. 10-33
    摘要

    This article uses a cultural-studies framework to revisit Soviet film songs. Songs in Stalin-era cinema presented in two types: those containing explicit propaganda were bracketed by film plots, lacked record releases, and are completely forgotten today; other, more popular songs enhanced storytelling, were mass consumed, and continue to circulate to this day. These functional divergences suggest that rather than negotiating appeals or serving as sites of indoctrination and resistance, each type of song satisfied different audience needs, making it easier to split the personal from the political.

  4. Labor Unions and Pink-Collar Ghettos: A Feminist Production History of 9 to 5
    Maureen E. Ryan · pp. 34-55
    摘要

    This article offers a feminist production history of the workplace comedy 9 to 5 (Colin Higgins, 1980). Drawing on archival documents, interviews, and historical research on feminist organizing in the 1970s, it shows how struggles over commercial palatability, directorial sensibility, and workplace expectations all collided, resulting in a clash between the visions of actor Jane Fonda, screenwriter Patricia Resnick, and director Colin Higgins. Higgins’s camp style simultaneously rolled back the feminism of Resnick’s script and made room for ambivalent forms of queer pleasure. As a result, the film embodies the contradictory desire to both articulate feminist, pro-labor ideals and contain their radical disruptions.

  5. Urbanized Functionality in the Production of Animated Films: Czechoslovak and Polish Film Studios, 1945–1960
    Pavel Skopal, Ewa Ciszewska, Michal Večeřa · pp. 56-76
    摘要

    The nationalized production of animated film in Czechoslovakia and Poland during the period from 1945 to 1960 was underfinanced because of postwar shortages. The comparative research presented in this article reveals the strategies applied by these two state-socialist film industries in confronting postwar contingencies: Animation’s spatial organization was typically adapted to preexisting spaces rather than being accommodated in purpose-built facilities tailored to its specific production needs. This reaction to the challenge, which we term “urbanized functionality,” outweighed the more costly alternative of integration into non-animation production complexes. This article analyzes the process of urbanization for animation studios and identifies its implications for animation studios’ arrangements.

  6. A Tree Is a Tree: Plants, Production Design, and the Studio Nurseries
    Graig Uhlin · pp. 77-99
    摘要

    Greenswork involves the provision of living and artificial plants for a film’s production design. This article documents the establishment of Greens Departments and plant nurseries at the major Hollywood studios, which were tasked with storing and growing trees, shrubs, and flowers as needed. It then considers how classical-era greenswork approached standards of verisimilitude, as it learned to navigate audiences’ expectations about realism for onscreen greenery. Greenswork also had to contend with the fragility of vegetation, which routinely wilted under the hot studio lights. Finally, I address how greenswork managed unruly vegetal matter either by using artificial substitutes or through physiochemical modification.

  7. Framing the Gaze of the Snow Leopard: Pema Tseden’s Filmic Gift of Fearlessness
    Yuqing Yang · pp. 100-119
    摘要

    Pema Tseden’s film Xuebao ( Snow Leopard , 2023) is a part of and a reflection on animal-themed visual culture. Set in the Sanjiangyuan nature reserve in China’s Qinghai Province, it follows a dispute over the treatment of a snow leopard confined in a sheep corral after harming a Tibetan herding family’s livestock. The film explores how animals are framed through diegetic cameras operated by journalists, residents, and forestry police. In establishing an equality of gazes between a human character and the CGI-rendered snow leopard, it offers an ecological perspective on the Buddhist concept of dāna , the gift of freeing living beings from fear.

  8. Spatial Performance of Socialist Memories: Space, Aesthetics, and Temporalities in Jia Zhangke’s 24 City
    Zhen Zhang · pp. 120-140
    摘要

    In Jia Zhangke’s Ershisi chengji ( 24 City , 2008), the director narrates the transformation and gradual disappearance of a typical socialist space in China, the factory complex, in the post-Mao era. This article approaches this issue from the perspective of critical social theory of space and historical reappraisal of the socialist space in actual socialist countries before undergoing an analysis of what I call the spatial performance of socialist memories in Jia Zhangke’s film aesthetics. The film reconstructs a narrative of socialist space that not only salvages real socialist living experiences from the erasing power of developmentalism but also creates a spatial commemoration for alternative futures of the working class in China.

  1. Introduction: A Curious and Profound Legacy: Powell and Pressburger’s A Canterbury Tale
    Eddie McMillan · pp. 297-305
  2. The Making of a Modern Magus: From Colpeper to Prospero Reimagined
    Ian Christie · pp. 306-318
    摘要

    The article explores the idea of Thomas Colpeper as a magus figure – one who anticipates other such figures not only in the Powell and Pressburger canon, but also in Powell's solo projects such as his film of Bartok's opera Bluebeard's Castle and his long-planned version of The Tempest. It also stresses how clearly crucial was Eric Portman to the portrayal of the strangely ambivalent Colpeper: an actor with an almost sinister reserve, a potential ruthlessness hidden behind an urbane exterior of measured calmness, and one who had an unusual willingness to play disturbingly perverse figures in other films of the 1940s. The article notes that Colpeper bears a strong resemblance to the wounded or damaged males identified by Edmund Wilson in his influential history of Late Romantic and Symbolist literature, Axel's Castle, figures who recur strikingly frequently in The Archers’ work, as well as in films by Powell without Pressburger.

  3. ‘We Modern Pilgrims See No Journey’s End’
    John Ellis · pp. 319-331
    摘要

    This article analyses the address of A Canterbury Tale (1944) to the transformation of rural England during the Second World War; the pressures of wartime agricultural policies; and conflicting views of nature between the instrumentalisation of nature and an emerging ecological and spiritual vision. It explores the film’s depiction of the countryside as both a site of spiritual renewal and practical labour. It also emphasises the importance of camaraderie and friendship over romance, and the active roles of women in the rural workforce. Through its characters and narrative structure, the film dramatises the uncertainties and hopes of wartime Britain, reflecting the debates about the fundamental values of the post-war world that are still relevant to the present day.

  4. Messages from Canterbury
    Julian Petley · pp. 332-352
    摘要

    This article examines A Canterbury Tale in the context of other works from the inter-war and wartime period which invoke in one way or another the idea of the pilgrimage to Canterbury. These range from G. K. Chesterton's study of Chaucer, which argues that a modern-day version of The Canterbury Tales would be impossible because of the disappearance of shared Christian belief, to a visit to Canterbury by H. V. Morton in I Saw Two Englands, which reaches a rather different conclusion, and finally to the British Council film Message from Canterbury in which ‘the camera comes like a pilgrim’ to Canterbury and its Cathedral in the wake of the air raid of June 1942. Except in the case of the Chesterton study, the article focuses in particular on the way in which the works with which it is concerned, and especially A Canterbury Tale, represent the rural. It concludes that Powell and Pressburger's film expresses a form of romantic-conservative pantheism in this respect, whereas Message from Canterbury, although its imagery is frequently rural, is actually rather less mystical and more forward-looking. This is particularly the case in the closing part of the Archbishop of Canterbury's sermon, in which the countryside is discussed in the context of post-war agricultural development and from a specifically Christian socialist perspective.

  5. ‘We Speak the Same Language’: A Canterbury Tale , Hollywood and Anglo-American Relations
    Gregory Semenza, Garrett Sullivan Jr · pp. 353-376
    摘要

    According to Michael Powell, A Canterbury Tale was made to address fraying wartime relations between Britain and America by appealing to the two nations’ shared cultural values. This article, which examines the film alongside production materials held by the British Film Institute, demonstrates how Powell and Pressburger’s project was also advanced by way of its allusive engagement with specific Hollywood films and genres. Through its intertextual and metacinematic elements, the film advocates for a mode of viewing that it associates with the Anglo-American values Powell and Pressburger were seeking to inculcate in their audience. The article also examines how, in the wake of the film’s lukewarm domestic response, the 1949 American cut of A Canterbury Tale qualifies The Archers’ aspirations for it. While the US version maintains the original’s emphasis on transforming how moviegoers see the world, it expresses scepticism about how frequently such change can occur.

  2. Water on cross-border screens, Hong Kong water tales from the 1960s
    Zimu Zhang · pp. 6-24 · online 2025-06-20
    摘要

    This paper examines the massive engineering of water, mediated through screen practices, in the 1960s Hong Kong-Guangdong borderscape. Facing severe water scarcity, both the communist Chinese government and the British Hong Kong colonial government launched large-scale water infrastructures as civil remedies and political tools, leaving lasting ecological, political, and cultural impacts on Hong Kong and the Pearl River Delta. Focusing on two 1960s documentaries with competing ideologies—Water Comes over the Hills from the East (1965, pro-Beijing leftist studios) and Plover Cove Reservoir (1967, British colonial newsreels)—I explore how film mechanisms serve as political, cultural, and aesthetic practices of ‘environing’ during the water crisis. I analyze how water infrastructures are (re)constructed on screen, how water narratives intersect with social metabolism and collective identities, and how water is reimagined alongside the control of more-than-human elements in cinematic environing. While both films are often dismissed as propaganda, this article argues that a reading of cinematic environing, contextualized within Cold War dynamics, offers new insights into the energy narratives of southern China during this pivotal period. It also highlights how vital water resources are mediated on screen, revealing the interplay between ideology, technology and film aesthetics.

  3. Small hydropower on and off screen: Mediating rural energy regimes in the People’s Republic of China (1958–2023)
    Hongyun Lyu · pp. 25-47 · online 2025-08-31
    摘要

    This paper focuses on the production and circulation of two films, The Young People in Our Village (1959) and its sequel (1963), to explore the mutual production of media and energy in socialist and post-socialist China. It first examines how The Young People was shot in rural Shanxi during the Great Leap Forward and how it presented humans’ ambition of taming water. It then details how the sequel was made in the post-famine years. The third section analyzes how the representations of small hydropower were changed and shaped by socialist politics. The last section discusses the circulation and interpretations of the two films that enabled the transition of energy aesthetics from revolutionary to sustainable. By showing the production and circulation of two films, I argue that small hydropower energized socialist cinematic production and circulation, and film, in turn, was involved in China’s energy transitions, powering the state to project its electricscape under socialism and embrace the global sustainability discourses in the twenty-first century. This paper shows how media and energy were fabricated and re-fabricated by China’s political economy and global sustainability concerns, and contributes to the current discussions on energy humanities, shifting the focus from oil to electricity and from capitalism to socialism.

  4. The Little Sun and cinema as energy in Chinese science education filmmaking, 1950–1964
    Muyang Zhuang · pp. 48-65 · online 2025-10
    摘要

    This article examines the science education film (kejiaopian) in early PRC (1950–1964). Before the state-owned filmmaking system collapsed in the 1990s, kejiaopian served as tools for disseminating scientific knowledge and official ideology. While most of the current scholarly discussions have indicated the role played by kejiaopian in the construction of the socialist state, this article explores how kejiaopian developed amidst the Great Leap Forward, portraying the interactions between politics, cinema, science, and energy. This article mainly analyzes a 1963 science education film titled The Little Sun, which cinematizes the representation of energy and anticipates socialist efforts to harness solar and other types of energy for state building. In addition, the film’s diverse techniques, including animated effects, stop-motion, and special filming, align with Thomas Elsaesser’s proposition that illuminates the inherent cinematic energy. This article argues that The Little Sun not only cinematizes socialist energy culture but also embodies cinema as energy in early PRC.

  5. Somatic intermediality: Bodies, environment and population policies in Chinese children’s science fiction films (1988–1992)
    Ziyang Li · pp. 66-85 · online 2025-10-07
    摘要

    This paper combines media studies and ecocriticism to analyze the narratives of children’s transcorporeal connections with energy and pollutants in the science fiction films Wonder Boy, Kiss of Poison and other related works. Contextualizing these films within the transitional period from the 1980s to the early 1990s, the paper explores how children’s bodies serve as contested sites where environmental anxieties and state concerns over population management converge. The state’s concept of ‘population quality’ framed the body as a vessel for biological optimization and progress. In parallel, the resurgence of somatic science in the 1980s emphasized the body’s extrasensory potential, blending ultra-scientific and paranormal powers with the informatic fantasy. Situating children’s bodies within both natural and electromagnetic environments, the paper theorizes a conceptual framework of ‘somatic intermediality’ to reconsider children’s bodies as an eco-medium. Children’s bodies, caught between the pregnancy trope as the idealized, high-quality body and the narrative of environmental vulnerability, become a biosemiotic index of China’s evolving strategies. These strategies intertwine population control, cybernetics, environmental concerns and scientism, reflecting a shifting socio-political landscape during the 1980s and 1990s.
