  1. From Dan to Musculinary Nüchou : Tam Lan-hing’s comic role from Cantonese Opera to Hong Kong Film
    Wanqiao Chen · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    Tam Lan-hing’s career is defined by her physical transformation from Dan (female lead) to Nüchou (female comic), which must be understood as a radical, non-assimilationist trans (Wang Citation2013). By studying three key films, Scent of Wild Flowers (1935), Fatso Married Skinny (1956), and My Kingdom for a Husband (a.k.a. The Romance of Jade Hall (Part One)) (1957), this article contends that Tam actively re-signified her non-normative body. The rigid aesthetic hierarchy of Cantonese opera, which mandated slenderness for the Dan, excluded Tam after her weight gain in the 1950s. This ‘gendered necessity’ forced her into the realm of comedy. Comedy, as such, becomes a trans zone to legitimate Tam’s transgressive body. To overcome the constraints of the passive Nüchou stereotype, Tam developed a ‘musculinary’ performance strategy. This strategy re-signified her fatness as ‘fierce, tough’ dominance, a ‘foreignising translation’ that contrasts starkly with the ‘soft, feminised Anima’ trope of male fat Cantonese film comics like Leung Sing-por. Situating Tam’s performance within the traditions of Cantonese opera (Dan vs. Chou) and regional fat-thin duos, this article illuminates how Tam’s ‘musculinary’ trans disrupted gendered hierarchies and redefined the meaning of comic corpulence in Hong Kong.

  2. Supplement and support: Elaine Jin, the ‘queen of supporting stars’
    Louis Lo · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    This article examines the supporting stardom of Elaine Jin in the Hong Kong New Wave and Taiwan New Cinema. While Jin is appreciated by a wide spectrum of Chinese-language filmmakers, I focus on films directed by Stanley Kwan and Edward Yang, investigating the extent to which her involvement shapes their work. By blending Jin’s supporting characters, her persona, and her stardom, I theorise the functions of supporting characters to show that the supporting actress is more important than previously thought. I will: (1) demonstrate how the hierarchy of foreground (narrative made possible by the leading roles) and background (supporting actors) is ‘deconstructed’ by focusing on the role of the supporting actress; (2) enlist Derrida’s concepts of the supplement and the parergon to show that supporting characters supply what is missing in the centre, and that the supporting actress serves as the parergon, a seemingly unimportant margin to the artwork but that which makes the artwork possible; and (3) examine Jin’s various mother figures in her career. Drawing on these aspects of Jin’s legacy, this paper creates a new framework for understanding supporting roles as a critical concept in general and serves as a prime example to re-examine the Hong Kong New Wave and Taiwan New Cinema in particular.

  3. Desire, judgment, and burdened agency: Female knight-errant subjectivity and production formation in three contemporary Chinese Wuxia films
    Yidan Ding, Anhua Zhou, Yichen Xiao · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-09-20
    摘要

    This article contributes to feminist wuxia studies by asking how contemporary Chinese wuxia films make women legible not only as fighters but as ethical subjects. Comparing Ang Lee’s Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon (2000), Hou Hsiao-hsien’s The Assassin (2015), and Yuen Woo-ping’s Blades of the Guardians (2026), it proposes a comparative vocabulary of desire, judgment, and burdened agency. Feminist film theory and narrative ethics serve as conceptual supports, Chinese cinemas as the comparative field, and production formation as a contextual heuristic rather than a causal explanation. The three films are read as a transnational prestige blockbuster, an auteur art film, and an industrial comic-book adaptation that condition how female agency becomes narratively thinkable. Through close analysis of movement, thresholds, off-screen space, tempo, convoy structure, martial transmission, kinship, apprenticeship, obligation, and aftermath, the essay argues that xianü subjectivity should not be reduced to empowerment, spectacle, mobility, punishment, or patriarchal containment. Women become ethically central when desire and judgment are tied to consequences that cannot be transferred to male heroes or institutions. The article thus offers a portable comparative vocabulary for understanding how contemporary wuxia makes female ethical agency legible through desire, judgment, and the bearing of ethical consequences.

  4. Classical fetishism and phantom flesh: Li Han-hsiang and the transregional grammar of Chinese erotica
    Ruoyi Bian · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-09-17
    摘要

    This article examines how Li Han-hsiang’s 1970s fengyue (‘wind and moon’) films at Shaw Brothers fashioned a transregional grammar of Chinese-language erotica. Within an East and Southeast Asian media ecology shaped by expanding erotic-film circulation and incompatible regimes of censorship and exhibition, Li organized sexual visibility without relying primarily on anatomical exposure. I conceptualize this grammar through two related but distinct operations. Classical fetishism distributes erotic action across historically coded bodies, objects, and spaces; phantom flesh assembles an apparently continuous character body from a star’s face, a substitute’s exposed flesh, obstructed framings, and editing. Shaw Brothers’ network made this grammar mobile, while Li’s formal organization gave the films a recognizable erotic and cultural identity under uneven conditions of visibility. A comparison with Mou Tun-fei’s sustained corporeal continuity and direct bodily impact shows that Li’s approach was not an automatic effect of censorship or studio production. The article finally considers the grammar’s reuse and transformation in Category III erotic cinema and its formal resonance in Raise the Red Lantern. Li’s historical significance lies in making the shifting relations between flesh and object, exposure and obstruction, and star and substitute the primary site of erotic meaning.

  5. Genre as cultural deployment: Rethinking genre formation in 1920s Chinese cinema
    Baoqi Liu · pp. 1-29 · online 2026-09-15
    摘要

    This article examines the formation of film genre in Chinese cinema of the 1920s. Existing scholarship has often treated genre either as a product of industrial standardisation or as an expression of cultural tradition. Bringing these dimensions together, the article argues that genre formation in 1920s Chinese cinema emerged through the selective deployment of culturally embedded narrative resources as an industrial strategy under conditions of market uncertainty. Drawing on Rick Altman’s semantic/syntactic/pragmatic model and Steve Neale’s emphasis on institutional context, the article introduces the concept of a ‘shared cultural repertoire’ to describe the circulation of recognisable narrative forms across literature, theatre, and film. This repertoire did not produce interpretive consensus, but functioned as a condition of intelligibility that enabled films to reach heterogeneous audiences. Through three case studies—Orphan Rescues Grandfather (1923), Romance of the Western Chamber (1927), and Red Heroine (1929)—the article identifies distinct mechanisms of genre formation, including ethical narrative structuring, intermedial visual systems, and serialised production. Together, these cases demonstrate that genre in 1920s Chinese cinema developed through the strategic mobilisation of cultural resources within industrial practice.

  6. Interview with film director and producer Mi-Mi Lee
    S. Louisa Wei, Tze-lan Sang · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-09-15
    摘要

    In this interview, Taiwanese pioneer woman director Mi-Mi Lee reflects on her career in film and television. She provides insight into the development of her distinctive style—marked by simple yet bold compositions, smooth camera transitions, and portrayals of strong, complex female characters. Her films often depict a kind of ‘female utopia,’ where women confront patriarchal pressures together. Girls’ School (1982), Unmarried Mothers (1980), and Evening News (1981) form what we call the Taiwan Urban Women Trilogy—relatively early examples of women’s cinema in the Sinosphere. Shot from a female perspective, these films explore women’s struggles and offer ensemble portraits of female experience. All three were produced by Lee’s Hong Kong–registered company, Chien Kee, with funding chiefly from the pre-sale of screening rights in Singapore and Malaysia. Though credited only as director, Lee also oversaw the scripts and took on executive production duties. Lee also discusses her multifaceted work in television, especially her adaptations of works by Taiwanese and Hong Kong women authors, her venture into documentary making, and her later projects in mainland China, which she pursued despite initially encountering prejudices.

  7. Herstoriographical (im)possibility: An illumination of the documented and printed first Chinese actress Lily Yim
    Gabriel F. Y. Tsang · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-27
    摘要

    Lily Yim Shan Shan (1896–1952), originally named Yim Shuk Kei, is widely recognised as the first Chinese actress after playing the role of the maid in Lai Man Wai’s Zhuangzi Tests His Wife (1913). She has received surprisingly insufficient scholarly attention and first-hand studies for two reasons: (1) her fame is shadowed by that of Lai, who is both her husband and the ‘Father of Hong Kong Cinema’, and (2) only few printed records can assist recovery of her biography. Based on visual-textual analysis, this article has four core arguments. First, the remaining male private records could only preserve subsidiary and typical female images of Lily Yim as a wife, a helper, a follower, and so on. Second, the images of this silent film actress were homogeneously reproduced, creating both ‘technologised visuality’ (borrowed from Rey Chow 1995) and limited visuality (a still decent woman with traditional dressing) towards the readers. Third, the entertainment-oriented presentation of Yim is framed by male and patriarchal simplification. Fourth, the printed media’s sole tracking of her fascinated side delimits herstoriographical recovery of her own voice.

  8. The Chinese railway journey: Sensory ethnography and the political aesthetics of speed
    Ziwei Chen · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-26
    摘要

    The article examines the political aesthetics of railway infrastructures in China through J.P. Sniadecki’s documentary The Iron Ministry (2014) and Chinese public responses to the 2011 Wenzhou high-speed rail accident. Analyzing the film’s sensorial portrayal of the train compartment, I argue that its vibrant, chaotic viewing experience produces a sensorium of slowness whose frictions and discomfort are the very material out of which a railway commons is built. The railway commons is a mode of collective life that, following Lauren Berlant, coheres through proximity rather than presumed belonging, disrupting the homogenized and sanitized experience of high-speed rail. Extending this analysis to the Wenzhou accident’s afterlife on Weibo in 2011, I challenge Susan Leigh Star’s classic claim that infrastructure becomes visible only upon breakdown: China’s high-speed rail is hyper-visible by design, so breakdown does not produce visibility but shifts its register from state-sanctioned spectacle to site of crisis, exposing the opaque bureaucratic apparatus whose contradictions become legible only when its smooth operation is interrupted. Attending to affects of grief, frustration, and hope across documentary and social media, I show how these sensory aesthetics unsettle the linear teleology of techno-modernity and gesture toward political socialities that sanitized infrastructure otherwise forecloses. The article thereby intervenes in infrastructure studies and film and media studies by theorizing a sensory aesthetic regime of contemporary China.

  9. From perfect wife to iron lady: Tong Yuejuan and Hong Kong nüxia films
    Huiyan Zhang · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-08-26
    摘要

    Tong Yuejuan, the co-founder of the Hsin Hwa Motion Picture Company, was an important figure in Chinese film history, although scholars have long overlooked her achievements. In fact, when her husband, Zhang Shankun, passed away in 1957, Tong took over Hsin Hwa. Over these decades, facing immense industrial pressure, like a shrinking Mandarin-language film market, Taiwan’s restrictive tax policies, and the development of Hong Kong film studios, she never gave up and managed the company until 1984 by herself. This article focuses on Tong’s nüxia (female knight-errant) films from the mid-twentieth century and argues that she thoroughly revamped the wuxia genre through a range of artistic and financial strategies. Her films engaged with Cold War politics by promoting values aligned with Taiwan’s nationalist discourse, thus sustaining Hsin Hwa’s survival within a tough market. Drawing on a research framework of ‘gender‑industry‑genre interconstruction’, this article highlights Tong’s combined role as a cultural innovator and a pragmatic producer. Her nüxia project not merely preserved independent filmmaking in Hong Kong but additionally inspired the aesthetic and narrative of new-school wuxia films of the late 1960s. Ultimately, Tong Yuejuan’s career works as a compelling example of female agency.

  10. Accenting Chinese cinema in Sinophone East Asia: Zhang Lü’s Yanagawa (2021) and transborder filmmaking
    Wenxian Zhang · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-08-26
    摘要

    This article examines Zhang Lü’s Yanagawa, the first film by the Korean-Chinese director to receive theatrical release in mainland China, as a critical intervention into prevailing frameworks of transnational Chinese cinema. The film has often been treated as evidence of Zhang’s return to national cinema after a long career shaped by South Korea, border-crossing production, and festival circulation. Against this narrative of return, the article argues that Yanagawa continues Zhang’s transborder aesthetics by turning transnationality inward, toward the linguistic, regional, and affective hierarchies that organize Chinese-speaking worlds. Building on debates in critical transnationalism, Sinophone studies, and accented cinema, I propose ‘accenting’ as a method for reading Zhang’s recent work: not simply as the mark of an exilic or diasporic author, but as a situated cinematic practice that makes dissonance, misalignment, and partial belonging perceptible. Through close analysis of Yanagawa’s treatment of Putonghua, Beijing speech, silence, translation, reflected imagery, and translocal space, the article shows how the film unsettles the opposition between national return and transnational mobility. Yanagawa is not merely a film about accent; it is a film that works as accent, registering the uneven politics of intelligibility within Sinophone East Asia.

  11. A cinema with feeling: Crises, sentiment, and the box office miracles in post-2019 Hong Kong
    Henry Kit Fung Chiu · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-08-17
    摘要

    Highlighting the role of film viewers, this article examines two recent top-grossing Hong Kong films to investigate the emergence of an evocative space that empowers audiences to reimagine Hong Kong—a cultural subjectivity that has been fading amid the city’s socioeconomic transformations over recent decades. Within this space, public voices are given room to exist on the fringes of power, beyond the dominance and control of any particular class or party. I argue that ‘sentiment’ functions as a transient means of expression, and facilitates the formation of spontaneous, pluralistic communities composed of individuals who are free to engage with and respond to change in subtle and metaphorical ways, especially in contexts where voices and expressions are heavily censored or silenced. This study aims to offer an alternative perspective from the viewer’s perception, in order to complement the industrial approach which is increasingly limited in its explanatory power.

  12. From ceramic materiality to animated visuality: Cross-media translation of traditional Chinese craft aesthetics
    Liyan Chen, Zeling Zheng, Bo Gao · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-08-10
    摘要

    This article examines how ceramic aesthetics are translated into the visual language of Chinese animation. Through close readings of The Porcelain Doll, Geng Xue’s Mr. Sea, and The Ways in Patterns: An Immersive Digital Exhibition from the Palace Museum, it traces a spectrum from material presence to digital dematerialization, arguing that this transformation constitutes cross-media aesthetic translation. Drawing on remediation theory and haptic visuality, it reveals how ceramic materiality undergoes a dynamic process of dematerialization and rematerialization through animation’s medium-specific properties. The central finding concerns four dimensions’ differential translatability: glaze and texture resist digitization but can achieve visual equivalents through haptic visuality; form involves dimensional compression; pattern translates most readily but risks losing cultural context. These findings illuminate a structural tendency in the digital transmission of traditional Chinese aesthetics: dimensions readily encodable as graphic data are systematically over-represented, while those dependent on materiality and tactility are marginalised, with implications for how digital media shapes the cultural memory of material arts.

  13. Publicizing peeping: Infrastructure, Milieu, and media culture in modern China, 1942–1949
    Rosalie Liu · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-03
    摘要

    This article examines the transformation of peepshows (yangpian) into ‘new peepshows’ (xin yangpian) by Chinese Communist propaganda artists from 1942 to 1949, revealing how this understudied medium challenges conventional understandings of propaganda as unidirectional communication. Through archival research, I trace how propaganda artists led by Shi Lu removed the peepholes and lenses characteristic of traditional peepshows, creating public exhibition environments and mobile distribution methods that fostered communal viewing experiences. Rather than viewing this transformation, or gaizao, as a linear progression from ‘old’ to ‘new’, I argue that the new peepshow embodied a dynamic process of constant adaptation—what Shi Lu theorized as ‘compounding’ (huahe)—where heterogeneous elements ranging from local dialects to meteorological conditions were continuously incorporated into the medium. Drawing on theorizations of milieu, I demonstrate how the new peepshow functioned as both medium and milieu, transforming audiences into socialist subjects while reconceptualizing borders through its distribution networks. The medium’s malleability enabled it to operate across diverse environments while transforming these spaces into pedagogical infrastructures. This study contributes to scholarship on medium/environment relationships and Chinese media archaeology, revealing how new peepshows generated productive rethinking of transformation, borders, and propaganda in late Republican China.

  14. The enframing of CGI in martial arts films: Action beyond the body
    Ziru Chen · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-07-23
    摘要

    This article examines how CGI operates as both a structuring and affective device within a strand of auteur-driven, transnational contemporary wuxia cinema, focusing on Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon (2000), Shadow (2018), and The Grandmaster (2013). Rather than treating digital effects primarily as spectacle or narrative markers, this article argues that CGI enframes wuxia action by organising the conditions under which movement becomes perceptually and culturally legible. Working alongside non-digital techniques, CGI connects production practice with spectatorship and narration by shaping trajectories and environmental traces into meaningful and narratively consequential forms. Through its integration with wirework, weaponry design, and environmental reinforcement, CGI shifts wuxia action from planar visibility towards volumetric legibility, transforming ­figure-ground composition into a dynamic field. By situating these techniques within the intermedial and transnational contexts of wuxia cinema, this article repositions CGI techniques as culturally located modes of configuring sovereignty that remap wuxia’s expressive and affective terrain.

  15. South China Urban Cinema: Polylocality and a satellite vernacular in 1980s Guangzhou
    Xu Hua · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-06-29
    摘要

    Current scholarship on 1980s Chinese mainland cinema has concentrated predominantly on the films about Northwestern rural China, while the diversification of cinematic localities, particularly urban representations, remains underexplored. This article calls for a polylocal reconfiguration of Chinese film historiography by investigating South China Urban Cinema, a series of films focusing on the urban context of Lingnan, produced by the Pearl River Film Studio in Guangzhou since the mid-1980s. Drawing on archival research encompassing film journals, magazines, and symposium records, it first situates South China Urban Cinema within the broader proliferation of regionally specific cinematic concepts advanced by state-owned studios in this period. Through analysis of Yamaha Fish Stall (1984), the article further contextualizes these films within Guangzhou’s urban and economic transformation, demonstrating how South China Urban Cinema and its street-level documentations functioned to shape new social norms and promote market reforms. Additionally, the article comparatively examines the presence of Hong Kong popular culture and characters in Yamaha Fish Stall and Swan Song (1985), to argue that South China Urban Cinema exemplifies what I term “satellite vernacular”, a mode which mediates the sensory experiences and critical reflections of modernity drawing from a translocal mediascape between Guangzhou and Hong Kong.

  16. When in Rome: Pai Ching-jui at the Centro Sperimentale di Cinematografia
    Matteo Cavelier Riccardi · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-06-26
    摘要

    Pai Ching-jui was famously the first Chinese-speaking graduate at the Italian state film academy in Rome, the Centro Sperimentale di Cinematografia (Csc), in 1963. Nonetheless, histories of Taiwanese cinema have yet to establish the impact of Pai’s studies in Italy on his career, nor have they taken into account that Pai arrived in Italy almost a decade after Italian neorealist cinema first took Taiwan by storm, to a dramatically transformed cinematic context. Using Pai’s Italy correspondent editorials, his Csc graduation short, ‘Un Amore’, and the interviews he gave and editorials he wrote about Italy upon returning to Taiwan, this article traces how Pai developed his notion of contemporary European cinema and how he planned to bring his discoveries back home to revolutionize Chinese-language cinema. In particular, it will uncover how Pai believed contemporary cinema was repudiating the ideal of verisimilitude and instead making cinema’s mechanisms more evident to spectators. Pai believed this conceptual shift in film storytelling was transforming the spectatorial experience and making cinema more integral to contemporary life.

  17. Monsters, viruses, and a zombie parliament: Taiwan’s trilogy of national calamity in the New Cold War
    Wu Yi Chun · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-06-25
    摘要

    This article examines three Taiwanese splatter and horror films—Mon Mon Mon Monsters (2017), The Sadness (2021), and Get the Hell Out (2020)—as a cinematic trilogy of national calamity emerging under the geopolitical conditions of the New Cold War. Drawing on psychoanalysis, phenomenology, monster theory, and political anthropology, it proposes the concept of calamity–aberration, an analytical framework rooted in Sinographic traditions of omen-reading and aligned with contemporary theories of democratic crisis and collective anxiety.Rather than treating disaster as a purely accidental or physical event, calamity–aberration designates a symbolic configuration in which ethical collapse, institutional dysfunction, and political deformation become legible through monsters, viral contagion, and zombies. The article argues that these films function as allegories of Taiwan’s internal crises while occupying a frontline position in U.S.–China confrontation.Specifically, Mon Mon Mon Monsters figures the abused monster as an internalized national Other, exposing unresolved historical betrayal and moral disintegration. The Sadness constructs a viral allegory in which democracy appears as a pathological experiment that unleashes violence and abjection. Get the Hell Out satirizes parliamentary zombification to reveal institutional paralysis and internal erosion. Together, these films diagnose Taiwan’s contemporary condition while prefiguring future democratic crises.

  18. Rewriting the Korean war: Re-monumental and counter-monumental cinema
    Feng Lan · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    This article offers a comparative examination of The Battle at Lake Changjin (China) and Taegukgi: Brotherhood of War (South Korea), two landmark blockbusters that exemplify a post-Cold War trend to reframe the Korean War for present national agendas, but pull in opposite aesthetic and ethical directions. Drawing on monumentality studies, this article argues that Lake Changjin re-monumentalizes Chinese war memory by revising China’s war justification and reconstructing a heroic national narrative, whereas Taegukgi operates as a counter-monumental intervention that contests the war’s ethical warrant and shifts attention from state purpose to personal trauma and human cost. These divergent commemorative strategies depart decisively from earlier Korean War films in their respective national traditions and establish distinct benchmarks for contemporary war filmmaking at home. Despite their differences, both productions are similarly conditioned by their respective sociopolitical contexts: Xi-era China’s promotion of state nationalism and Sunshine Policy-era South Korea’s embrace of ethnic nationalism. Combining historical inquiry with cinematic analysis, this study demonstrates how war cinema functions as a form of modern monumentality that, capable of rebuilding a nation’s pantheon or dismantling it, actively reshapes collective memory and political consciousness while remapping the moral-affective terrain upon which national identities are forged, contested, and renewed.

  19. Fractured localities: Imaginaries of the Kowloon Walled City in pre-handover Hong Kong cinema
    Rachel Ng · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    Before its demolition in 1993, the Kowloon Walled City was a hyper-dense squatters’ enclave within British Hong Kong that was de jure under Chinese sovereignty. Distinct from the dystopian, cyberpunk visions it has inspired internationally (e.g. Blade Runner [Ridley Scott, 1982] and Ghost in the Shell [Mamoru Oshii, 1995]), the Walled City has been depicted in more familiar terms in local film productions. This article examines the Walled City’s significant and perennial role in Hong Kong’s cinematic and cultural imaginaries as a site of locality, where ideas and feelings about the city as a home are explored, shaped, and questioned. Drawing on Arjun Appadurai’s theorisations on the production of locality and applying them to two pre-handover films, Lam Nai-choi’s Brothers from the Walled City (1982) and Johnny Mak’s Long Arm of the Law (1984), it develops the concept of the ‘Walled City imaginary’, where the enclave serves as a spatial construct for negotiating Hong Kong’s myriad sociopolitical contradictions and contestations, as well as ever-shifting ideas of what constitutes the ‘local’. Far from historical obsolescence, the Walled City and its cinematic imaginings continue to resonate amidst contemporary debates about local identity formation—arguably more than ever in post-2019 Hong Kong.

  20. Blue Island: Toward a democratic sociality through affective dissonance
    Victor Fan · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    In Jauwat zi dou (Youyu zhi dao in Mandarin or Blue Island, 2022), director Chan Tze-woon puts into dialogue four traumatic memories and experiences that were considered by many Hong Kongers as mutually incommensurable. In the initial screenings of the film, some viewers even found such comparison objectionable. This article is an outcome and an attempted ‘continuation’ of my conversation with Chan Tze-woon from 2023. Through a close reading of Blue Island, I argue that Chan Tze-woon uses the cinema to foster an opportunity for the dependent co-arising of individual subjectivities and a democratic sociality (a way in which a democratic society can be constituted). It does so through a process that has not been imaginable under Hong Kong’s historical conditions. If being a Hong Konger is a process of becoming that requires constant negotiations among individuals who need to work through mutually contesting traumas, Blue Island enables spectators to try appreciating their differences as sites of conversation and connection.

  21. Speed politics and socialist contradictions in Maoist documentary cinema
    Luyang Sun, Qiliang He · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-06-12
    摘要

    During the Great Leap Forward (1958–1960), Chinese cinema found itself trapped between heightened political pressures and a fervent pursuit of shortened production cycles, using exaggerated numerical information within its cinematographic language to represent extreme labor intensity and the dreamlike speed of historical development. Bridging sensory history and media discourse analysis, this study examines the “artistic documentary”(yishu xing jilupian), a hybrid genre weaponized to navigate this crisis. Through two foundational case studies, The Caprice of the Ming Tombs Reservoir (Shisanling Shuiku Changxiangqu, 1958) and Huang Baomei (1958), this article argues that cinema weaponized sensory stimulation to achieve the following functions: manufacturing political identification and mass subjectivity; simulating utopian futures to compel popular endurance of present bodily exhaustion and material scarcity; juxtaposing individual heroism with collective narratives to facilitate ideological integration, a synthesis that ultimately fractured as model workers were physically detached from the labor process; and projecting a socialist teleology wherein hyper-acceleration would paradoxically deliver human emancipation, an end to bodily alienation, and a “decelerated” future capable of mastering time itself.”

  22. Appropriating CinemaScope’s Screens: The Love of Susan ( Xin Yutangchun , 1954) and Hong Kong Widescreen Cinema’s ‘Ersatz’ Beginnings in the 1950s
    Yumo Yan · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-06-09
    摘要

    Prior scholarship has long debated whether The Love of Susan (Xin Yutangchun, the Grandview Film Company, 1954) was Hong Kong’s first widescreen production, as well as the widescreen process that the film used. This paper presents new evidence from Grandview’s obscure in-house journal, Grandview Movieland, to argue that the film was indeed Hong Kong’s first widescreen feature, but it was not shot in an anamorphic process like CinemaScope, rather was projected in ersatz widescreen. I further theorize how the ‘ersatz’ was collectively used by Hong Kong producers and exhibitors as a creative response and a survival strategy to combat technological disparities with Hollywood in the early widescreen era, when producers did not have access to anamorphic equipment due to Hollywood’s firm monopoly of the technology, and exhibitors did not have CinemaScope films to put on their screens because Hong Kong was at the end of the global distribution line. Through contextualizing Grandview’s The Love of Susan in local industrial practices and within global widescreen history, I theorize how Hong Kong’s case presents an alternative genealogy of widescreen cinema, one that celebrates the creative and political possibilities of the ersatz.

  23. Disembodied voice: Qiu Yuefeng and aural stardom in reform-era China
    Xiaoning Lu · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-05-27
    摘要

    This article examines the rise of aural stardom in early reform-era China through the case of Qiu Yuefeng, the celebrated dubbing actor of the Shanghai Translated and Dubbed Film Studio. Moving beyond the image-centered paradigm of star studies, it argues that Qiu’s celebrity exemplifies a form of disembodied aural stardom in which the dubbed voice, rather than the screen image, became the primary object of audience recognition and attachment. Tracing Qiu’s career from the institutional formation of socialist dubbing in the 1950s, through the covert production of internal reference films during the Cultural Revolution, to the public circulation of dubbed foreign films in the late 1970s and early 1980s, the article reconstructs the changing listening regime that made his stardom possible. It shows that Qiu’s voice acquired exceptional affective force through the convergence of three conditions: the institutional prestige of dubbing as a socialist art, the remediation of dubbed films across radio, television, and cassette culture, and audiences’ desire for new emotional and imaginative worlds at a moment of political and cultural transition. Qiu’s disembodied voice thus became a privileged medium through which listeners apprehended psychological individuality, intimacy, and previously marginalized forms of subjectivity.

  26. Rethinking Spring in a Small Town through the temporality of neurasthenia
    Hui Li · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-05-21
    摘要

    This article reinterprets Spring in a Small Town through the lens of neurasthenia, a once-prevalent but now largely forgotten diagnostic category in modern China. It argues that neurasthenia should not be understood merely as a thematic element or medical condition within the film, but as a historically specific discourse that structures its narrative, interpersonal relations, and cinematic form. By reconstructing the medical and cultural context of neurasthenia and offering a re-diagnosis of the protagonist Dai Liyan, the article shows how illness reorganizes affective expression and social relations. More importantly, it shows that neurasthenia embodies a temporal paradox: as both a product of modernity and a sign of weakness, it disrupts the linear temporal logic through which modernity promises recovery and progress. This disrupted temporality is central not only to the film’s narrative structure but also to its historical reception. The rejection of Spring in a Small Town by leftist critics is thus understood not simply in ideological terms, but as a response to a form of temporal experience that resists a progressive vision of history. In recovering this forgotten discourse, the article contributes to broader debates in health humanities and film studies on the relationship between illness, temporality, and modernity.

  27. An introduction to caring Chinese cinemas
    Pengnan Hu, Jeroen de Kloet, Patricia Pisters · pp. 1-8 · online 2026-04-30
    摘要

    This introductory article proposes ‘caring Chinese cinemas’ as a conceptual and methodological framework for rethinking the relations between cinema, ethics, aesthetics, ecology, politics, and criticism. Starting from recent debates on care, we emphasise both the productivity and the ambivalence of the concept: care can sustain, protect, and repair, but it can also control, exclude, or reproduce unequal relations of power. Bringing this discussion into the study of Chinese cinemas, the article approaches cinema in two interconnected ways: as a medium through which care is represented, performed, and felt, and as an object that itself requires care through criticism, institutional support, and scholarly engagement. Finally, taken together, the contributions suggest that caring for Chinese cinemas also requires a recalibration of criticism itself, moving beyond detached critique towards more affirmative, reparative, and situated modes of reading.

    Cameron L. White · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-04-30
    摘要

    A City of Sadness (1989) has long attracted Western scholarly attention for its linguistic diversity and aural politics, with many analyses repeating the inaccurate claim that City was the first Taiwanese film to use sync sound rather than post-production dubbing.   29. The sea beyond the stage: Location shooting in the model opera film Boulder Bay
    Jiahe Mei · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-04-19
    摘要

    Released toward the end of the Cultural Revolution, Boulder Bay (1976) is unique for its extensive on-location representation of the seascape. By closely analyzing the film’s treatment of the sea, this article rethinks the ostensible dichotomy of opera and cinema through the dynamic tension between set and location—forces that do not simply oppose, but overlap, conflict, and reinforce one another. The article unpacks this dynamic by examining three distinct modes of spatial construction: the match cuts in transitional empty shots; the montage and special effects that transform the sea into an affective landscape; and the political reconstruction of the actual location into an open-air studio. Overall, this article challenges the linear historiography that typically frames the transition from socialist to post-socialist cinema as a simple shift from "operatic formalism" to "cinematic realism." By revealing how the "real" seascape is meticulously reconstructed, Boulder Bay destabilizes the boundary between the theatrical and the cinematic, offering a new perspective on the aesthetics of the late Cultural Revolution cultural works.

  30. Spies next door: Cinema as counter-espionage campaigns in socialist China (1949–1965)
    Fangyuan Huang · pp. 1-26 · online 2026-04-01
    摘要

    In the early years of the People’s Republic of China, following the retreat of the Nationalist Government to Taiwan and the outbreak of the Korean War, a vigorous campaign against spies and internal saboteurs gained momentum, prompting the production of films known as fante pian (counter-espionage films) between 1949 and 1965. In this article, I challenge conventional interpretations of these films as bound by the generic codifications of spy films. Drawing on a diverse corpus of primary sources, including historical archives, newspaper reports, and film magazines, I unveil the rigorous and critical energies embedded within counter-espionage cinematic experiences of that era. I contend that counter-espionage films not only reflected the impact of political campaigns, as other critics have suggested, but also functioned as a highly-publicized national defense campaign in their own right. To do so, I show how the production, exhibition, and reception of counter-espionage films in socialist China built an embodied network of mass surveillance that monitored and disrupted the secret network of spies. In particular, the multimedia practices—including pre-screening exhibitions, interstitial commentaries, and post-screening forums—fostered a mode of participatory spectatorship, thereby re-enacting the unmasking of hidden enemy bodies and the mobilization of socialist subjects both on and off the screen. This paper aims to enrich our understanding the interplay between socialist subject bodies, media practices, and political movements. It reveals how media practices seamlessly blended every day experiences with the nation’s warfare against spies, thereby contributing to the actualization of these campaigns.

Journal of Japanese and Korean Cinema

Vol. 18, No. 2 · 2026-07-03 · 7 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Film
  1. The ghostly remapping of the camptown: the aesthetics of subversive documentary in The Pregnant Tree and the Goblin (2019)
    Hyun Jung Kong · pp. 85-99 · online 2026-02-06
    摘要

    The camptown genre – literary and cinematic works depicting military camptowns and the lives of military sex workers – emerged after US dominance replaced Japanese colonial rule in Korea. Feminist scholars have critiqued this genre for appropriating the voices of the survivors and subsuming their experience under national allegories of postcolonial trauma. The Pregnant Tree and the Goblin (2019) experiments with the conventions of documentary film as a way to address this. By summoning the dead and staging the memories and trauma of victim-survivors, the film employs counterfactual aesthetics that allow survivors to express their desires while resisting the gaze of the other. This approach unsettles the power dynamics between observer and observed and disrupts the indexical link between representations of military sex workers and their lived experiences. The deployment of the counterfactual blurs the boundaries between reality and fiction, the living and the dead, by dismantling the dichotomy of truth and falsehood. By so doing, the film creates a cinematic space in which past and present coexist, allowing victim-survivors to mourn losses while resisting the institutionalization of their trauma. Ultimately, this paper argues that the film is a subversive documentary that expands the boundaries of truth and testimony in its form and aesthetics.

  2. Echoes of silent cinema: the benshi’s lingering voice, modernity, and the soundscape of nostalgia
    Eun Jeong Choi · pp. 100-118 · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    This article examines how benshi performance was transformed and recirculated through radio, SP records, and other forms of entertainment during the transition to sound. I argue that the continued presence of the benshi alongside their nostalgic representation complicates a linear narrative of media replacement: even as benshi remained active, they were increasingly presented and marketed as figures of a disappearing cinematic past. Nostalgia therefore emerged not simply after the disappearance of silent cinema but during its ongoing transformation, shaping how older media practices were remembered, represented, and commercially recirculated. Through the benshi, this article demonstrates how old and new media practices, as well as different temporalities of cinematic experience, continued to coexist within the changing media culture of prewar Japan.

  3. Steel robots and space turtleships: the divergence of Korean animation and Japanese anime
    Zachary Samuel Gottesman · pp. 119-142 · online 2026-10-07
    摘要

    During the Park Chung-hee military dictatorship, Korea shadowed Japanese industrial upgrading at every stage. The same was true in animation, in which Korea took Japanese animations, indigenized them, and imbued them with a techno-nationalism that positioned Korean animation as both dependent on Japan and destined to overcome it. Instead, the opposite happened: today South Korean animation is a pale shadow of Japanese anime’s global success. This essay argues that this divergence, rather than the result of flawed state policies or overdependence on outsourcing, is the result of objective shifts in global capitalism that began in the 1970s. This essay analyses two Korean animated films that mimicked Japanese animated works: Robot Taekwon V (1976), which mimicked Japanese animated film Mazinger Z (1972) and Fly! Spaceship Geobukseon (1979), which mimicked TV series and film Space Battleship Yamato (1974–1975; 1977). While the former Japan-Korea couplet was situated in convergence between the HCI (heavy and chemical industry) economic plans of Japan and Korea, the latter couplet marked the divergence between Korean animation and Japanese ‘anime,’ i.e. the rise of a new commodity form constituted by global ‘otaku’ consumption that reshaped the global economy and ended the previous period’s possibility of economic development.

  4. Between Chekhov and modernization: dramaturgies of immobility and choral forms in the cinema of Mikio Naruse
    Aleix Rodríguez-Gracia · pp. 143-154 · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    This article examines the relationship between Anton Chekhov’s dramaturgy and Mikio Naruse’s cinema through a comparative study of The Girl in the Rumor and Three Sisters with Maiden Hearts (1935), Flowing (1956), and Late Chrysanthemums (1954). Chekhov’s Japanese reception through translation and shingeki supplies the cultural context for this formal comparison with The Cherry Orchard and Three Sisters. The Girl in the Rumor, loosely inspired by the former, provides its documented point of departure. The films’ two production moments allow different arrangements of collective life to be compared. Through a close analysis of mise-en-scène, editing, sound, and the spatial organization of bodies, the study examines how Naruse gives dramatic form to the social and material transformations of modern Japan. Immobility describes the persistence of frustrated aspirations amid physical movement, and chorality distributes dramatic attention across connected lives. A distinctive affinity with Chekhov resides in the changing perceptual weight of everyday space. A room or a street can outlast the conflict that made it central, giving historical transformation a duration that continues through familiar gestures.

  5. Nature, gender, and the production of framed spaces: an ecomedia analysis of Ozu Yasujiro’s The Munekata Sisters
    Dingding Wang · pp. 155-172 · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    This paper challenges simplified interpretations of Ozu Yasujiro's The Munekata Sisters (1950) as a cliché representation of the binary opposition between tradition and modernity. Applying an ecomedia analytical framework, the study examines how the film engages nonhuman nature not as a static cultural code or passive mise-en-scène prop, but as a dynamic, embodied, and sensory medium. Through close readings of lighting, shadow aesthetics, and visual-auditory framing, this paper demonstrates how contrasting cinematic spaces – the traditional Kyoto temple and the modern Ginza bar – are artificially constructed and how abstract Japanese ‘secondary nature’ is juxtaposed with shadowy, colonial flora to demonstrate the inescapable ‘sense of planet’. Furthermore, by analyzing human-nonhuman affective encounters and the spatial mobility of the sisters alongside the structural parallels of postwar masculinity, the paper demonstrates how Ozu's spatial worldmaking articulates a nuanced interpretation of Japanese female subjectivity and national identity. The Munekata Sisters provides a way of understanding how Ozu's cinema is rooted in ideals of ecology and environment that have been naturalized in specific ways and how it frames a world in which both human and nonhuman animals, plants, and the physical setting interact and interconnect, contributing to the coherent ecological worldmaking in cinema.

  6. The rise and fall of changgeuk cinema: the 1959 adaptation of The Tale of Chunhyang in North Korea
    Roman Husarski, Gabor Sebo · pp. 173-188 · online 2026-09-05
    摘要

    This article examines the first North Korean cinematic adaptation of the well-known pansori text, The Tale of Chunhyang. Although three DPRK productions based on this narrative exist, this study represents the first sustained scholarly engagement with the 1959 version. Directed by Yun Ryong-gyu, this film was long considered lost due to political suppression until its recent rediscovery by the authors in Warsaw. Drawing on political and film analysis, the article argues that the 1959 production constitutes a distinctive form of ‘Changgeuk Cinema,’ reflecting a broader cultural realignment in the late 1950s North Korean state. This hybrid form synthesizes the aesthetic principles of socialist realism with the performative conventions of Korean opera (changgeuk), mediated through pansori vocalization, traditional dance, and indigenous instrumentation. Contrary to prevailing interpretations, we contend that the film’s suppression stemmed not primarily from a sexual scandal involving its lead actress, but rather from a more fundamental ideological repudiation of the changgeuk genre itself. By foregrounding the film’s articulation of ‘national flavour,’ this study traces the brief ascendancy and subsequent decline of a cinematic form initially celebrated as a socialist-nationalist achievement, before being displaced by new iterations of ‘Juche cinema’ under Kim Jong-il in the 1980s.

  1. Paranoia Pictures: Cause or Consequence of Cynicism About the American Government?
    R. Grant Steen · pp. 139-154 · online 2026-06-05
    摘要

    An earlier analysis of whether movies lag or lead historical events failed to yield a definitive answer. This finding of chronological ambiguity could be because the events evaluated were discrete historical moments, not broad societal trends spanning decades. To evaluate whether movies lag or lead broad societal trends, we examine movies that may relate to the growing distrust of governmental authority in the United States. Such “paranoia pictures” were first made during WWII, enjoyed a sharp rise in popularity in the early 1970s, and began to decline in popularity in the early 2000s. The rise in popularity reflects a growing distrust of the motives of government, while the decline in paranoia pictures may mean the genre is flawed as it fails to offer viewers a sense of catharsis. Paranoia pictures became prevalent coincident with our growing distrust of public institutions, and our distrust was reflected back to us from the movie screen, in a way that validated and may have augmented our angst and anxiety. We conclude that movies can both lag and lead societal trends; they are not so much a cause or consequence of societal trends as a way to process emotions associated with historical events.

  2. “Sacrifice and Redemption: Fury (2014) and the Cultural Memory of the Second World War”
    William Stewart Skiles · pp. 155-173 · online 2026-09-08
    摘要

    The film Fury (2014) tells the story of a tank unit driving deep into Nazi Germany at the end of the Second World War. Fury depicts the inevitable result of a morally bankrupt National Socialist ideology, a worldview inspired by aggressive militarism and fanatical racism. The aim of the article is to explore how the filmmakers tell the history of the Second World War through the perspective of the Fury’s crew to emphasize the religious and moral nature of their mission. The filmmakers show that the fruits of National Socialism are destruction, death, and the loss of meaning. The crew confronts nihilism in the fury of combat, yet their mission keeps them on a path of meaning, as they struggle against the evil of Nazism. The film hints of a vision for a better world in religious and symbolic language and the presentation of religious symbols. The tank unit drives through the devastation, holding steadily on to meaning, in completing its mission to save American soldiers caught in the crosshairs of the SS. This article explores the importance of the film in representing for cultural remembrance the sacrifices of soldiers of the Second World War.

    Stephen Schillinger · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-09-27
    摘要

    The Coen brothers’ 1998 film, The Big Lebowski is a comic inversion of the thematic concerns, philosophical aspirations, character expectations and genre tropes common to film noir.   4. “Splurching” the Military: The Ideological Uses of Slapstick in Blake Edwards’s Operation Petticoat and What Did You Do in the War, Daddy?
    William R. Glass · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-09-08
    摘要

    The crew of a pink submarine during World War II desperately trying to escape the Japanese and a platoon of Americans trying to capture a strategic Sicilian village from its residents who just want to have a party: these two scenarios are the core plots of films directed by Blake Edwards. The former is Operation Petticoat (1959) and the latter is What Did You Do in the War, Daddy? (1966). Service comedies in particular may have an intrinsically subversive impact on the subject of their humor so that over the course of time and under repeated attack that subject has its authority weakened, and slapstick may be a particularly effective means of undermining military authority. In Operation Petticoat, slapstick is ideologically deployed to demonstrate the sub’s commander’s competence to overcome obstacles to fulfill his mission.   10. Kafkan Attention and Distraction in Orson Welles’s The Trial
    Daniel Burns · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    This essay reexamines Orson Welles’s Citation1962 adaptation of Franz Kafka’s The Trial through the recent cognitive turn in Kafka Studies, a critical approach that explores how theories of perception, emotion, and behavior shape the aesthetic experience of Kafka’s work. I argue that Welles’s film translates Kafka’s cognitive realism to the screen by visualizing attention and distraction as interanimating forms of cognition. Moving beyond fidelity- or production-centered approaches, this reading emphasizes how Welles’s imaginative use of mobile framing and disjunctive editing generates a phenomenologically responsive mode of spectatorship. Through analyses of three mutually reinforcing threshold effects—eyeline diffusion, infinite regress, and projection-as-positionality—the essay shows how Welles scales disorientation across interpersonal, spatial, and allegorical registers. Taken together, these effects produce a cumulative cinematic experience that mirrors the reader’s engagement with Kafka’s prose, demonstrating how Welles’s adaptation both preserves and expands Kafka’s exploration of cognition under modernity’s crisis of attention.

Journal of Screenwriting

Vol. 17, No. 2 · 2026-07-01 · 8 篇 · Intellect · Film
  1. Documentary storytelling: Crafting reality through screenwriting and fictional story devices
    Levi Dean, Sadia Jamil, Simon Weaving, Thomas William Whyke · pp. 139-147
    摘要

    This Special Issue examines screenwriting and fictional storytelling devices within the context of documentary filmmaking from a global perspective, exploring a range of practices from those found on mainstream streaming services to those developed in first-person documentary work. The six articles comprising this collection highlight how industrial curation, personal practices, established narrative techniques and hybrid strategies have shaped the influence of fictional storytelling devices on documentary form. A primary aim of this Special Issue is to explore analytical frameworks that can deepen understanding of screenwriting and fictional storytelling devices present in documentaries, benefiting scholars, students and filmmakers alike.

  2. True-crime storytelling: Crafting reality through fictional narrative devices
    Sean Maher, Sue Cake · pp. 149-165
    摘要

    This article examines how long-form true crime storytelling operates at the intersection of documentary and drama through a comparative analysis of the Netflix docuseries The Staircase (2018) and its HBO dramatized adaptation The Staircase (2022). Situating both series within the contemporary streaming environment, the study explores how serial formats reshape narrative organisation, audience engagement and representational strategies across factual and fictional modes. Focusing on the structuring effects of long-form seriality, the analysis considers how narrative uncertainty, character-centred storytelling and sustained tension function across both texts, challenging established distinctions between documentary and drama. The article also addresses the ethical and representational consequences of adapting a documentary into scripted drama, particularly where the production process itself becomes part of the narrative. By examining these dynamics, the article contributes to debates on media hybridity and demonstrates how streaming-era true crime destabilizes genre boundaries and reshapes audience relationships to fact, fiction and the representation of real events.

  3. Scripting the past: Narration and historical re-enactment in long-form documentary – A case study of Wyatt Earp and the Cowboy War (2024)
    Simon Weaving, Levi Dean, Thomas William Whyke · pp. 167-187
    摘要

    This article explores the way that documentary screenwriters shape narration in long-form documentary series, which are becoming increasingly common on streaming platforms that have embraced non-fiction storytelling as part of their offering to subscribers. Of particular interest is the use of dramatic re-enactments, which allow documentary filmmakers to embrace a range of storytelling techniques typically associated with screenwriting for fictional drama. Using a case study of Netflix’s Wyatt Earp and the Cowboy War (2024), which delivers 80 per cent of its content through re-enactments, we examine the screenwriting decisions involved in organizing the historical material using re-enactment, voice-over, interviews with subject-matter experts and archival material. Our research reveals telling insights about the ways in which re-enactments provide increased narrative control for the screenwriter and how the delicate interplay of scripted re-enactment with scripted voice-over allows for screen narration to efficiently set out factual exposition whilst creating emotional engagement, thereby balancing dramatic storytelling with historical accuracy.

  4. First-person documentary, essay film, fiction and documentary screenwriting: The case of Welcome Back, Farewell (2021)
    Marcos Yoshi · pp. 189-206
    摘要

    This article examines the screenwriting processes behind Welcome Back, Farewell (Brazil, 105 minutes, 2021), a first-person documentary centred on my family’s experience of migration between Brazil and Japan and the emotional consequences of long-term separation.   5. Finding the story in Love Opera (2020)
    Margaret McVeigh · pp. 207-222
    摘要

    Documentary filmmaking occupies a liminal space between narrative construction and factual representation. Unlike fiction, documentary scripts must remain responsive to real-world developments, often evolving throughout production. This article explores scriptwriting at the treatment stage for the award-winning feature documentary Love Opera (2020). Initially the documentary was envisioned to be a story centred on two visionary women and their quest to provide a world-class opportunity for emerging Australian Opera singers via the Lisa Gasteen National Opera Program. However, it evolved into a character-driven story focused on the transformative experience of the programme’s students as they strived to make the most of this unique opportunity. Drawing on the work of documentary theorists and screenwriting reflective creative practice, this article explores how documentary writers may envisage a narrative scenario based on an as-yet unknown and emergent reality. It examines how fictional narrative strategies – including character, drama and metaphor – may be employed to explore universal truths within factual storytelling. Through excerpts from the treatment, writer’s notes and the final documentary synopsis, this article contributes to the growing field of documentary screenwriting by providing a first-hand account of finding the fictional drivers of storytelling in documentary to find the story waiting to be found.

  6. Interweaving sociopolitical realities and fiction: Adapting real events into dramatic structures in The 37th State
    Nicodemus Adai Patrick · pp. 223-256
    摘要

    This article examines The 37th State , a feature-length docufiction screenplay exploring the modern sociopolitical reality in Nigeria through fictionalization and narrative displacement. The analysis places the screenplay within the context of docufiction and political drama traditions by drawing on concepts from historiographic metafiction, narratology and Brechtian epic theatre to explore strategies of allegory, focalization and distanciation. Through close analysis of narrative structure and formal devices the article explores how documentary textures, fictional settings and mediated public discourse are integrated into the screenplay. Through this, the article advances screenwriting scholarship by projecting narrative form and genre hybridity through the ethical and political work of dramatic writing.

    摘要

  1. ‘Pawing the story forward’: The producer as embedded creative mediator in a Danish writers’ room
    Nathali Herold Solon Pilegaard
    摘要

    Collaborative screenplay development is frequently associated with distributed creativity and collective authorship within writers’ rooms. At the same time, screenplay development unfolds within institutionally embedded production environments shaped by broadcaster expectations, production realities and organizational accountability. Drawing on an ethnographic case study of the Danish television Christmas calendar , this article examines how a producer participated within a Danish writers’ room as an embedded creative mediator.   2. Céline Sciamma’s writing through desire: A new approach to writing women’s stories
    Philippa Burne
    摘要

    The hero’s journey and the conflict model of storytelling have had a strong hold on western screenwriting since the mid-twentieth century. Increasingly, this is being questioned, especially by feminist scholars and storytellers, who find the hero’s journey exclusive of women’s stories and narrow in storytelling possibility. Maureen Murdock, Kim Hudson and others have all developed models of the heroine’s journey, but do these go far enough? Are they still too closely related to the conflict-based model entrenched by the hero’s journey and not bold enough in their scope of change? In her 2019 British Academy of Film and Television Arts (BAFTA) Screenwriting Guru talk, French writer/director Céline Sciamma proposed a new model of screenwriting emerging from her own practice that might more widely offer new possibilities for telling stories on-screen. Sciamma calls it writing through desire and argues for driving screenplays through desire, not conflict, especially when telling the stories of women and gender-diverse characters. Her three-step approach involves identifying the desires of the writer, working at the scene level and mapping the scenario. Sciamma used this method to develop and write the screenplay of her breakthrough film, Portrait de la jeune fille en feu ( Portrait of a Lady on Fire ) (2019), which won the ‘Best Screenplay’ at Cannes in 2019.

  3. Web series written by women and targeting female audiences: An exploratory analysis of narrative practices in a cross-cultural context
    Danyang Li
    摘要

    This study presents an exploratory analysis of narrative practices in web series authored by women and targeting female audiences, examined through an intersectional feminist framework. Focusing on creative verbalization (the verbal expression of the narrative within a series, including dialogues, monologues and narrative storytelling devices), narrative expression and audience reception, the research analyses linguistic, literary and sociocultural features of ten widely viewed women-oriented web series produced in the United States and China for target audiences aged 18–45. A multidisciplinary methodology was employed, combining quantitative linguistic analysis (including type-token ratio and metaphor usage), natural language processing techniques for assessing lexical complexity, qualitative literary and thematic analysis and a sociological survey involving 386 female respondents. The findings indicate a high degree of lexical variation and a context-dependent relationship between metaphorical density and reported emotional engagement within the analysed corpus. Series that foreground marginalized identities and complex narrative perspectives earned higher audience ratings (4.3–4.8 on a five-point scale), underscoring the interpretive relevance of culturally situated storytelling. Rather than offering prescriptive models for content creation, the study contributes to feminist media scholarship and screenwriting studies by highlighting how female-authored web series employ narrative structures, character development and dialogue strategies to negotiate identity, representation and narrative agency in digital spaces.

  4. Script development and audience influences: Towards a typology of audience-making practices
    Jakob Freudendal
    摘要

    In this theoretical article, I offer new insights into script development as a negotiated and sociocultural process. Adapting the cultural studies concept of audience-making to screenwriting research, I argue that audiences are a contextual structure that industry practitioners contend with in various ways during negotiated script development processes. In doing so, the article addresses a research gap in screenwriting studies pertaining to the interplay between industry practitioners and audiences. I argue that audiences are embedded in script development processes, as feedback and suggestions for improvement are often based on notions of audiences and because audience analytics and direct feedback are increasingly employed during development, albeit to varying extents. To theorize this interplay, the article introduces a typology of four audience-making models deployed during script development. These models consist of imagined, measured, competent and creative audiences. Each model affords audiences different levels of agency during script development with implications for the creative process. This framework is especially timely, given the growing reliance on data-driven decision-making in contemporary screen production and the increased potentials for interaction between industry and audiences in the digital realm.

  2. Feeling survival: diasporic visual practices by UK–Hong Kong indie publishers, Deacon Lui and Kayla Lui
    Kin-Long Tong · pp. 17-39 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    This article examines how diasporic visual practices mediate the emotional aftermath of Hong Kong’s 2019 protests and the ensuing wave of migration to the UK. Focusing on the self-published works of Hong Kong artists Deacon Lui and Kayla Lui, the author explores how zines, comics and experimental photography register not only political rupture but the ambiguous affective states that follow – fear, absurdity, ambivalence, intimacy. Together, their work resists linear narrative and stable identity, holding space for forms of feeling that are still in formation. This analysis positions indie publishing as a method of survival – an affective and aesthetic strategy through which diasporic subjects navigate dislocation, remain connected and make sense of life in transit.

  3. Edging aesthetics: queer and trans undocumented migrant images and imaginations
    stef torralba · pp. 40-57 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    This article attends to erotic imaginations dreamed within the everyday material realities of queer and trans undocumented migrants in the US. Focusing on fictional representations in narrative cinema, the author analyzes depictions of nonnormative noncitizen life in two independent narrative feature films: Aurora Guerrero’s Mosquita y Mari (2012) and Isabel Sandoval’s Lingua Franca (2019). The author argues that, amid feeling out of sync with the normative nation-state’s organizations of space and time, quotidian erotic imaginative acts (like daydreaming, reminiscing and fantasizing) allow these films’ gender- and sexual-nonconforming illegalized main characters momentary access to otherwise arrangements of time–space and bodies. These different arrangements of spatiotemporality and interbodily relation rebut the placelessness that conditions queer and trans migrant life while opening further possibilities for survival and togetherness across time and space. The article proposes ‘edging’ as a framework for studying these imaginative rearrangements of time–space and proximity and the cross-ethnic decolonial coalitions they can activate. The author then reads edging’s filmic representations through what they call ‘edging aesthetics’. In so doing, they suggest these films surface the oft-overlooked significance of ordinary rituals of imagining for staging forms of being-with that exceed the US nation-state’s attempts to manage and control intimacies between people.

  4. Identifying for discarding phobias: alterity endorsed
    Mieke Bal · pp. 58-73 · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Based on the third session of her inaugural lecture at the Collège de France in 2023, in this article Mieke Bal focuses on the phobias, the excessive fears, against confrontations with alterity such as, today, refugees and other migrants. The famous masterpiece of cultural heritage, Don Quijote, from the 16th century, is so striking in that its world-wide fame seems challenged by its poetic, chaotic anti-linearity. The author explains that lack of structure due to the experience of slavery that the author, Cervantes, suffered during more than five years, without a sense of an ending. The strange situation in which the author found himself when, after a victory of the Spanish army, he and his brother were captured and sold into slavery, can only have traumatized him. The article examines how such awful socio-cultural situations make reasonable story-telling impossible. Through that narrative incapacitation, the true masterpiece from the past resonates with today once we realize that slavery still exists, but also with the indispensable anachronism that we need to understand how it makes the past an integral element of the present.

  5. Solidarity movement photography: visual activism for Palestine in Berlin
    Franca Marquardt · pp. 74-98 · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Protest photography is important for articulating and materializing emotions, and for connecting movements across different places. Through conversations with four photographers in the Palestine solidarity movement in Berlin, the article discusses how they not only seek better ways to represent protest but also deploy photography as a practice of solidarity and care for the activists involved. As such, movement photography creates an ‘oppositional gaze’ to domination, based on a ‘civil contract’ between the photographer, the photographed and the viewer, that is upheld not by empathy alone but by relationality and mutual commitment to the cause of Palestinian liberation. Taking these arguments further, the article explores photography as a collective emotional practice that sustains political bonds and imagines alternative futures. Tracing how photographers navigate care, accountability and emotional intensity within protests, it highlights the role of images as both evidence and affective expressions of solidarity amid the growing repression of internationalist movements.

Vol. 48, No. 8 · 2026-11 · 12 篇 · SAGE · Media
  1. Enacting postcolonial digital citizenship: Malay Muslim women, social media and the politics of (un)belonging to “Thainess”
    Treepon Kirdnark · pp. 1531-1548 · online 2026-05-29
    摘要

    Although Malay Muslim women’s increasing role in the peace process in Thailand’s southern border provinces has become a subject of scholarly interest, their use of social media in activism remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, this paper examines how Malay Muslim women activists employ social media to strengthen their human rights advocacy, based on in-depth interviews with 10 women activists. The findings reveal that, given the highly securitized environment and the shortcomings of Thai mainstream media, social media provides an alternative civic space where Muslim women activists form a political subject, namely “pious online rights claimants.” This paper argues that this emerging social media practice contributes to the enactment of “postcolonial digital citizenship” which allows these women to contest the constructed category of Malay Muslims and Muslim women. Such intersectional contestations not only legitimize their political participation in the peace process, but also strategically manage the multifaceted boundaries of belonging as Malay Muslim women in the male-dominant sphere of the peace process and the enduring colonial notion of “Thainess.”

  2. Whose voices count? Participation, power, and communication in the Global South
    Vinod Pavarala · pp. 1549-1567 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    This paper critically reviews participatory communication in the communication for social change (CFSC) tradition in the Global South, following its development from radical origins—grounded in Freirean pedagogy and grassroots action—to its co-optation within neoliberal development frameworks. Through a comparative analysis of Latin America, Africa, and Asia, the paper questions the enduring gap between rhetoric and practice while observing how participatory action is usually caught between donor agendas, state regulation, and structural social hierarchies. Conceptually, the paper thematizes tensions around power, voice, and epistemic justice, contending that real participation necessitates more than technical participation; it needs the redistribution of communicative agency and a critical attention to the politics of voice, listening, and representation. In conclusion, the article urges a rethinking of participatory communication as a locally specific, contested practice grounded in local epistemologies and collective struggle that can promote social transformation in the face of persistent structural inequalities. The paper also contributes to ongoing efforts to rethink communication theory from Global South perspectives within contemporary media and communication scholarship.

  3. “My body has gone out of style”: A critical examination of fashion magazines’ GLP-1 agonist drug discourse
    Katie Baker Jones · pp. 1568-1583 · online 2026-06-06
    摘要

    This paper examines the fashion media discourse on the rise of the “Ozempic Era” in the American editions of Vogue and Elle magazines published in print and online since the announcement by the FDA clearing a GLP-1 agonist to be prescribed as a weight loss aid. Using critical discourse analysis, I explore the coverage of Ozempic and other brand name drugs in the fashion press. I situate the discourse on GLP-1’s ability to reshape the body to fit a long-held esthetic ideal at the intersection of lifestyle/fashion and health/wellness journalism and the intertextual context inherent to each.   4. Building transversal feminist politics online: A digital ethnography of Ni Una Menos (2015–2018)
    Ayleen Cabas-Mijares · pp. 1584-1600 · online 2026-06-16
    摘要

    Ni Una Menos is one of the most powerful transnational feminist collectives of Latin America, drawing from legacies of feminist organizing in Argentina as well as the media acumen of its founding members.
