  1. Practicing love with algorithms: How online communities narrate and make sense of human–AI intimacy
    Fanjue Liu, Haiyan Hao, Kun Xu · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes embedded in everyday life, emotionally intimate interactions with AI companions are becoming increasingly visible. Yet prior research has focused mainly on individual experiences, leaving limited understanding of how human–AI intimacy is collectively interpreted and negotiated in online communities. This study examines community discourse on RedNote, a major Chinese social and lifestyle platform, using 13,059 comments from 58 relevant posts. Combining BERTopic modeling with a human–large language model (LLM) hybrid emotion classification procedure, the analysis identifies three thematic clusters: intimacy as co-construction, intimacy as infrastructure, and intimacy as boundary negotiation. Co-construction centers on persona design, roleplay, and narrative world-building and is marked mainly by joy and curiosity. Infrastructure-related discourse is associated with frustration and sadness, while boundary negotiation reflects ambivalence toward intimacy with AI.

  2. The managed sisterhood: Gendered labor and intimacy in Chinese fashion influencer industry
    Qiheng Lin, Chenyu Dong · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    As China’s leading multi-channel network (MCN) institutions become increasingly industrialized and bureaucratic, their operations are paradoxically characterized by intensified emotional dynamics. Drawing on ethnographic research within a women-dominated fashion influencer team, this study identifies a sisterhood-based production model that embeds sisterly ties within hierarchical organizational structures. It traces the formation and collapse of this managed sisterhood. By cultivating an illusion of unity and belonging, intimacy makes labor more governable, obscuring coercion while facilitating the mobilization of emotional, aesthetic, and physical capacities. In this way, sisterhood becomes integral to production, intensifying labor and rendering precarity routine. Under platform capitalism, traditional Chinese sisterhood, once grounded in kinship and flexible resistance, is reconfigured into an occupational form of informal labor control, producing “disposable kinship” in which affective bonds become temporary and replaceable. This study challenges dominant political readings of sisterhood and demonstrates how intimacy itself becomes a mechanism of governance within platform capitalism.

  3. Guardians of the cyberverse: Dispositional, attitudinal, and autobiographical correlates of user engagement against misogynist digital hate
    Rinat Meerson, Kevin Koban, Jörg Matthes · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    As digital misogyny remains widespread, user intervention is an essential complement to lenient platform governance. Yet, willingness to intervene varies considerably across individuals and cultural contexts. In a cross-national survey conducted in Austria ( n = 791), France ( n = 800), Hungary ( n = 775), and Sweden ( n = 785), we examine how dispositional, attitudinal, and autobiographical characteristics relate to users’ direct and indirect intervention and support for moderation. Empathic anger consistently emerged as the strongest positive predictor of all three outcomes across countries. Perspective-taking, online self-presentation, and victimization experiences showed meaningful positive associations with intervention, while misogynist attitudes were inconsistently related. Toxic disinhibition showed a dual pattern, negatively associated with moderation support but positively associated with direct intervention across all countries, suggesting its potential to be cleverly repurposed toward counterspeech. These findings underscore the importance of emotionally activating literacy programs and highlight the need for culturally sensitive intervention models.

  4. Stress and self-efficacy under artificial intelligence disruption: A mixed-methods study on the usage of artificial intelligence among Finnish workers
    Atte Oksanen, Teo Keipi, Moona Heiskari, Iina Savolainen · online 2026-10-15
    摘要

    Rapid workplace integration of artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping work practices and challenging employees’ self-efficacy and well-being. This mixed-methods study examines these experiences using national survey data ( N = 2109) and qualitative interviews ( N = 54) with Finnish workers. Frequent AI use, AI-induced identity threat, and organizational AI deployment are associated with higher AI strain, whereas AI self-efficacy relates to frequent use, positive AI attitudes, and subjective well-being. Identity threat moderated several of these associations, highlighting the role of professional identity in AI-related experiences. Analysis drawing on Greimas’ semiotic modalities (must, can, know, and want) explains how these patterns are interpreted and negotiated in practice, revealing tensions between perceived self-efficacy and pressures to integrate AI. AI use was often described as voluntary. However, participants anticipated increasing competence demands and responsibility. The findings emphasize the need for organizational strategies that address identity-related challenges and support employee well-being in AI-driven work transformations.

  5. Praise, rent, and the platform: Substack and the enclosure of blog culture
    Will Riley · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    This article situates Substack within the platformisation of blogging and the enclosure of cultural production within proprietary infrastructures. Tracing the genealogy of blogging as a decentralised practice that carried within it the seeds of citizen journalism and informal intellectual life, I argue that Substack does not restore an apparently lost cultural practice but renegotiates and interpolates blogging into a platformised logic. Framed as a return to independent, writer-led publishing, Substack instead, as I argue, renders free-labour interaction as a source of rent. I analyse the rhetoric and revolving discourse around Substack’s internal curatorial project, ‘The Substack Post’. Drawing on Jodi Dean’s account of blogs as displaced mediators alongside theories of digital labour and platform capitalism, and approaching Substack as a simultaneously platformised and infrastructuralised formation, I argue that praise operates as a platform-specific form of commodity fetishism, animating the platform while obscuring the labour relations that sustain it.

  6. Platformed affective withdrawal: Quiet quitting and the refusal of gendered emotional labour on TikTok
    Aybüke Büyükkağnıcı · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    This article examines #quietquitting on TikTok as a platform-mediated articulation of gendered affective withdrawal. While organisational research interprets quiet quitting as reduced discretionary effort or declining work engagement, this study examines how quiet quitting is articulated as a digitally circulated refusal of feminised emotional labour. Drawing on feminist theories of affective labour and critical platform studies, the analysis explores how workers renegotiate expectations of emotional availability within algorithmically structured visibility regimes. The study is based on a qualitative-dominant mixed-methods analysis of 586 TikTok videos shared under #quietquitting between 2020 and 2026. Combining thematic discourse analysis with descriptive engagement indicators, the findings show that creators frequently frame quiet quitting as a refusal of unpaid relational mediation, emotional management, and workplace care. The article introduces the concept of platformed affective withdrawal to explain how narratives of workplace disengagement circulate through algorithmic visibility systems that amplify and individualise gendered labour inequalities.

New Review of Film and Television Studies

Vol. 23, No. 2 · 2025-04-03 · 13 篇 · Taylor & Francis · Film/TV
  1. Amateur, avant-garde, or underground? François Reichenbach’s early homophile films
    Hugo Ljungbäck · pp. 135-163 · online 2025-10-22
    摘要

    François Reichenbach was a prolific filmmaker and integral part of the French New Wave, and made several dozen short and feature-length documentaries over the course of his career. This article reassesses his early amateur filmmaking period by focusing on two recently rediscovered films from 1954: Last Spring, a gay narrative fiction film, and Nus masculins, a reel of ‘home movies’ documenting Reichenbach’s encounters with beautiful men in equally stunning settings. To contextualize the films, I provide an overview of Reichenbach’s amateur filmmaking activities throughout the early 1950s, as well as an analysis of the first two films on Reichenbach’s ‘official’ filmography, released through Pierre Braunberger’s production company in 1955. In doing so, I situate Reichenbach’s early filmmaking activities within the context of a transcontinental gay modernist art scene as well as the emergence of the homophile movement. While Reichenbach was neither a participant in the American postwar avant-garde of the late 1940s, nor in the New York underground film scene of the 1960s – perhaps explaining his neglect by scholars of both movements – he was part of a 1950s gay art world centered in New York, inhabited by the likes of author Glenway Wescott, curator Monroe Wheeler, photographer George Platt Lynes, and painters Pavel Tchelitchew and Bernard Perlin. These influences, I argue, helped Reichenbach develop and articulate a particularly ‘homophile’ vision of gay filmmaking.

  2. “World-famous”: the Nicholas Brothers’ Black stardom on television, 1951–1977
    Pamela Krayenbuhl · pp. 164-189 · online 2025-10-29
    摘要

    This article argues for an expanded understanding of the career and star text of African American film stars the Nicholas Brothers. Though mainly remembered for their tap dance specialty numbers in 1940s Hollywood musicals, Fayard and Harold Nicholas also toured the world and appeared on television variety programs throughout the 1950s–1970s. I hold that their performance style transitions from ‘40s jazz-tap to a more layered, international style in this understudied latter half of their career, and I show how these updates engage with both musical and social changes of the period. Across their television appearances, I demonstrate that the brothers embody a looser, more ‘soulful', and more explicitly sexual masculinity than was possible during their earlier Hollywood phase. This more complete understanding of the Nicholases’ star text helps map the shifting representational limits and possibilities for Black media stars of the mid-twentieth century and beyond.

  3. Guilty of being a dwarf: Peter Dinklage and American fantasy television
    Ryan Banfi · pp. 190-210 · online 2025-06-13
    摘要

    Drawing on interviews, online chat forums, magazines, and scholarly work concerning Peter Dinklage’s acting career, this article argues that although the TV industry positions Dinklage to play parts that address his restricted height, his performance as Tyrion Lannister in Game of Thrones (2011–2019) permits exceptional visibility and complex roles for height-restricted actors in fantasy TV. This inspection of the fantasy TV genre and height-restricted actors performing in fantasy TV allows us to understand Dinklage’simpact on Game of Thrones and fantasy television and the professional progression of height-restricted actors in the entertainment industry.

  4. Black and white in colour: looking across race in Spike Lee’s Clockers and Summer of Sam
    Thomas Austin · pp. 211-230 · online 2025-08-12
    摘要

    This essay focuses on two films by Spike Lee: Clockers (1995), adapted from the novel of the same name by the Jewish New York crime writer Richard Price, and Summer of Sam (1999), for which Lee revised an initial script by two Italian Americans, Victor Colicchio and Michael Imperioli. I argue that while Clockers leaves intact and fails to challenge normative tropes of white responsibility for Black rescue and improvement, Summer of Sam offers a more complex and insightful interrogation of whiteness. The film confounds myths of white superiority by exposing the anxieties, insecurities, and fears of difference within an Italian American community. Acts of verbal and physical violence are directed at white alterity even more than people of colour, but both responses are shown to share the same cause: a quest for distinction and self-validation that symbolically denies, yet inadvertently attests to, white fragility.

  5. Self-reflexive stardom and the early television sitcom: parafictional persona in The George Burns and Gracie Allen Show
    Bradley J. Dixon · pp. 231-254 · online 2025-06-11
    摘要

    Scholars of early television comedy in the United States have noted the prevalence of self-reflexivity among the sitcoms and variety programmes of the period, typically understood as a set of formal and presentational techniques that call attention to the apparatus and artifice of broadcast transmission. But one characteristic of the early television sitcom that warrants further consideration as distinct from other forms of self-reflexivity is the use of parafiction, a narrative mode that blurs the distinction of reality and fiction by depicting performers playing versions of themselves. The programme that made the most extensive and effective use of parafiction in early television is The George Burns and Gracie Allen Show (1950–1958), a sitcom depicting the fictionalised domestic lives of real-life comedy and life partners George Burns and Gracie Allen. The show borrows heavily from Burns and Allen’s real lives, makes frequent reference to their careers in vaudeville and radio comedy, and further calls attention to its own self-reflexivity through a narrative device in which Burns' character literally steps out of the studio set and addresses the home audience directly. Both within and beyond the diegesis of the show, Burns and Allen maintained elaborate parafictional personas that largely defined their public images for the entirety of their careers, and which continue to influence their popular conception even now, decades after their deaths. This article examines Burns and Allen’s self-presentations on stage and screen, as well as traces of their ‘real’ lives as found in memoirs, biographies, and interviews, to demystify their complex personas and argue that they represent perhaps the earliest and most significant example of parafictional persona in American screen comedy.

  6. Consumer Survival Kit : U.S. public television, economic imaginaries, and counter-visions of a responsive state
    Kit Hughes · pp. 255-282 · online 2025-10-08
    摘要

    Maryland Public Television’s Consumer Survival Kit (Maryland, 1973–1974; national PBS, 1975–1979) bills itself as an aid to the average consumer looking to save money, avoid bad investments, and know when to sue. As a show that emerged as an uncontroversial mainstay in the highly politicized context of Nixon’s early 1970s campaign against PBS, the lessons CSK teaches – discipline, keen recognition of one’s own individualized goals and values, and the righteousness of legal frameworks and official channels of redress – align with a liberal democratic quest to train rational citizens without threatening the economy’s reliance on developing hedonistic and selfish consumers. In this article, however, I argue CSK also claims a useful legacy for considering the political potential of consumer education broadcasting as a public project that resolves the ostensibly irreconcilable differences between consumer and citizen subjectivities. Through its universal address, popular entertainment aesthetics, critique of market relations, and attempts to link viewers to consumer welfare infrastructures, CSK normalizes the social provisioning role of the state and a logic of entitlement. In so doing, it offers a preliminary counter-vision of how consumption could catalyze the formation of publics by positioning viewers as economic agents mutually (if differently) served and shaped by governmental provisioning policies and regulatory frameworks. A multimedia, multi-sited example of ‘public pedagogy’, CSK teaches people to make demands of government while positioning the state – and its consumer protections in particular – as an incomplete project in need of revision by its citizens and lawmakers.

  7. Reconsidering old Hollywood fantasies of Black citizenship: plantation, whiteness, and civil rights in Pinky
    Daria Goncharova · pp. 283-306 · online 2025-11-14
    摘要

    Historically, scholars have grouped Elia Kazan’s Pinky with other 1949 ‘race problem’ films by white filmmakers (Home of the Brave, Lost Boundaries, and Intruder in the Dust), thus limiting their analyses to the genre conventions of dramas and passing narratives. While insightful, previous scholarship has failed to consider how Pinky reflects the possibilities and limitations of Black citizenship in relation to not only the American postwar moment, but also the legacy of the plantation – the film’s primary setting and the source of conflict. Released at a time when discourse around the rising civil rights movement, desegregation, and the Red Scare made the postwar South a key space for negotiation of minorities’ national belonging, Pinky, I argue, reveals a deep schism between the cultural and legal definitions of citizenship in postwar America. Foregrounding the film’s geographies and drawing on Michael Bibler’s queer reading of the ‘meta-plantation’ and Cheryl Harris’ concept of ‘whiteness as property’, this article posits that in attempting to reconcile the sensibilities of the ‘Old South’ and the demands of the Cold War containment culture, Pinky ultimately reveals the inadequacies of both cultural models. At a time when concerns over property damage outweigh concerns for Black lives and when white savior narratives (such as The Help [2011] and Green Book [2018]) continue to captivate the American public, this article fills a gap in the history of the construction of Black citizenship on film by untangling the relationship between whiteness, sexuality, and property ownership in Pinky.

  8. The vengeful dad: violence, paternalism, and antifeminism in New Hollywood cinema
    Harrison Whitaker · pp. 307-329 · online 2025-06-11
    摘要

    While studies of New Hollywood have long identified the vigilante as a key type in the era’s films, the exact nature of the figure’s extralegal violence has gone unexamined. Films often cited as emblematic of 1970s cinema’s fixation on men who enact their own form of justice — Joe (1970), Death Wish (1974), and Hardcore (1979) among them — center not just anticrime avengers, but specifically avenging dads. The vengeful dad is thus a key type for understanding some of the larger ideological moves of the New Hollywood era. Often conflated with the more general vigilante figure, he represents a specific flavor of paternalistic backlash against evolving social norms and perceived social decay; his ‘justice’ is enacted in reaction to the violation of his daughter’s sexuality, leading to violent confrontations which often play out across class lines. Critically, his inability to balance the demands of work with the stresses of a collapsing domestic sphere is dependent on the inadequacies of his spouse, depictions of whom feature tropes which can be traced back to the literature of the burgeoning ‘men’s liberation’ movement contemporaneous with New Hollywood. This article demonstrates how the vengeful dads of New Hollywood not only offer a critical reformulation of how that era’s filmmaking is understood but also presage seemingly unrelated cinematic depictions of the father in the 1980s and beyond.

  9. The rhythmic space of affects: how atmospheres shape music videos
    Tomáš Jirsa · pp. 330-354 · online 2025-06-13
    摘要

    Opening a dialogue between music video scholarship and affect theory, this article argues for grounding the concept of atmosphere in the affective agency and movement of audiovisual forms, their spatial arrangement, and musical composition. Drawing on Eugenie Brinkema’s affective formalism and recent theorizations of cinematic motion, it analyzes the mutual transformations between space, bodies, and rhythm in Jlin’s ‘Carbon 7 (161)’ (dir. Joji Koyama, 2017) and Earl Sweatshirt’s ‘Grief’ (dir. Hiro Murai, 2015). Rather than treating atmosphere as a vague overall mood or subjective experience, the article explores the performative interactions between moving images, music, and sound as shaped by specific affective operations. To explain how music videos generate atmosphere and what aesthetic and conceptual roles it plays in a video’s composition, style, and viewer engagement, I first discuss the absence of the term atmosphere in music video studies, including its frequent substitution with the more diffuse concept of mood. Building upon Jordan Schonig’s account of camera movement and cinematic motion forms, the critique then joins several contemporary voices in affect theory whose philosophical elaborations of atmosphere allow to better understand the affective structure of music video space and movement. Finally, these theoretical interlocutors inform a close reading of the two videos, demonstrating how particular affective operations drive the audiovisual atmosphere, which in turn reshapes both their visual and sonic structures.

  1. Suiting up, taking off: movement, special effects, and superheroes
    Zoë Cary-Beckett · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    Superheroes have been moving between comics and film since the earliest days of the genre, and this long and uneasy process of reciprocal adaptation reveals affordances of the moving image that are completely inaccessible to the still images on a comic book page. The gaps between juxtaposed images in comics – governed by the mental processes gathered under the term ‘closure’ – are smoothed over within the continuity of the moving image. In this formal transit between stillness and movement, the superheroic body reveals or hides its aspects like a faceted stone rotating in the light. Moments of thematized physical transformation, which are inherently discontinuous on the four-color page, become live enactments enabled or stymied by the historical contingencies and formal affordances of film technology. These transformations, represented in this article by the quick-change suit up and the effect of continuous flight, do not only capture the tension between superhero comics and superhero movies, but also weigh the stakes and ideals carried within the superhero body.

  8. Factory as laboratory: Vinyl – the Tavel/Warhol Clockwork Orange
    Benjamin Halligan · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    The initial acclaim and subsequent critical reputation of Stanley Kubrick’s 1971 film adaptation of Anthony Burgess’s novel A Clockwork Orange have effectively overshadowed a pre-Kubrick history of adaptations – attempted, actually made, and indeed lost. This article considers one such adaptation: the 1965 theatre performance/16 mm film Vinyl, from a script by Ronnie Tavel and ostensibly made by Andy Warhol in his New York studio, known as the Factory. The article examines the genesis of this project and its overlap with the developing Theatre of the Ridiculous via Tavel’s script. It further illuminates how this loose film adaptation utilized Burgess’s text as the prompt or catalyst to present or even enact BDSM-inflected alternative lifestyle cultures and practices. These are considered as signalling towards a (queer) politics of resistance in the 1970s, and beyond. In this respect, the Tavel/Warhol project represents a quite different take on Burgess than that of Kubrick (of a liberal/Hollywood disposition) – anticipating the coming politicisation and political efficacy of alternative lifestyles in the coming decades.

  9. Moving out to come out and feel at home: queer cosmopolitanism in Karim Aïnouz’s Futuro Beach
    Humberto Saldanha · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-10-06
  10. Cincinnati as generi-city: cinematic gentrification in Anomalisa, The Killing of a Sacred Deer , and Carol
    Kirk Boyle · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article analyzes how three Cincinnati-based indie films from the 2010s illustrate the hidden costs of effacing the referential city in the pursuit of abstraction. Charlie Kaufman’s Anomalisa (2015), Yorgos Lanthimos’ The Killing of a Sacred Deer (2017), and Todd Haynes’ Carol (2015) aspire to abstraction in their theatrical style, genre, themes, and settings to critique the alienation and subsequent anomie of capitalist society, but their narrative focalization on the malaise of cosmopolitan elites, which surfaces in confrontations with lower-class counterparts, ignores the more profound alienations of urban renewal as a capital spatial fix. Fittingly, the artistic and allegorical integrity of these films entails the concomitant disintegration of Cincinnati. While Kaufman’s animated film reduces Cincinnati to nothing more than a comic signifier of a boring, standardized city, Cincinnati turns itself into a series of nondescript signifiers for Lanthimos’ and Haynes’ live action films. Unnamed despite its skyline being on full display in The Killing of a Sacred Deer and doubling as mid-century New York with its historical architecture on exhibit in Carol, Cincinnati trades its identity to play a generic metropolis – Generi-City. In disregarding Cincinnati as a particular urban place, specifically in terms of gentrification and race, these auteur productions unwittingly replicate the city’s own embrace of urban renewal. Even if their intentions are noble, e.g. to satirize capitalism in the case of the auteur or to generate local economic activity in the case of the city, the result is the same: a generic cosmopolitanism mired in neoliberal gentrification.

  11. Broadcasting dispossession: television, nostalgia, and the Hawaiian non-place in The White Lotus
    Duncan Caillard · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-09-23
    摘要

    This article presents a comparative analysis of American television representations of Hawai‘i and their role in the neocolonial erasure of Kānaka Maoli (Native Hawaiian) placemaking. Since the overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawai‘i in 1893, its lands and peoples have been incorporated into the global cultural imaginary as a feminised site of pleasurable escape and exploitation, concealing Indigenous resistance while stylising their cultural heritage for external consumption. With the emergence of television, jet travel and the ratification of Hawaiian statehood in the 1950s, Hawaiian cultural geography has been further reordered for external consumption, threatening to erase communities, identities and practices incompatible with the intensities of the US military-industrial occupation of its islands. Drawing upon Marc Augé’s theorisation of non-place, this article argues that television’s remediation of Hawai‘i abstracts and expropriates Kānaka Maoli cultural relationalities and intimacies, facilitating its colonial exploitation. Analysing the prominent recent example of The White Lotus (HBO, 2021-) within broader broadcast histories of Hawai‘i, this article argues that these series perpetuate the spatio-cultural erasure of Hawai‘i by playing into settler nostalgic fantasies of vacancy and availability. Therefore, despite The White Lotus’s pretensions of decolonial critique, the series structurally maintains the representational dispossession of Kānaka Maoli by concealing crises within the tourism industry and discounting acts of sovereign Indigenous resistance.

  12. Pioneer fantasy media: inclusive representation and imperial logic in contemporary television
    Emmanuel Ramos-Barajas · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-09-18
    摘要

    Taylor Sheridan’s Yellowstone franchise has garnered acclaim for redefining the Western genre in the era of streaming television. In the limited-run prequel 1883 (Paramount+, 2021–2022), the fictional Dutton family, the series’ central protagonists, serves as the focal point for tracing both the intergenerational plot and the narrativization of the U.S. frontier. In this manner, U.S. history is miniaturized. Rather than concentrating on the more unsavory aspects of U.S. imperial history, the historical fiction series turns its attention to matters more narratively alluring – such as, romance, visceral thrills, and interpersonal dynamics. I call this approach pioneer fantasy media, a subgenre that centers its dramatic arcs on the relationships of diverse and gendered characters in an attempt to settle the contradictions of U.S. imperialism. My framing question is why these stories return to a national originary moment, while war, genocide, climate crises, mass migration, and wealth inequality run rampant. To answer, I explore how dominant television production sustains the inertia of imperial expansion while integrating marginalized populations into the national narrative. Through an interdisciplinary framework that considers nineteenth-century literature and art history, I argue that these modifications are crucial re-tunings that ensure the continuation of imperial ideology.

  13. Bad tourists: national identity and cosmopolitanism in postwar American cinema
    Dylan Caskie · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-09-15
    摘要

    The Marshall Plan of 1948, with goals of developing the economies of postwar Western Europe and defending against the ideological influence of the USSR, saw the emergence of postwar American mass tourism as a private-level diplomatic activity with potentially productive or destructive consequences. In this context, the representations of the American tourist in Europe in popular American cinema and serial travel publications of the 1950s and 1960s took on new significance. Serving a pedagogical function for new American tourists, such representations developed along a binary of ‘bad American tourists’ and ‘good American tourists’. The ‘bad American tourist’ stood in for cloistered, materialistic, and provincial forms of travel only to set up the ‘good American tourist’ as a positive alternative. The ‘good American tourist’, who is mobile, romantic, and innocently open to worldly influences, serves as both a utopic goal and an already-realized identity for all American tourists traveling abroad. Such a representation, this article argues, helped establish a cosmopolitan American national identity in the rise of the United States to global superpower status in the early days of the Cold War.

    Pankaj Kumar Mishra · pp. 1-5 · online 2026-09-11

  15. Ephemeral archives of the vanishing present: the hauntological and the Uncanny in The Blair Witch Project website
    Michael Stock · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-09-11
    摘要

    Working from the perspective of transmedia archaeology, this essay examines how the website for The Blair Witch Project (Daniel Myrick and Eduardo Sánchez, 1999) continues to generate meaning more than two decades after it was created. As an example of ‘dead media’ that continues to haunt the Internet, the experience of it can now summon nostalgia, not only for the film and furor surrounding it (for those who have been around long enough to remember), but for a wider audience, a nostalgia associated with earlier versions of the Internet, back when it was still known colloquially as ‘the Web’. To address how the website engages users and operates within the media ecology of the Blair Witch franchise, my essay utilizes two theoretical perspectives: Jacques Derrida’s conception of hauntology and Ernst Jentsch and Sigmund Freud’s notions of the Uncanny. Confronted with this innovative website from the past, we can shake neither the sensation of its antiquated-ness, nor its influence on the future that followed. It is a confusion of the familiar and the strange; a confusion of the animate and inanimate (as Freud and Jentsch describe); and a confusion of times, as we use a 21st century interface to access a visionary 20th century website designed for 20th century users (from the ages of 16–30), for whom this form of embodied perception and interaction was still a new experience.

  16. Tarzian’s television: broadcasting geography and industry in Bloomington, Indiana
    Anna Stamm · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-09-11
    摘要

  17. Of gasoline and violence: liquidity, toxicity and combustible encounters in Julio Hernández Cordón’s Gasolina ( Gasoline , 2008)
    Aarón Lacayo · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-09-11
    摘要

    In Julio Hernández Cordón’s Guatemalan film Gasoline (Gasolina, 2008), three middle-class teenagers – one of them with asthma – embark on a nightmarish escapade. In the span of a day, they steal gasoline, pick fights with others, and drive out to the sea. This essay considers how a story of urban violence in Gasolina offers a contemporary take on Guatemala’s postwar malaise fueled literally and metaphorically by gasoline. The ways in which the disaffected youth use and abuse gasoline point to the different forms that toxic violence – racial, social, and generational – continues to take hold in Guatemala. I argue that Gasolina offers a novel way of understanding the legacies of the country’s civil war (1960–96), framed in terms of a damaged ethical response to the country’s violence. In this light, Gasolina engages obliquely with a nation’s past in sharp contrast to the master text of Guatemalan cinema, Luis Argueta’s El silencio de Neto (The Silence of Neto, 1994), in which the protagonist’s asthmatic disability resolves in national unity. Instead, in Hernández Cordón’s film, the thick and sticky substance of gasoline spreads through a claustrophobic narrative of silence and complicity without promising any reconciliation. Gasolina suggests a cinema of toxic substances in which the volatile and liquid nature of gasoline becomes intertwined with other bodies, objects, and fluids by way of what I call combustible encounters. These episodes register the (in)visible ways in which gasoline marks a trajectory of escalating violence while also indicating a steady erosion of civility and respect for others.

  20. Queer (in)visibility and the production of queer space in postwar Lebanese film
    Raed El Rafei · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-09-10
    摘要

    The appearance of the very first Lebanese films to openly address homosexuality at the beginning of the new millennium can be seen as a response to an increasing exposure to globalized queer narratives in Western media.   24. When Billie Holiday went to Hollywood: the biopic as reputation management in Lady Sings the Blues
    Landon Palmer · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-07-27
    摘要

    When Lady Sings the Blues, the autobiography of jazz vocalist Billie Holiday, was published in 1956, the image that the book presented of Holiday defied midcentury politics of respectability and fashioned a notion of ladyhood borne from the struggles of Black women. While numerous producers showed interest in adapting the source material to the screen, the book’s image of Holiday proved difficult to reconcile with the strictures of Black representation within Hollywood. By analyzing archival materials that give insight into several attempts to adapt this book to film, this article chronicles Holiday’s relationship to Hollywood both via her own brief screen career and through the fifteen-year period in which producers sought to bring Holiday’s life to the screen as Hollywood negotiated the possibilities of Black representation from the Civil Rights years to the Black Power era. In so doing, I argue that biopics constitute a form of reputation management wherein the cultural value of a public figure is negotiated within the industrial priorities of entertainment institutions. The case of Holiday and Lady Sings the Blues illuminates the politics of the possible in adapting a Black woman’s defiant image of selfhood to the screen within the industrial conditions and narrative conventions that defined Hollywood during a crucial period for African American cinema.

  25. “My job is to live my life, not save yours”: queer emotional labor, audiences, and Netflix’s Queer Eye
    Cameron Lynn Brown · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-07-27
    摘要

    In this article, I argue that audience members of Netflix’s Queer Eye expect the Fab Five to perform emotional labor to access a genuine self. Specifically, audiences’ expectations of emotional labor come from their (self-)reflexive understandings of the Fab Five’s role as laborers responsible for helping the people they make over start and finish the process of self-actualization, a core tenet of the reality makeover genre. To accomplish this, I conducted in-depth interviews with 30 adults who had seen at least one episode of Netflix’s Queer Eye. From these interviews, I argue that audiences of Netflix’s Queer Eye engage with the Fab Five, and by extension queer men, as people who are expected to perform emotional labor. These reception practices reveal that queer folks are still burdened with unique pressures and expectations to perform a specific task to bolster a heterosexist culture.

  26. Serializing Islamophobia: anti-Muslim racial paranoia and HBO’s The Night Of
    Hatim El-Hibri · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    Analyses of racism and its contradictions in the US in the 2010s must contend with a dialectical tension between public expressions which support tolerance and inclusivity, and the public resurgence of white nationalism. This article analyzes The Night Of (HBO, 2016) to examine how anti-Muslim racism in this period found expression not just in the form of the flat Islamophobic stereotype, but also in the form of complex and sympathetic main characters who nonetheless remain suspicious.   27. Scalar vicissitudes and minute creatures: reexamining insects as cinematic subjects in Microcosmos and Life in the Undergrowth
    Heidi Mikkola · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    This article examines insects as subjects in the documentary moving image and scalar vicissitudes in two different kinds of wildlife documentaries: Life in the Undergrowth (2005) and Microcosmos (1996). Western relationships with insects are intimately tied to technology and visual perception. The mass extinction of insects remains largely overlooked; however, their disappearance poses a significant threat to ecosystems and biodiversity. I argue that insect documentaries have roots in early depictions of microscopic illustrations as well as in the tradition of wildlife documentary. Although the wildlife documentary genre focuses on animals, its narrative tradition lies in representing bigger animals, usually mammals. By contrast, the theorization of cinematic scales lies in relation to the human figure. The convergence of these visual traditions raises considerations regarding documentaries about insects, while it also prompts us to reflect on the ethics of scale in the human – insect relationship. I argue that examining insects as proper cinematic subjects contests anthropocentrism in cinematic scales and rethinks understandings of ecological relations. The article examines the spatial scales and framings of the films, especially close-ups, when the subject of the framing is an insect instead of a human figure. I argue that taking the insect instead of the human body as the reference point shifts the scalar framework through which these films operate, unsettling the human body’s role as the implicit measure of scale. This shift brings questions of image size and scale to the fore and makes visible alternative ecological relations.

  28. National identity and global imaginary in China’s ‘citizen evacuation’ films: from Wolf Warrior 2 (2017) to Home Coming (2022)
    Mei Gao · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-06-17
    摘要

    With the expansion of China’s global economic involvement since the 2000s, ensuring the safety of Chinese nationals and assets abroad has become a diplomatic imperative for Beijing. In recent years, the evacuation of overseas Chinese from conflict-stricken countries such as Libya and Yemen have inspired a number of film adaptations, exemplified by Wolf Warrior 2 (2017), Operation Red Sea (2018), and Home Coming (2022). These ‘citizen evacuation’ films adopt Hollywoodized narrative and generic conventions, portraying China’s evacuation efforts as thrilling rescue missions led by a powerful nation-state. Given their subject matter and market positioning, these films are uniquely located at the intersection of Chinese cinematic traditions, Hollywood narrative and generic conventions, and China’s foreign policy. As such, they act as a focal point for the negotiation between local and global (Hollywood) narrative patterns as well as for Chinese cinema’s search for a cultural vision of ‘the global’ that is aligned with Chinese political discourse. This article examines how these forces have shaped the national and global imaginary in ‘citizen evacuation’ films and traces changes across the three films. The article further argues that Home Coming embodies a significant shift in the representation of China’s global activities and national identity, characterized by a return to indigenous narrative tropes and an emphasis on the non-interference principle, the central pillar of China’s foreign policy that has been called into question as China becomes increasingly engaged in the political dynamics of other regions.

  29. ‘ The Invisible Man is in your home’: pandemic-era Watch Parties as ephemeral media events
    Cory Barker · pp. 1-31 · online 2026-01-29
    摘要

    The COVID-19 pandemic has transformed the distribution of many films since 2020, but none more so than films that were in theaters when lockdowns closed multiplexes. To compensate for the collapsed theatrical window, studios quickly shifted films like Emma, The Hunt, and The Invisible Man to premium video-on-demand (or PVOD) rentals and fashioned ad-hoc promotional campaigns accentuating the surprise of their sudden digital availability. The centerpiece of these studio efforts was the ‘Watch Party’, which asked people to rent and watch the films ‘together’ with synchronized start times and real-time Twitter conversations. Studios also enlisted celebrity participants, including filmmakers who shared behind-the-scenes insights and digital influencers who delivered reactionary riffs on the films. Close analysis of promotional materials (e.g. social media posts, videos, key art) reveals that rather than replicating the theatrical viewing environment, Watch Parties instead imitated Social TV initiatives of the 2010s, including live-tweeted chatter, targeted hashtags, celebrity contributions, and canned production anecdotes. Although Watch Parties waned as the pandemic brought new distribution challenges, their emergence demonstrates Hollywood’s continued interest in constructing shared, limited-time, and now multi-screen experiences. This article identifies Watch Parties as ephemeral media events that are part of the lineage of ritualistic media events and nine-figure marketing plans but that also leverage the surprise of unique distribution circumstances to create short-lived bursts of virality and immediacy. The framework provides a new perspective on digital distribution and media events amid the increased industry and consumer preoccupation with on-demand streaming portals.

  30. From the figure of the obscene rich to the fantasy of its destruction: on Succession , schadenfreude and the godlike power of the super-rich on screen
    Hanna Kuusela · pp. 1-25 · online 2025-11-04
    摘要

    A significant part of contemporary popular culture portrays the rich as obscene, self-destructive, addicted and deranged. This article analyses the affective intensities circulating around this trope by close reading the award-winning television series Succession and its media reception. Drawing from cultural analyses of the entrepreneurial myth and of the tensions between patrimonial capitalism and the ideals of meritocracy, the article analyses the figure of the obscene rich, the ambiguities such portrayals seem to carry and the experiences of schadenfreude they generate. The article asks what is at stake in the tendency to represent the rich as obscene and analyses the subjectivities and affects this trope creates or postulates both for the rich characters and the audience. Looking beyond the critical concepts of ambiguity and ambivalence, the article asks to what extent the affects around the obscene rich are linked to the contemporary economic formation, characterised by a strong belief in meritocracy but growing dynastic tendencies. It argues that Succession creates a fatalistic sense of no escape, leaving its audiences with ambiguous affects, mixing disgust with envy and moral superiority. Ultimately, the article analyses Succession as a series that fosters a fantasy in which rich dynasties will eventually destroy themselves, or at least suffer, while the rest of us are left entertained.

  2. The Practice of Everyday War
    Pamela M. Lee · pp. 124-149 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This essay examines how art and visual culture participate in what it terms “the practice of everyday war”—the routinization of political violence across ordinary spaces and times, rather than its confinement to discrete battles or events. Taking Barton Gellman's 2022 Atlantic essay “January 6 Was Practice” as a point of departure, the author argues that the Capitol riot functioned less as an isolated event than as rehearsal for an ongoing authoritarian project, one whose consequences have deepened by 2026. Drawing on Michel de Certeau's distinction between strategy (spatial, top-down) and tactics (temporal, opportunistic) and Carl von Clausewitz's writings on “small war,” the essay traces how paramilitary violence—historically associated with irregulars and the “weak”—has been appropriated as a tool of state and reactionary power. The argument moves through three case studies. First, it analyzes photographs from January 6 taken inside the Capitol rotunda, where rioters clash beneath John Trumbull's monumental Revolutionary War paintings, arguing that the juxtaposition collapses supposedly discrete historical registers into a single continuum of “everyday war.” Second, it turns to Trumbull himself, reading his decades-long practice of picturing “1776”—and his fraught encounter with the French Revolution of 1789—as an early instance of art mobilizing history as ideological material. Third, the essay examines photographer An-My Lê's work, particularly Small Wars and 29 Palms, as counter-practices that expose the recursive, non-linear temporality binding past wars (Vietnam, the Civil War) to present and future militarism, without recourse to conventional antiwar rhetoric. Ultimately, the essay contends that art's engagement with war's imagery—whether complicit, critical, or ambiguous—reveals history itself as a kind of ideological arsenal, one weaponized anew amid the contemporary erosion of democratic norms.

  3. Crimson Soil
    Siobhan Angus, Jennifer Raab · pp. 150-179 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    On July 4, 2020, Donald Trump's proposal for a “National Garden of American Heroes” at Mount Rushmore framed national memory as something that can be fixed in soil. Against this, the article reads W.E.B. Du Bois's account of Georgia's “crimson soil” as a counter-archive in which land serves as a record of imperial conquest, slavery, and racial violence rather than stable national foundations. Soil is a witness to history. Beginning with Mount Rushmore and the Black Hills, we examine the logics of subtraction that produced the monument and trace this settler-colonial system of extraction, displacement, and substitution to the nineteenth-century agricultural crisis, when soil exhaustion in plantation economies drove the rise of industrial fertilizers. Guano, ground bison bones, and phosphate rock became central to regenerating depleted land, converting ecological and human violence into agricultural possibility. Fertilizer thus emerges as a key infrastructure of settler expansion. Visual culture is central to this process, rendering these transformations legible and naturalizing them as the nation's destiny. The article concludes with George Washington Carver's soil experiments, and Amanda Williams’ contemporary paintings, which are indebted to his research, as a counterpoint that reopens questions about environmental knowledge, labor, and land rights.

  4. Memory Practices: Murray Sinclair, the Medicine Box, and His Portrait by Kent Monkman
    Shawon Kinew · pp. 180-194 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    The Carlisle Indian Industrial School was the subject of one of the most arresting images in American history: hundreds of Indigenous children from across the country arranged in neat rows before a school building, not a parent in sight. A record of the assimilative project and nation-building ambitions of the federal Indian boarding school system, the photograph also serves as a prompt to remember. Canada's Truth and Reconciliation Commission stands as a blueprint for a process that many hope might take place in America. A bentwood medicine box by sculptor Luke Marston and a portrait by Kent Monkman of the late Murray Sinclair—both tied to Canada's history of Indian residential schools—embody the mnemonic practices essential to truth-telling.

  5. Soft Censorship
    Steven Nelson · pp. 195-200 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This essay examines the phenomenon of “soft censorship” in American museums during the second Trump administration, drawing on the author's firsthand experience as dean of the Center for Advanced Study in the Visual Arts at the National Gallery of Art. Nelson defines soft censorship—a concept long familiar to librarians—as indirect suppression through relocation, reduced visibility, relabeling, or “contextualization” of controversial content, rather than outright removal or cancellation. The essay centers on the case of “Color Lines Across the United States and Mexico,” a Wyeth Foundation symposium on race in American and Mexican art, scheduled at the National Gallery for May 2025. Following a visit from DOGE representatives and mounting institutional anxiety, National Gallery leadership ordered the symposium moved off-site rather than canceled outright, resulting in its removal from the museum's website, relocation to a smaller venue, and loss of virtual access—despite ultimately taking place as planned via an emergency partnership with Dumbarton Oaks. Nelson situates this episode alongside other instances of institutional response to political pressure, including Amy Sherald's withdrawal of her exhibition American Sublime from the National Portrait Gallery, the Smithsonian's handling of The Shape of Power, and Philadelphia's successful legal fight to reinstate a slavery exhibit at the President's House Site. He argues that unlike outright refusal or legal resistance, the National Gallery's and the Smithsonian's strategies of anticipatory compliance—reducing access while claiming to preserve content—constitute a distinct and insidious form of censorship enabled by executive orders targeting DEI initiatives and reframing American history. Nelson concludes that such preemptive self-restriction, rather than protecting institutions, normalizes self-censorship and transforms museums into complicit agents of authoritarian control, undermining their stated commitments to diversity, academic freedom, and the free exchange of ideas.

    The Editors · pp. 201-203 · online 2026-08-01

    From this second, radically incomplete chronology of the Trump administration's assaults on American culture,1 it is clear that the concerted, multipronged effort to control the American cultural landscape, one unprecedented since the McCarthy era, continues unabated.   7. On MONUMENTS : A Conversation with Hamza Walker and Bennett Simpson
    George Baker, Leah Dickerman, David Joselit, Pamela M. Lee, Steven Nelson · pp. 204-221 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This article documents a roundtable conversation held on December 12, 2025, in which October editors George Baker, Leah Dickerman, David Joselit, Pamela M. Lee, and art historian Steven Nelson met with curators Hamza Walker and Bennett Simpson to discuss MONUMENTS, a two-part exhibition co-presented at the Brick and MoCA's Geffen Contemporary in Los Angeles. The show paired ten decommissioned Confederate monuments and related statuary (including works depicting Robert E. Lee, Stonewall Jackson, and Jefferson Davis) with contemporary art by figures such as Andres Serrano, Stan Douglas, Hank Willis Thomas, and Leonardo Drew, as well as a new work by Kara Walker composed of the fragments of a decommissioned monument, all addressing the afterlives of the antebellum period and Jim Crow. The conversation traces the exhibition's eight-year gestation, situating it against the 2017 wave of monument removals following Charlottesville, the 2015 Charleston church shooting, and the current administration's efforts—via executive orders such as the proposed “National Garden of American Heroes”—to reinstate celebratory national narratives ahead of the 250th anniversary of the Declaration of Independence. Walker and Simpson describe their curatorial strategy as nuanced: Rather than staging a debate over whether monuments should stand or fall, they treat the decommissioned objects as aesthetic and material artifacts—removed from outdoor “unidirectional” public speech and recontextualized indoors, on the floor, in dialogue with contemporary work. This reframing, they argue, exposes monuments’ mythic (rather than strictly historical) function and their entanglement with surveillance, racial capitalism, and militarized control. Participants discuss the exhibition's reception, including right-wing accusations comparing it to the Nazi Degenerate Art exhibition. The dialogue extends to the Los Angeles context—LA's own submerged Confederate and Southern migrant history—and closes with broader reflections on the distinction between monuments, memorials, and sculpture, and on art's comparatively “slow” but reflexive power to counter the accelerated, euphemistic rhetoric of contemporary political discourse.

  2. The Exchange Model of Mainstreaming
    Mikkel Bækby Johansen · pp. 402-417 · online 2025-10-15
    摘要

    This paper proposes a conceptual model of online mainstreaming. The model emphasizes communication flows and actor trajectories back and forth between center (the mainstream) and periphery (the extreme), identifying a logic of exchange as a key driver of contemporary mainstreaming processes. The model illustrates a perpetual exchange of content, actors, and practices between mainstream and off-mainstream domains. The paper focuses on three under-theorized aspects of mainstreaming in the digital age: (1) the linguistic configuration of the concepts of extremism and mainstreaming, including their inherent spatiality; (2) the possible significance of mundane and arbitrary media practices rather than intentional and strategic engagement of extremists; and (3) the enabling and constraining conditions of online platforms and internet culture. Understanding the exchange logic of mainstreaming can help scholars, media professionals, and security practitioners to better distinguish between controversial and harmful phenomena in a hybrid and fragmented media environment.

  3. Everyday extremism: memes and the mainstreaming of extremist narratives around the 2024 European Parliament election
    Jullietta Stoencheva, Miriam Haselbacher, Tina Askanius, Ursula Reeger · pp. 418-438 · online 2026-02-16
    摘要

    This paper takes the 2024 European Parliament election as an empirical starting point to examine the circulation of extremist narratives on social media in three European countries: Sweden, Austria, and Bulgaria. Drawing on a visual content analysis of memes, the study focuses on visual articulations of violence and hostility directed towards perceived enemies and out-groups. The study asks: How did extremist narratives emerge around key contentious political issues? What forms of violence are articulated through these narratives? What shared visual grammars and coded symbols underpin the translocal entanglement of extremist narratives enabling them to travel across national boundaries and fuel everyday extremism in the digital mainstream? The findings highlight how memes contribute to what we term everyday extremism to describe a context in which dehumanising and violent narratives have been normalised to the point that they increasingly saturate everyday political discussions between ordinary citizens across Europe.

  4. Ideological entrepreneurship, memetic participation, and the co-production of misogynistic ideology on X
    Katrine K. Pedersen · pp. 439-455 · online 2026-04-22
    摘要

    This study examines how misogynistic ideology is co-produced on X through the interplay between ideological entrepreneurship, participatory audiences, and algorithmic infrastructures. Using Andrew Tate as a case study, the analysis draws on a dataset of 500 of his most liked posts and 500 highly visible user posts to investigate the circulation of hierarchical gender narratives in high-engagement environments. Building on research on ideological entrepreneurship, understood as actors who mobilize algorithmically favored and emotionally resonant content, the article foregrounds the memetic dimension of ideological reproduction. Drawing on the concept of deep memetic frames, it shows how hierarchical gender narratives are negotiated and amplified within algorithmically structured visibility environments. In doing so, the article contributes to research on influencers and the manosphere by demonstrating how ideological entrepreneurship operates within a broader ecology of memetic circulation, where meaning emerges through participatory repetition and infrastructural amplification, rather than solely as strategic persuasion.

  5. Cultural transgressions and media contestation: a qualitative study of the tradwife trend in Dutch media
    Guilherme Giolo · pp. 456-470 · online 2025-09-22
    摘要

    This article explores how the tradwife (short for traditional wife) movement, an internet trend where women embrace traditional gender roles and decry feminism, is contested by mainstream media in the Netherlands. Analyzing 44 articles published by diverse outlets between January 2023 and December 2024, this study identifies three key themes of contestation: “Return to the past,” “Fantasy and amusement,” and “Female unawareness.” These discourses frame the movement as regressive, escapist, or misguided. Considering tradwife to be a highly aestheticized form of cultural transgression, it is proposed that these arguments not only reflect a broader cultural pushback against perceived rejections of feminist values but that by framing the movement as relatable or unserious, these contestations inadvertently contribute to the normalization and hybridization of fringe ideologies. This contribution expands previous research on the mainstreaming of extremism, demonstrating how media contestation relates to the diffusion of transgressive ideologies in the digital age.

  6. Invisible string(s): Exploring conspiratorial participatory practices among Swifties
    Simone Driessen · pp. 471-489 · online 2026-01-29
    摘要

    This study explores how Taylor Swift fans, so-called “Swifties,” engage in playful, conspiratorial participation by interpreting hidden messages and developing fan theories, especially around Swift’s queer identity (the “Gaylor” discourse). Drawing on interviews with 18 fans, this article presents an in-depth perspective, demonstrating how speculative fandom blurs the line between interpretive play and conspiracist logic. This helps reveal how participatory practices mirror the structures of (political) conspiracies. Fans organize around theory-building, decipher cryptic clues, and negotiate interpretive boundaries, which echoes behaviors found in extremist communities. These findings suggest that conspiratorial thinking and practices are no longer confined to fringe groups but are embedded in everyday cultural participation, including mainstream pop-culture fandoms. By tracing these participatory practices among Swifties, this research contributes to a broader understanding of how conspiratorial play becomes normalized, offering a cultural lens into the mainstreaming of extremism in today’s societies.

  7. Mainstreaming violent extremism through language play in the Halle manifesto
    Line Nybro Petersen, Marie Bojsen-Møller, Tanya Karoli Christensen · pp. 490-506 · online 2026-01-28
    摘要

    In 2019, a perpetrator opened fire outside the local synagogue in Halle, Germany. Prior to the terrorist attack, he had posted several documents and links on social media, including a manifesto. These documents draw on participatory practices familiar to mainstream participatory communities, while deemphasizing the ideological motivation of the attack. Through an interdisciplinary approach combining media studies with linguistics, this article analyses the multi-document manifesto posted by the perpetrator ahead of the attack and considers how these documents engage with mainstream participatory culture through language play. Most significantly, the three documents engage in language play addressing its intended audience through fan practices, humor, and memetic gamification of his terrorist plans. The analysis demonstrates how far-right terrorists are not just “lone-actors” motivated by extremist ideologies, they are also eclectically intertwined with different mainstream practices, thus underlining the importance of ingroup interaction and incitement in cases of far-right terrorism.

  1. Politicization of lies about the indigenous peoples in Brazil: frequency and thematic of information in Lupa digital fact-checking agency
    Allysson Martins · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    The insertion and daily use of social media, from the 2010s onward, provided a wide circulation of content. This information is sometimes false and affects vulnerable groups, such as indigenous peoples. To understand this fake and false information about Brazilian indigenous peoples, we evaluate the fake news checked by the Lupa agency since its creation. From 2018 to 2023, there were 36 items focusing on the indigenous peoples, including only three without the “False” label, but still contributing to information disorder. Although speech by far-right politicians and supporters has brought lies about the indigenous peoples to the attention of Lupa, this movement by the agency is strengthened by the current government, since it created the Ministry of Indigenous Peoples and increased attention around this population. The politicization was observed indirectly in two lies and directly in 28 fake news items, with 24 benefiting the far-right and four the left part of the political spectrum.

    Dacia Pajé, Alexander Jenkins · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

  2. Reading sociopornographic books: genre and dirty work
    Jonathan A. Allan · pp. 290-307 · online 2024-07-18
    摘要

    During the 1970s, before the rise of freely accessible hardcore pornographies, there was a brief moment of a few years where books and magazines ostensibly sought to ‘teach’ about sex, while providing pornographic imagery to curious readers. These books were often marketed as ‘educational’ and often marketed towards ‘married couples’, or at the very least, committed couples. In this article, I seek to study these books by framing them as ‘sociopornographic’, defining what is meant by that term and outlining key elements of the genre.   3. ‘You can’t do that’? The ambiguities of Wet and Messy fetish videos
    Melissa Anne Beattie · pp. 308-321 · online 2025-01-16
    摘要

    Although the study of transformative works and the study of pornography are both decades old, there has been little analysis of any pornographic text as a transformative work.     Anthony J. Morris · pp. 322-334 · online 2025-06-16
    摘要

    The article analyses Andy Warhol’s Sex Parts print series through a connoisseurship methodology to argue that the work is a culmination of subject matter and formal aesthetics the artist developed throughout his career.   5. Male rape, female dominance and new sexual economics in Gereon
    Mikhail Osokin · pp. 335-357 · online 2025-06-19
    摘要

    The first Russian erotic novel The Story of the English Lord Gereon and the Brandenburg Margravine Friederike Luise (c. 1747) remained in manuscript form and was never published in full. In the world of Gereon, it is not men who pursue women, but women who actively seek sexual encounters with men, and when the latter resist, they are raped. Lustful women are portrayed as a deadly threat to the protagonist. Gereon is forced to suppress his sexual desires and defend himself against the often aggressive and intrusive advances of women. Behind this is the humoral theory of sexual physiology, which posits that male libido is finite and must be preserved, unlike female libido, which is seen as inexhaustible. The erotic and pornographic episodes contribute to an erotic narrative about the value of male sex, modulated by a traditional medieval plot about marital fidelity.

  6. Julie Lee and Trilogy of Lust (1995): pornography, moral panic and the politics of belonging in pre-handover Hong Kong
    Jing Jing Chang, Agnes Tam · pp. 358-373 · online 2025-05-15
    摘要

    In 1995 Julie Lee co-wrote, co-directed, co-produced and starred in Hong Kong’s only commercial pornographic film, Trilogy of Lust. Since then, Julie Lee has been vilified in the popular media, leaving her contribution to Hong Kong film culture and beyond either marginalized or obscured within the scholarship on Hong Kong film history. This article provides a needed corrective to Julie Lee’s erasure by examining how the reception of Julie Lee and her film exposed widespread moral panic in Hong Kong around pornography’s impact on youth, along with allied issues of obscenity, morality and freedom of expression in advance of the 1997 handover of the former colony to China. We argue that despite being culturally denigrated, Julie Lee’s hardcore performance in Trilogy of Lust forged an alternative vision of Hong Kong identity to challenge the former colony’s cosmopolitan middle-class version of Hong Kong citizenry, thereby exposing an uneasy tension between the sense of belonging and unbelonging in pre-handover Hong Kong.

    Emily Stokes · pp. 374-393 · online 2025-06-09
    摘要

    Within the gay pornographic videos produced by the Berlin studio Cazzo, images of East German skinheads fighting in streets, chasing down punks, and engaging in hardcore, violent gay sex can be found.   8. Becoming pornographic, being ethnographic: exploring the heteroerotic, hierarchies of desire and affective atmospheres in swinger clubs
    Chris Haywood · pp. 394-408 · online 2024-10-10
    摘要

    The relationship between pornography and ethnography has a long history. It is a history that has often been characterized by ethnographers treating their research participants as sexual objects, to be pornographically used and consumed by ethnographers and their audiences. Drawing upon 18 ethnographies of swinger clubs in the UK, this article, in contrast, explores how the ethnographer, through their experience of pornographic affects, is productive in capturing the erotic dynamics of swinger communities. The article does this by exploring the voyeuristic nature of ethnography and how such voyeurism enables a better understanding of the hetero-erotic in swinger club spaces. It then develops this fusion of ethnography and pornography further by discussing how the ethnographer, in many ways, experiences their research practice as a pornographic practice. Finally, the article then suggests that the ethnographic writing up process can in many ways be pornographic writing. Importantly, the article concludes that rather than viewing the relationship between pornography and ethnography as problematic, the relationship has a queering potential that can provide insights and understandings that ‘straight’ ethnographies may overlook.

  9. Sexual media practices in private homemade amateur porn
    Sven Lewandowski · pp. 409-426 · online 2025-06-09
    摘要

  10. ‘Sex influencers’: a survey of porn workers in the USA
    Sophie Pezzutto · pp. 427-447 · online 2024-11-28
    摘要

    There are relatively few survey data on the lives of porn workers. This article presents and analyzes broad-based data from a survey of 531 porn industry workers in the USA. The article provides key demographic, economic, and social insights into the lives of porn workers. According to the data, porn workers are not all young, can have long careers in the industry, have high levels of education, do not all come from poverty, are not exclusively cisgender women, and are disproportionately white. Analysis of the data also dispels binary notions of porn workers as either exploited or uniquely empowered – as sex workers have argued repeatedly. Instead, the data show that many porn workers are satisfied with their work yet economically precarious, and that there are notable inequalities within the industry. Crucially, the data add to current conversations in porn studies about porn work as either gig work or influencer work. The article concludes that the data from this survey support the latter framing and that porn workers are best thought of as ‘sex influencers’. By providing a data-driven, broad picture on porn workers, the author hopes that future reporting and research will be more empirically grounded and nuanced.

  2. Rethinking Fast Feminism slowly
    Erika Biddle · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-09-24
    摘要

    As the editor of Shannon Bell’s Fast Feminism, I worked closely with Bell from 2007 to 2010, observing (with delight) her high-velocity encounters with the speed theories of Paul Virilio – including the ‘staircase’ method introduced in Pure War: ‘once an idea is introduced and deployed, jump to linkages with another idea and always, always do violence to the original context’ so that distinctions among points of departure collapse or blur. Fast feminism is an accident of speed theory, a method for writing as morphing, performative action, and situated embodiment. When theory is embodied it is always inflecting, reflecting, disturbing, and relational. In this article, I rethink, engage longitudinally, and do a performative intervention into fast feminism, focusing on how and when this philo-porno-political practice’s affective, high-velocity interventions are deployed with slowness and fast feminism becomes a medium and method for deceleration.

    Animesh Kashyap, Yashwant Singh Panwar · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-09-08
    摘要

    This article examines the precarious digital labour of Indian queer porn performers on X (formerly Twitter), analysing how the platform's algorithm simultaneously enables the visibility of queer sexuality and monetizes performers' free labour. Drawing on content and thematic analysis of 10 performer accounts belonging to gay men and trans women of Indian origin, we analyse 100 posts, 100 captions and 200 audience comments. The comments fall into three categories: requests for sexual contact or specific acts, expressions of appreciation and derogatory remarks.   8. The copulative hermeneutics of the veil: pornographizing the truth
    Canberk Doğalı · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-08-18
    摘要

    This article explores Shannon Bell’s project of deploying the obscene, grotesque, and pornographic to dismantle totalitarian philosophical thought. Bell challenges the notion of ‘truth’ as a pure, noetic concept, reframing it as an authoritarian mechanism that suppresses bodily contingency and ambiguity. By reinserting corporeal excess – scatology, filth, and staged exposure – into intellectual discourse, Bell seeks to democratize philosophy and expose systematic exclusions sustaining its claims to sovereign reason. I foreground Bell’s key methodologies: parrhesia, a risky, dissenting form of truth-telling, and the ‘copulative both/and’ logic that upholds contradiction rather than resolving it. Ultimately, I argue that, for Bell, the excessive, abject, pornographic body functions as a deconstructive and emancipatory force against fascistic interpretive and political imperatives.

  11. Perceptions of pornography among UK sex education practitioners: a qualitative study
    Francesca Fiennes · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This research explores the perceptions of UK sex education practitioners around pornography within sex education for young people between 11 and 18 years.   13. Very large, largely silent: how three pornography-hosting platforms assess their governance of sexual content under the Digital Services Act
    Carlotta Rigotti, Nicola Döring, Isotta Rossoni · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-08-06
    摘要

    In 2023, the European Commission designated Aylo (Pornhub.com), Stripchat, and XVideos as very large online platforms under the Digital Services Act, subjecting them to binding obligations to identify, assess, and mitigate systemic risks. In 2025, these platforms submitted their first risk assessment reports to the European Commission. This article offers a systematic and critical analysis of these reports from a sex-positive, rights-based perspective – treating both non-consensual sexual content and the suppression of consensual sexual content as systemic risks. The article concludes with recommendations arguing that effective governance must hold protection and freedom in productive tension.

  14. My year as a Dominatrix: non-recuperable pornography, labour, and the work of Shannon Bell
    Dr Joy · pp. 1-12 · online 2026-08-06
    摘要

    In this article, I analyse my experiences working as a Dominatrix in a small Ontario dungeon through Shannon Bell’s writings on the politics of gender, sexuality, and the body. Bell’s philosophic criteria for non-recuperable pornography, outlined in her chapter ‘Post-Porn/Post-Anti-Porn’, provide the structure for my argument. These philosophical criteria include ‘the saying of the work is not lost in the said; the work is produced from a new post-porn/post-anti-porn positionality; [and] the work is an act of labor as self-activity and not labor alienated as labor-power’. Because non-recuperable pornography works to recodify gendered bodies and bodies engaged in sexual action, assessing where it is, and is not, operative is important. I argue that my work in the dungeon (what I call durational pornographic scenes) adheres to Bell’s first two criteria for non-recuperable pornography but not the third (and that this third failure undermines the first and second criteria as well). I conclude that, ultimately, the project of recodifying bodies and bodies engaged in sexual action needs to be tied to the larger project of transforming economic social relations more generally.

    摘要
